#5002. Reformation’s Debut Home Collection Gives French Rugs an LA Attitude

For years, people shopping at Reformation have asked about something the brand didn’t sell. “One of the biggest comments that we get is that the stores look so beautiful and the rugs are amazing,” says Lauren Caris Cohan, the brand’s chief creative officer. Typically, she says, one question follows that observation: “Where can we get them?” Until now, the answer was nowhere; Thomas Coccimiglio, founder of the French rugmaker Les Editions de Tapis, is “really precious” about his designs for Reformation stores, Cohan says, and doesn’t reuse them for other clients.

Today, Reformation’s collaboration with Les Editions de Tapis makes the coveted rugs available to the public, with nine limited-edition styles, all made to order and priced between $1,000 and $5,000.

Long before he began outfitting Reformation showrooms, Coccimiglio spent more than 15 years as a textile designer in fashion, working in London, Paris, and New York. At Tory Burch, his trips to meet embroiderers in Italy and India eventually led him to rug-making, and during the pandemic, he left fashion for interiors, though his fashion background continues to influence him, even in the way he speaks about rugs: “I approach a rug almost as I would a piece of embroidery on a garment.”

Reformation opens more than 15 stores a year, Cohan says, and each one gets its own hand-sketched motif and custom colorway from Coccimiglio. “There’s a bit of a French sense of humor, a bone-dry sense of humor, in our [Reformation] brand DNA,” Cohan says, “that’s reflected in Thomas’ designs.”

For the new capsule collection of rugs, Reformation gave Coccimiglio full creative direction. The conversations that launched the collaboration involved Marie Antoinette, swans, a lot of pink and blue—all components also reflected in the partnering 12-piece clothing campaign, live today. To help inspire a cohesive thread linking Coccimiglio’s line to the ethos of the brand, he also revisited years of store designs for details to incorporate. “There’s something nostalgic about these pieces, almost like a little Proustian madeleine,” Coccimiglio says. “For customers who have experienced Reformation's stores over the years, these pieces might feel strangely familiar, almost iconic.”

The Chintz rugs set two crossed palm trees (Cohan’s idea, which Coccimiglio sketched by hand) between trailing vine borders, with herons and swans tucked at their roots. Swans Fans puts two black-outlined swans among spiky fan palms on a French blue foundation. Le Chat leaves a field of lilac almost entirely empty, save for one striped cat walking a broken black line. Le Classique has an ivory ground, two wavering oxblood borders, and a single dot, fitting anyone with French sensibilities sans menagerie.

The rugs are hand-knotted from wool, silk, and linen. Coccimiglio manufactures his larger pieces in India, where he finds the wool softer and better suited to big formats. Smaller editions come from Nepal, where Himalayan wool is denser, and the results are ornate, “like jewelry,” says Coccimiglio. While you could find a less expensive rug elsewhere, Cohan points out that Coccimiglio’s are designed for longevity of use—plus, the rugmaker’s own work outside the Reformation collaboration costs considerably more. “This is a much more accessible price point for something so detailed and so custom,” she says.

Cohan, who restores midcentury houses in Los Angeles with her husband Matt Jacoby under the name Bob Audrey, doesn’t picture a single kind of room to house these rugs. “You could have it in a midcentury house in the Hollywood Hills, but it also would look so stunning in [a] herringbone-floor apartment in New York City,” she says.

The campaign was shot in a house at the top of Wonderland Park in Laurel Canyon, a 1950s enclave of single-story midcentury houses that includes Pierre Koenig’s Case Study House No. 21. Coccimiglio, meanwhile, works from a 17th-century hôtel particulier in Dijon. It’s fitting that the premise of the collection enmeshes old-world France with modern LA, as Coccimiglio places his work “somewhere between these two worlds: French heritage and contemporary culture.”

The collection is available to shop on Reformation.com on Nov. 9.

#5001. Who Is Colette Kress, Jensen Huang’s Successor as Nvidia CEO?

Who Is Colette Kress, Jensen Huang’s Successor as Nvidia CEO?

For the first time in 33 years, someone other than Jensen Huang is running Nvidia. And the person taking over is not an engineer, a chip designer, or a Silicon Valley founder.

Colette Kress, Nvidia's 59-year-old chief financial officer, was named interim CEO Thursday following Huang's abrupt resignation amid reports of extramarital affairs and allegations involving a suspected Chinese intelligence operative.

Kress will remain CFO while the board searches for a permanent replacement, according to Nvidia's regulatory filing. She inherits a company worth trillions of dollars, a shaken investor base, and a management structure built around one man.

Kress joined Nvidia in September 2013, when it was primarily known for graphics cards used in video games. She arrived with nearly 25 years of financial experience across some of America's largest technology companies.

She spent eight years at Texas Instruments before joining Microsoft in 1997. During her 13-year tenure there, she became CFO of the Server and Tools division, overseeing financial strategy for Microsoft's enterprise computing business.

In 2010, she moved to Cisco as senior vice president and CFO of its Business Technology and Operations Finance organization, managing financial planning and business development across the networking giant's operations.

Kress holds a finance degree from the University of Arizona and an MBA from Southern Methodist University, according to her official biography.

Her move to Nvidia came with an unusual endorsement. Her two sons, then enthusiastic gamers, encouraged her to join the company responsible for the graphics chips powering their favorite games, according to Forbes.

Huang personally recruited her, explaining his vision for AI computing on a whiteboard during their discussions, Wired reported. At the time, Nvidia was the smallest company Kress had worked for.

When Kress joined Nvidia, its annual revenue stood at approximately $4.13 billion. By fiscal 2026, that figure had reached $215.9 billion, an increase of more than 50 times.

Huang drove Nvidia's technological strategy, but Kress oversaw the finances supporting its expansion from gaming hardware into data centers and AI infrastructure.

Her tenure included Nvidia's $6.9 billion acquisition of networking specialist Mellanox and an increasingly aggressive strategy of investing in companies that purchase its computing hardware.

Those relationships now include CoreWeave, in which Nvidia invested another $2 billion in January. CoreWeave's rapid growth reflects enormous demand for Nvidia-powered computing, alongside the financial costs of building that infrastructure.

Kress also became Nvidia's principal financial voice to Wall Street, regularly explaining margins, chip demand and revenue forecasts during earnings calls.

Forbes ranked her among America's richest self-made women in 2026, with almost $750 million in Nvidia shares as of Wednesday's close.

Kress takes charge as Nvidia faces questions about the sustainability of AI infrastructure spending. Nvidia had already lost approximately $1.27 trillion in market value from its October peak before the board publicly announced its investigation on Huang’s love affairs.

She must also navigate Washington's restrictions on advanced chip exports to China and scrutiny surrounding Nvidia's commercial relationships. The board has said it has no reason to believe company secrets or U.S. national security were compromised in the allegations involving Huang.

Her financial expertise cannot entirely replace Huang's technical background or his personal relationships with developers, customers, and governments. Maintaining Nvidia's engineering leadership will require close coordination with the executives responsible for its chips and software.

Kress has promised to build on Huang's legacy, saying that “Nvidia's strength has always come from its people.”

Her first major public test arrives Tuesday, November 17, when Nvidia reports its third-quarter fiscal 2027 earnings. The company has scheduled its results for approximately 1:20 p.m. Pacific, followed by an investor call at 2 p.m.

#4999. How a Society Photographer Fell in Love With an Unexpected Upstate Victorian Home

I had no intention of buying the house on Willard Place. I only added it to the short list of Hudson properties I planned to visit out of sheer morbid curiosity. The house looked completely crazy online, with its wild colors and eccentric embellishments, inside and out—a confounding mix of hideous and fabulous. I felt compelled to check it out in person. Real estate agent extraordinaire Julia Crowley made the appointment at Willard the first stop on our house-hunting itinerary. The artist Helen Marden, a dear friend and owner of the nearby Hotel Tivoli, decided to come along for the ride. “What are we doing here?” she asked when we got out of the car. I’d forgotten to mention this purely voyeuristic side trip.

The polychrome exterior of Craig-Martin's house in Hudson, New York.

Craig-Martin on the front steps of her home with her beloved Roo, a whippet and chihuahua mix.

Above the mantel hangs a painting by Haitian artist Ramphis Magloire. To the left is an etching by Brice Marden, a gift from the artist to Craig-Martin’s son on the occasion of his birth. To the right is a Fernand Léger print, a gift from the homeowner’s artist father, Michael Craig-Martin. A mix of Indian, Moroccan, Italian, Chinese, and Indonesian art and furnishings testifies to a well-traveled life.

I was fully expecting the polychrome Victorian house to be crammed full of furniture, as seen in the online listing. But when Helen, Julia, and I stepped in, it was breathtakingly empty, save for shafts of winter sunlight that illuminated the generous rooms and old wood floors. It became clear that the grotesque furniture I’d seen in the photos was virtual—a terrifying misuse of AI. We sat on the old iron radiators, quietly absorbing the beautiful proportions and unexpected calm. It was fabulous—that was undeniable—but with five bedrooms, it was far more house than I was looking for, and seemed daunting for one person. Helen disagreed. At the next stop, she took one look at the house from the car and didn’t bother to get out.

Craig-Martin seated on a chaise on the terrace off the dining room. A painting by Joe Brainard hangs above a vase from Regan & Smith in Hudson, New York.

George Nakashima chairs surround a table from India made of dismantled boat parts.

An Indonesian stool sits beside a rattan chaise beneath an Indian elephant mobile on the terrace.

A barracuda sculpture by Dalwa Donoffre rests on an Aalto stool in the window bay of the dining room. Beside it, an Ashley Bickerton sculpture sits on a marble pedestal from Rajasthan, surrounded by Stone Age tools, a whale vertebra, and a turquoise bowl by Mary Heilmann.

In the entry hall, a Josef Albers print is installed above a rustic table with a Cameroonian fertility figure.

Over the following months I continued looking in the area, but nothing struck a chord. “You should be thinking about Willard,” Helen kept saying. “It’s a good house.” Completed in 1875, the classic Victorian was built during the whaling boom that enriched Hudson and dotted the town with other equally eccentric Victorian homes replete with multiple bow windows, terraces, and turrets. It had been standing empty and neglected for years, but a thorough assessment did not threaten any looming “old house” disasters. Unable to get the beauty of the place out of my mind, I made an offer.

An ant sculpture from Deyrolle in Paris joins an array of copper pans in the kitchen.

A pastel drawing by Finbar Craig-Martin hangs above a Chinese console table with bagel sculptures by Hanna Liden.

I enlisted a friend to help me move in, though furniture would have to come later. We drove up from the city in a rented van filled with Moroccan rugs and two inflatable mattresses, and ate pizza on the floor in front of a roaring fire. Two years later, I can’t imagine living without the house’s spaciousness and light, or its grand old-world flourishes. Frankly, I don’t know how I coped for so many years without a Norma Desmond staircase. It seems unrealistic now to imagine life without one, which confirms my suspicion that luxury is a one-way trip.

Artworks in the primary bedroom include (clockwise from far left) a painting by Michael Craig-Martin, a painting by Charlie Lucas (above mantel), a light sculpture by Theo Rosenblum (on mantel), a Jessica Craig-Martin photograph of Fran Lebowitz (floor), and a round painting on glass by Finbar Craig-Martin.

A crayon drawing of the homeowner at age two by her mother, Jan Hashey, sits on a Balinese child’s chair in the bathroom.

Jessica Craig-Martin in the bedroom of her son, Finbar. To the right of a movie poster from Ghana is a photograph by Jason Noyes.

Craig-Martin’s son’s room has a Paul McCobb desk, an Eames chair modified by artists Eric Wesley and Piero Golia. and a poster of Ursula Andress from the movie Dr. No above a rustic kitchen cabinet.

In Finbar’s room, a Nate Lowman pizza painting hangs beside a foam-core cutout of David Bowie.

I added a red refrigerator and stove to match the kitchen’s vintage red Formica countertops, and a lifetime’s worth of art and ephemera, from artwork by my parents and friends to artifacts, textiles, and natural oddities collected on various travels. There are unnatural oddities, too. For example, I have a weakness for cheap Japanese plushy cushions, my favorite being a huge pink peach that, when viewed from a certain angle, morphs unmistakably into a huge pink ass. It is also evident that I favor crocodiles and alligators in any form, though if you asked me why, I couldn’t say. One thing is certain: We are a very long way from “form follows function.” Fortunately, at least to my eye, the house absorbed my proclivity for both the ridiculous and the sublime with grace. Helen was right. It is a good house, and it has welcomed me, perhaps recognizing in me a madness that in some way matches its own.

The home office/library features an Alvar Aalto chair and stool, a low alligator table Craig-Martin found on the street in New York City, and a totem from the Solomon Islands displayed beside the fireplace. On the mantel, a Michael Craig-Martin hammer sculpture is surmounted by a Brice Marden etching. A tower of Hermès boxes holds a corner of the room.

Detail of the Hermès box tower. Behind it is an etching by Joel Shapiro, a gift from the artist.

Exterior view of Willard House and its eccentric, polychromatic Victorian façade.

Jessica Craig-Martin’s memoir, I Regret I Am Able to Attend, publishes November 10, 2026.

#4997. Gemini’s quarterly retail trading volume sinks to 3-year low

Gemini’s third-quarter trading rebound masked a deepening retail slump.

Crypto exchange Gemini has just reported its lowest quarterly retail trading volume in three years, despite an increase in overall crypto trading volume.

Total spot trading volume rose 18% from the second quarter to $4.5 billion, according to Gemini’s Q3 earnings report. Institutional volume climbed 26% to $3.9 billion, while retail volume fell 14% to $600 million, its lowest level since the third quarter of 2023.

Gemini said the quarterly increase in spot trading volume was “supported by increased institutional Zcash activity and expanded affiliated market-making,” but did not give a specific reason for the decline in retail trading.

Despite the retail slump, Gemini’s exchange revenue increased 7% from the previous quarter to $13.4 million as institutional revenue more than doubled. Retail traders remained its largest source of exchange revenue, contributing nearly three-quarters of the total.

A number of crypto exchanges also reported softer crypto trading activity in the most recent quarter.

Coinbase reported softer retail and institutional activity, with consumer spot trading volume falling 9% from the previous quarter to $23.4 billion. Coinbase chief financial officer Alesia Haas said “continued growth in prediction markets helped offset softer crypto trading activity.”

At Robinhood, crypto revenue fell 55% year-on-year to $120 million, offset by a sixfold increase in event-contract revenue to $173 million.

Related: Strategy stock sheds 5% amid MSCI index removal, Bitcoin sell-off

Gemini also got some help from its own prediction exchange, Gemini Predictions, with revenue from that business unit increasing 52% quarter-on-quarter to $796,000, though it wasn’t significant enough to offset declines elsewhere.

Gemini reported stronger prediction-market activity heading into the fourth quarter, with average daily traders in October running about 50% above the third-quarter average.

Nearly a third of new prediction traders were new to Gemini, more than double the proportion in Q3.

The company is also seeking to distribute its event contracts through other platforms. An August nonbinding agreement with Apex Fintech Solutions would make Gemini Titan the exclusive venue for crypto event contracts distributed through Apex’s futures brokerage to its brokerage customers, subject to definitive agreements.

Gemini is set to host a Q3 earnings call on Friday.

#4996. Why the Path to Quantum Resistance Runs Uphill for Bitcoin Wallet Makers

Why the Path to Quantum Resistance Runs Uphill for Bitcoin Wallet Makers

Bitcoin custody has always involved some degree of risk, but wallet manufacturers like Trezor—which build physical devices designed to keep a user’s private keys secure offline—are preparing for a post-quantum world where new challenges arise.

That’s because SHRINCS, the community’s leading candidate for a post-quantum signature scheme, allows users to make transactions using stateful signatures that require rigid state tracking to prevent funds from being stolen, according to Trezor CTO Tomáš Sušánka.

Proposed late last year by Blockstream Research Director Jonas Nick and cryptographer Mikhail Kudinov, SHRINCS centers on a composite hash-based approach: users can make transactions using either stateful signatures or stateless signatures.

Stateless signatures are quite large—potentially affecting fees, signature speed, and other efficiency metrics—but are safe to reuse, while smaller, more efficient stateful signatures come with serious security risks when reused beyond a particular signing budget.

The stateful component of SHRINCS is made up of a tree of one-time signatures, and if these keys are accidentally used more than once, enough cryptographic information is revealed for someone else to forge that user’s signature and effectively steal funds.

If Bitcoin successfully adopts SHRINCS, maintaining a meticulous count of the wallet’s state will become a critical responsibility for wallet developers—with lots of potential edge-case security risks.

“You’re giving them an option to mess up,” Sušánka said during a recent episode of MARA Foundation TV, in reference to state tracking. “It’s our job to make it in a secure way, right? It shouldn't be the user’s job.”

To mitigate this risk, Trezor is weighing options for how to support secure UX in a post-quantum world. For instance, Sušánka said the company is considering making the heavier, slower stateless signatures the default setting for all users, tucking the riskier stateful option behind an advanced settings wall.

While Sušánka recognizes that SHRINCS may be the conservative cryptographic choice because it relies on the same cryptographic assumptions as SHA-256, he is “kind of torn on the topic because maintaining the state is a burden.”

“For example, you can have two Trezors with the same recovery seed. How do you share the state between those two? You really don’t,” he added.

“That being said, as it is with everything, this is about trade-offs,” Sušánka continued. “The [stateful] signatures are 10 times smaller ... That makes a big difference. So, I just don't want to throw it off the table completely.”

Sušánka went on to share that while many are focused on Bitcoin’s migration path to post-quantum addresses and signatures, hardware wallet devices will also need to be physically updated for full quantum protection. Most signing devices use quantum-vulnerable cryptography for hardware-specific security functions, like firmware and device authentication.

If users want to ensure their devices are secure in a post-quantum world, they will need to use “quantum-ready” devices—that is, devices that use post-quantum cryptography for device and firmware authentication as well.

In anticipation of these needs, the company is already shipping products like Trezor Safe 7 that support post-quantum cryptography for wallet authentication processes. Of course, transactions made on these devices will only be quantum-secure once Bitcoin itself upgrades to post-quantum security.

That said, Sušánka warned developers not to wait too long to give users migration options, as he believes the migration of potentially millions of users will take time.

“People should be ready to make compromises,” Sušánka said. “If we’re all going to be very strict on what can and cannot be done, we’re not going to get anywhere.”

André Beganski is a Senior Content Manager at MARA Foundation, producing content on topics including the intersection of quantum computing and cryptography. He has previously worked for Decrypt.

#4994. 7 Best Faux Garlands for Long-Lasting Holiday Cheer (2026)

With Halloween in the rearview, it’s time to take down the cobwebs and inflatable skulls and go full throttle into the holiday season. While seasonal garlands are fittingly festive, the natural editions tend to make a mess. For anyone who’d rather not be tasked with vacuuming the dead pine needles that seem to accumulate by the minute, faux garlands are a worthy solution. They offer the look (and often the feel) of the real thing without the sticky sap or endless cleanup.

Fake garlands have come a long way since their humble, low-quality beginnings. Now made with premium artificial foliage and durable wire frames, some of our favorites are nearly indistinguishable from their natural counterparts. Better yet, you can use them year after year to deck the halls (and mantels, bannisters, front porches… you get it), sometimes both indoors and out. No need to strip a tree of its limbs; simply light a pine-scented candle and let everyone think you did.

There’s not much more you could want in a garland than what this single, super-realistic-looking strand can accomplish. Afloral's bestselling Norfolk pine garland is eight feet long and more voluminous than many faux alternatives. Its branches and needles are intentionally spaced irregularly to give it a natural appearance, and the wired base makes it easy to shape, drape, and arrange just about anywhere. Wind it around a staircase or use it to dress up your front door; the possibilities are pretty much endless.

For those looking for a pre-arranged garland, we recommend Crate & Barrel’s Kew Garden garland by florist Abigail Ahern. A curated selection of faux evergreen fronds, berries, and twigs combines with velvet ribbons for an English-cottage-inspired centerpiece. The decoration work has been done for you; all you need to do now is hang it.

Simple but effective, the Fraser Fir is the kind of garland that practically begs to be draped over your mantel. Constructed with Balsam Hill’s proprietary True Needle foliage, the Fraser’s natural texture and full volume (read: no bare spots) make it look so convincingly real that your friends will wonder how you keep your home so tidy all season long. It’s available in multiple lengths (including the 10-foot strand for $159), with the option to add LED lights if you're after a little extra twinkle.

If traditional greenery isn't quite your thing, consider this garnet-hued strand as a fun alternative. Made of clusters of velvety, flocked bobbles, it adds rich color and plenty of texture to your seasonal setup. Weave it through a wreath or let it take center stage on its own. It's a little unexpected, which is sometimes exactly what a room needs.

If your priority in a faux garland is a natural-looking arrangement, consider this six-foot garland from McGee & Co., which has all the makings of a woodland find. Natural-looking pinecones and delicate, handcrafted white berries dot its lush, layered swaths of greenery, giving it plenty of intrigue without rendering it overly adorned. It's a lovely option for anyone who prefers their holiday decorations a little more rustic than glitzy.

This faux garland adds bright red berries and natural-looking pinecones to its lifelike pine greenery for an overall look that feels like it was gathered on a winter walk. At six feet long, it’s quite versatile, able to dress up a mantel or wind around a staircase. A plaid ribbon makes a nice finishing touch, if you're so inclined.

Who says Christmas decorations have to be red and green? This playful ball garland from CB2 takes a more eye-catching approach, combining warm metallic tones in a mix of sizes for plenty of visual interest. It's an apt design choice if you’re planning to go full tinsel mode this season, or simply looking to add a little shine to an otherwise understated setup.

#4993. Strategy Made $19.5 Billion in Profit. So Why Is MSCI Removing It From Stock Indexes?

Strategy Made $19.5 Billion in Profit. So Why Is MSCI Removing It From Stock Indexes?

Strategy made $19.53 billion in profit last quarter. Yet MSCI, one of the world’s biggest providers of stock indexes, has decided Michael Saylor’s Bitcoin company no longer belongs in some of its major investment benchmarks.

MSCI’s November review lists “STRATEGY A”—the company’s MSTR shares—for deletion from the MSCI USA Index after trading closes November 30. That also removes the stock from broader indexes that include U.S. companies, such as the MSCI World and MSCI All Country World indexes.

That sounds technical, but the distinction matters: MSCI is not removing Strategy from the Nasdaq stock exchange, shutting down its business, or making it sell Bitcoin. It is taking MSTR out of stock lists that investment funds use to decide which companies to own.

Consider an ETF designed to copy the MSCI USA Index. When MSTR disappears from the list, the fund generally has to sell its shares, regardless of whether the fund manager likes Saylor’s strategy.

This is why it’s bad news for Strategy. It means less indirect exposition to investments.

MSCI adopted new rules in October for so-called non-operating companies. The idea is to keep investment vehicles—businesses that mainly hold financial assets—out of indexes meant to track operating companies. Its screening process considers a company’s operating assets and other financial measures.

MSCI’s deletion list does not spell out exactly which tests Strategy failed. The company, however, argues that raising capital, managing its Bitcoin treasury and issuing financial products amount to a genuine operating business.

In an August letter to MSCI, Strategy called the proposal “discriminatory, arbitrary, and misguided.” It argued that MSCI should be a “mirror of the market, not an arbiter of it.”

Strategy also maintains a software business, but Bitcoin now dominates its finances. The disagreement is whether its Bitcoin activity counts as running a company or mainly managing an investment portfolio.

Strategy’s latest quarterly earnings show $19.53 billion in net income. But that does not mean its software customers paid the company anything close to that amount.

Nearly all of its $20.91 billion in reported operating income came from a rise in the accounting value of its Bitcoin. Under rules adopted in 2025, changes in Bitcoin’s market price appear in Strategy’s earnings even when it has not sold the coins. A decline in Bitcoin can produce the opposite result.

Its software division, meanwhile, recorded $125.4 million in quarterly revenue, down 3% from a year earlier. Its $477.2 million in revenue for all of 2025 was slightly below the $486.3 million reported in 2019, before Strategy began buying Bitcoin.

The immediate risk is selling pressure on MSTR, not on Bitcoin itself. Strategy estimated that funds linked to MSCI indexes hold roughly 13 million of its shares, or 3.1% of its outstanding common stock. Not all of that necessarily translates into sudden sales, and Strategy says the amount is manageable.

But Strategy has repeatedly raised money by selling shares and other securities, then used those proceeds to buy Bitcoin. If its stock falls, selling the same number of new shares brings in less cash.

So the company would have to issue more shares to raise the same amount—or buy fewer Bitcoin.

That is why a stock-index decision could eventually matter to the crypto market. The exclusion does not force a Bitcoin sale, and it does not guarantee fewer purchases. It could, however, weaken a key source of funding for one of Bitcoin’s biggest corporate buyers.

#4991. The 17 Most Beautiful Ski Resorts to Visit This Winter—Including Three in the US

When winter arrives, the world’s most idyllic mountain towns transform into playgrounds for serious adrenaline seekers. But, as the most beautiful ski resorts prove, the appeal of an extraordinary alpine escape extends well beyond its slopes. Why kick off your ski boots just anywhere when you could do so in a castle-like hotel in the Swiss Alps or a secluded Japanese ryokan tucked among snow-covered forests? Sure, the world-renowned runs are worth traveling for, but sometimes so are the ski hotels themselves. Think Michelin-starred restaurants, award-winning spas, art-filled interiors, and architecture that makes the most of its dramatic surroundings. Here, AD rounds up 17 of the most beautiful ski resorts to visit this winter, whether you’re an alpine ace, a beginner ski bum, or purely an après enthusiast.

Getting to Zaborin Ryokan is an adventure in itself, which means you know it’ll be good. After landing at New Chitose Airport in Sapporo, it’s a two-hour drive to the ryokan, which is hidden in the Hanazono woods of Hokkaido. Arriving at the property feels like discovering some sort of secretive lair from a Bond flick—a series of low-lying villas covered in snow suddenly reveal themselves. Built on natural hot springs, each of the villas has its own indoor and outdoor onsen, plus floor-to-ceiling windows and a private balcony with views of Mount Yōtei, the area’s dormant volcano, in the distance. Make an appointment with the in-house matcha master and prepare your taste buds for a multicourse Japanese kaiseki meal. If you can tear yourself away from your absurdly serene suite long enough to ski or snowboard, the ryokan will arrange your transfer to and from Hanazono Niseko ski area, just seven minutes away by car.

Modernist design meets mountainside adventure at Pendry Park City, a ski-in, ski-out luxury resort where “hitting the slopes” means basically just stepping outside. Tucked in Canyons Village at Park City Mountain—the largest ski resort in the US, with over 7,000 skiable acres spread across two connected mountains—this slopeside sanctuary is right next to the brand-new, high-speed Sunrise Gondola. After a day appreciating the powder, guests gather at Après Pendry for fondue and crème brulée espresso martinis either by the fire or outside. Alternatively, they head up to the rooftop for a dip in the year-round heated pool and hot tub, plus immaculate mountain views.

Located in St. Moritz, Switzerland, the Carlton Hotel boasts 60 luxurious suites that blend old-world grandeur with contemporary elements. The resort is also home to the Michelin-starred restaurant Da Vittorio.

The Gstaad Palace in Switzerland opened in 1913 and has catered to jet-setters and stars such as Elizabeth Taylor, Grace Kelly, and Marlene Dietrich throughout its long history. The castlelike hotel overlooks the streets of Gstaad, which are filled with high-end boutiques.

The Pines Lodge, a RockResort is a rustic Colorado retreat with ski-in, ski-out access set above Beaver Creek Village. After a long day on the slopes, guests can enjoy views of the Rockies from the acclaimed Grouse Mountain Grill.

Architect Mario Botta designed the striking spa at the Tschuggen Grand Hotel in Arosa, Switzerland. The alpine retreat, which was built in 1929, features 130 rooms and suites with opulent interiors by Carlo Rampazzi.

Designed by architect Sybille de Margerie, the intimate Cheval Blanc Courchevel in Courchevel, France, is the ultimate in alpine luxury. The resort, which is owned by LVMH chairman Bernard Arnault, is also filled with blue-chip artwork by Michel Othoniel, Xavier Veillant, and Bruno Peinado, whose Trojan Horse sculpture is seen here.

Founded by railway tycoon William Cornelius Van Horne in 1888, the Fairmont Banff Springs is set within Banff National Park and offers stunning views of the Canadian Rockies. The sprawling hotel, which was inspired by a Scottish baronial castle, includes 764 rooms and suites and nine restaurants and bars.

The St. Regis Aspen Resort in the Colorado Rockies received a $40 million makeover by interior architect Lauren Rottet in 2012. Set at the base of Aspen Mountain, the hotel includes 179 rooms and suites outfitted with Ralph Lauren beds and desks and marble-lined baths.

The ski-in, ski-out Hôtel des Dromonts in Avoriaz, France, was built in the 1960s by architect Jacques Labro, who was inspired by the work of Le Corbusier and Charlotte Perriand. The interiors of the distinctive angular building were recently refreshed and upgraded by designer Jocelyne Sibuet.

Munich-based firm Oikios Architect took inspiration from pine cones for the design of the AlpenGold Hotel in Davos, Switzerland. The oval façade uses over 790 curved, overlapping steel panels to create the scaled appearance. Inside, 216 tasteful guest rooms overlook the city—home to the World Economic Forum—and surrounding mountains.

Offering a unique combination of Asian-inspired design motifs and alpine rustic touches, The Chedi Andermatt is one of Switzerland’s most wonderful offerings. The boutique hotel is located 4,747 feet above sea level and includes a 25,833-square-foot spa to take advantage of when you’re not out on the slopes.

With rustic and understated architecture and interiors, Le Chalet Zanier is designed to mimic the natural beauty of the surrounding Mont Blanc mountain range. The resort is made up of a 12-room main structure as well as a separate three-bedroom private cottage. Like the snowflakes that top the roofs, no room is exactly like any of the others.

Located in a densely wooded area, Forestis is situated exactly where the Austrian monarchy established a tuberculosis sanatorium in 1912. Designed with simplicity in mind, the rich stone, wood, and glass of the hotel are inspired by the nature that surrounds it.

#4988. Etsy Says Gah! The Handmade Marketplace and Playful Fashion Label Team Up on a Home Collection

Since its founding in 2015, Lisa Says Gah has become known for its bowtie-pasta cardigans, checkerboard coats, and swans. Today, the California-based label is releasing its first home collection, which has a similarly playful ethos: “The Lisa Says Gah world really naturally extends into the home,” says founder Lisa Bühler. “Home and clothing ultimately tell the story of who you are.”

A pin board and two eclectic frames from Frame Mavens comprise a third of the collection.

Founder Lisa Bühler co-judged the Etsy Design Awards and selected three winners to co-design Etsy Says Gah!, her brand’s first foray into pieces for the home.

The collection is a collaboration with Etsy, a platform Bühler often uses for her own inspiration (things that make her shout: Gah!). “When you go to Etsy, you’re always going to find something that delights you,” she says.

Lisa Says Gah has stocked independent designers alongside its own line for more than a decade. This year, Bühler co-judged the Etsy Design Awards, and selected three winners to co-design Etsy Says Gah!, a nine-piece collection exclusively available via Etsy. Bühler says she prioritized “unexpected, collected things that you just don’t automatically go to when you think of homeware.”

UK-based Cait Mellor executed Bühler’s mood board through crochet in a floral table runner, a horseshoe decal, and swan coasters.

The home collection’s foundational inspiration comes from Lisa Says Gah’s two fall lines, Pastoral Daze and Swan Dance, which pair horses and swans with paisleys and cabin-inspired knits. Bühler worked with the Etsy designers by sharing mood boards, giving notes when she wanted more of a motif or a different color combination, and trusting each maker with her own medium. “They’re the expert[s],” she says. “The process felt like really trusting and putting it in their hands.”’

Each maker applied the same mood board differently. Jacqueline Sanchez, a Houston-based ceramicist who won this year’s Grand Prize for a vase covered in more than 1,500 hand-applied scales, sculpted the swan in relief on a pale pink vase wound with green vines. She also set a horse’s head in a blue baroque cameo on a cream mug and dotted a wavy platter with tiny fruit as a nod to the brand’s food prints. “[I liked] having the freedom to incorporate elements that feel really true to my work,” says Sanchez. The vase is a piece Bühler keeps coming back to: “You would just be happy to have it on the table without flowers,” she says.

The vase is the piece Bühler keeps coming back to: “You would just be happy to have it on the table without flowers,” she says.

“Because it’s our first home collection, we wanted it to feel like something you collect over time in your house versus a uniform grouping,” says Bühler.

Cait Mellor, a UK-based seller, worked the swan and horse elements into the open grid of filet crochet: swans on a set of coasters, a horseshoe under the word Lucky on a small tapestry, and flowers down a cream table runner. Her goal is to “bring this traditional art form into a more contemporary setting.” Bühler is already picturing the coasters at dinner parties, where “everyone is going to talk about the swans.”

Emma Miller and Kristen Kellar, chosen for their fabric-wrapped frames, focused on color and pattern rather than the animals. They made a burgundy frame with black polka dots on a blue background, a frame featuring blue pixelated flowers that look almost cross-stitched, and a pin board that uses pink fabric crossed with orange ribbon.

The home collection’s foundational inspiration comes from Lisa Says Gah’s two fall lines, Pastoral Daze and Swan Dance, which pair horses and swans with paisleys and cabin-inspired knits.

Bühler didn’t want the nine pieces to look like a matched set. “Because it’s our first home collection, we wanted it to feel like something you collect over time in your house versus a uniform grouping,” she says. Each item costs less than $200, and with the holidays coming—with endless-feeling opportunities for hosting and gifting—now is a great time to start collecting.

#4987. North Korea Stole $400 Million in Crypto Using Deepfake Video Calls, DOJ Says

North Korea Stole $400 Million in Crypto Using Deepfake Video Calls, DOJ Says

North Korean hackers have stolen more than $400 million in cryptocurrency over the past 18 months using a social-engineering scheme built on secretly recorded footage and AI deepfakes, the U.S. Justice Department said Thursday, as it announced the seizure of the infrastructure behind the operation.

The scheme, which the FBI calls “FaceStealer,” turns video conference calls into a trap. According to the DOJ, hackers lure targets into meetings using accounts stolen from previous victims, then play prerecorded webcam footage of trusted contacts to simulate their presence and win the target’s confidence.

The group also generates deepfake videos of well-known figures in the crypto and related industries, using AI to make the ruse more convincing.

Once a target is at ease, the attackers manufacture a fake technical glitch, such as claiming the person can’t be heard, and offer a link to “fix” it, which instead installs malware.

From there, the hackers can drain crypto wallets or exploit the victim’s access to other systems, sometimes waiting months or more than a year before striking. The FBI said it seized over 900 webcam recordings of FaceStealer victims under a federal warrant.

The DOJ tied the operation to North Korean groups known variously as Sapphire Sleet, BlueNoroff and APT38, among others, and warned the hackers have increasingly targeted open-source software developers in an apparent bid to enable supply-chain attacks.

The announcement came paired with an update on the record-breaking Binance hack. The department said it has now obtained court authorization to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion of the roughly $6.4 billion stolen from the exchange on Oct. 29, part of over $2.7 billion frozen so far.

The FBI attributed that theft to the North Korean group TraderTraitor, and the regime has been laundering the proceeds at speed, spinning up a new wallet roughly every 25 seconds. North Korea has denied involvement, calling the FBI’s finding a political provocation.

The actions underscore how central crypto theft has become to Pyongyang’s finances. The U.S. recently sanctioned 11 people over a North Korean IT-worker scheme that moved millions in crypto, and researchers have cataloged the many ways the regime’s hackers break into crypto firms.

Officials said efforts to trace and recover the stolen Binance funds are ongoing.

#4985. Diana Ross at Home: 10 Photos of the Pop Diva Off-Duty

As the breakout star of the Supremes and later a solo sensation, Diana Ross proved herself a force to be reckoned with. The Detroit native’s booming career allowed the singer-slash-actor to fashion her own residences in her own singular style: A statement purple sofa, a shag-lined conversation pit, beaded curtains, and a mirrored piano were among the features that might’ve stood out elsewhere, but all appeared seamlessly at home with The Boss. As she declared in her 1979 anthem of independence: “There’s my chair / I put it there / Everything you see / Is with love and care / It’s my house and I live here.”

Through 12 number-one singles with The Supremes, six number-one hits as a solo artist, a Lifetime Achievement Award from the Recording Academy, and a Presidential Medal of Freedom, Ross apparently managed to maintain a sense of normalcy in her domestic life. “My mom is such a mom,” Ross’s daughter, actor Tracee Ellis Ross, said on The Kelly Clarkson Show. “I had a really regular upbringing.” Read on for a roundup of the icon in her offstage moments.

Ross poses on a floral sofa in her Miami suite in this photo, dated March 17, 1970. It was a big year for the songstress: She performed her final concert as lead singer of The Supremes that January, and her debut solo album was released in June.

The singer unwinds on the sofa during an interview.

When these portraits were snapped, Ross was in the middle of a nine-day run of shows at Miami’s Eden Roc hotel—her official debut solo show after a preview run in Framingham, Massachusetts. “Welcome to the ‘Let’s see if Diana Ross can do it by herself club,’” the vocalist said during the performance, journalist Shirley Eder wrote in a March 20, 1970, issue of The Philadelphia Inquirer. Eder also reported that “Diana takes a long time to unwind after a performance… she screens movies in her suite almost nightly.”

A pregnant Ross leans against her reflective piano.

Ross, eight months pregnant with Tracee in this 1972 photo, leans against the mirrored piano in her Beverly Hills home during an interview about her first film role: Billie Holiday in Lady Sings the Blues, directed by Sidney J. Furie. Ross’s depiction of the jazz singer garnered her a Golden Globe and an Oscar nomination, among other accolades. “She’s the greatest improviser I’ve ever known,” Furie told The Hollywood Reporter in 2021. “Some of the main scenes were improvised…. She was the consummate pro and not a diva at all.”

The musician at home with her daughter Rhonda.

Ross lounges in her Beverly Hills home alongside her eldest child, Rhonda, in 1972. The “Ain’t No Mountain High Enough” singer’s career was launched by Motown founder Berry Gordy, and the two shared a relationship that was romantic as well as professional. Rhonda was born in 1971 as a result of that liaison, but Ross married music executive Robert Ellis Silberstein two months into the pregnancy, and he raised Rhonda as his own. The couple welcomed two more daughters together: Tracee, who was born not long after this photo was taken, and Chudney, who was born in 1975.

Ross at home taping an appearance for an ABC special.

In the mid-1970s, The Wiz star was once again photographed on the purple sofa in her Beverly Hills living area, which was complete with a white-shag-lined conversation pit and a mirror-bedecked fireplace. According to a 1972 New York Times profile of the artist, her 6,833-square-foot home had 30-foot-tall ceilings and an Andy Warhol tapestry of Marilyn Monroe. The grounds also hosted a swimming pool and a tennis court.

Ross with daughters Tracee (left) and Rhonda.

The Ross family’s Beverly Hills pad boasted five bedrooms and nine bathrooms. According to The Los Angeles Times, the property was built in 1964 for art collector Donald Factor. Because its walls maximized space for paintings, it was reportedly known in the neighborhood as “the house with no windows.” The conversation pit abutted a window adorned with a beaded curtain, as shown in the background of this photo of Ross with Tracee and Rhonda. Both daughters followed in their mother’s footsteps as performers: Tracee is an actor (who recently won her first Daytime Emmy for the program Solo Traveling with Tracee Ellis Ross), while Rhonda was nominated for a Daytime Emmy for her role in the soap opera Another World.

Ross, flanked by her daughters, cradles infant Chudney.

A newborn Chudney also made an appearance during the mid-’70s shoot, as seen in this snapshot. Ross and Silberstein divorced in 1977, and the singer went on to marry Norwegian businessman Arne Naess Jr. in 1986. With Naess, she welcomed two sons, Ross and Evan. The “I’m Coming Out” singer and her second husband split in 2000.

In 1978, a newly single Ross welcomed Barbara Walters to her Beverly Hills home for an interview about her career and life as a single mother in front of a window overlooking the backyard pool. “Her success has brought her every material comfort,” Walters narrated in the television special. “Believe it or not, within 24 hours before we came here she completely refurnished the living room.” The television special offered a glimpse of the conversation pit, now done up with dark maroon carpet and a navy sofa with white cushions.

Ross dressed to impress for her birthday party.

The “Upside Down” singer celebrated her 38th birthday with a party at her Beverly Hills residence in March 1982, as seen in this glamorous snapshot. Later that year, she released Silk Electric, her 13th studio album, and was honored with a star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame.

Ross’s daughters were in attendance, as shown in this photo of her bustling birthday party. The 13-time Grammy nominee sold the Beverly Hills property three years later for $1.75 million. These days, the 82-year-old icon lives at Quarry Farm, a property in Greenwich, Connecticut. In 2021, she released her 25th studio album, Thank You.

#4984. Republican Senator Wants to Freeze AI Data Center Boom as Congress Pushes to Expand It

Republican Senator Wants to Freeze AI Data Center Boom as Congress Pushes to Expand It

Republican Senator Josh Hawley wants Congress to hit the brakes on new data centers, just as lawmakers are moving forward with legislation intended to make building the infrastructure behind America's AI boom easier.

The Missouri senator called for a nationwide moratorium Wednesday, listing it among five priorities for Senate Republicans during the six weeks before Democrats take control of the chamber.

“Senate Republicans trying to figure out what to do in the next 6 weeks?” Hawley wrote on X. His answer included a moratorium on data centers, alongside defunding Planned Parenthood, suspending the gas tax and other Republican priorities.

A spokesperson told Politico that Hawley wants a federal pause until Congress passes a “bill of rights giving taxpayers guarantees of no rate increases.” Hawley has not presented detailed legislation specifying which projects would stop or how long the freeze would last.

Data centers are the warehouses packed with powerful computers that train and operate services such as ChatGPT. Building more AI systems means installing more servers, which requires additional electricity, cooling equipment and often upgrades to local power grids.

Senate Republicans trying to figure out what to do in the next 6 weeks? Here you go:DEFUND Planned ParenthoodMORATORIUM on data centersSUSPEND the gas tax FUND rural hospitals SAVE AMERICA ACT

— Josh Hawley (@HawleyMO) November 11, 2026

The scale helps explain the concern. U.S. data centers consumed an estimated 183 terawatt-hours of electricity in 2024, according to figures cited by Pew, and that could climb to 426 by 2030. In other words, their annual electricity demand could more than double in six years.

On Monday, the Senate voted 73-25 to advance debate on the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, a proposal backed by lawmakers from both parties and President Donald Trump's administration.

The bill would speed federal reviews for energy projects and transmission lines, helping bring new power supplies online. It would also require data center operators to pay the additional grid costs their projects create, rather than shifting those expenses onto ordinary electricity customers.

In practical terms, Congress is considering how to keep construction moving without making households subsidize it. Hawley is asking lawmakers to stop new construction until consumer protections are in place.

His position is not entirely new. In February, Hawley introduced the GRID Act with Democratic Senator Richard Blumenthal, seeking to make new data centers obtain electricity from their own sources rather than relying on existing grid supplies.

He now joins independent Senator Bernie Sanders and Democratic Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez in publicly supporting a national pause. Their moratorium proposal goes further, calling for rules addressing AI's effects on workers and public safety as well as the environment.

The politics have shifted quickly. In the November 3 elections, voters backed restrictions on data center development across the country, including local bans and measures requiring public approval before construction.

A Pew Research Center analysis published Wednesday found 60% of Americans uncomfortable with a new data center in their area, including 54% of Republicans and 68% of Democrats. Another survey found 58% disliked political leaders who support building data centers near them.

Opponents worry about rising bills, water consumption and noise. Cities have already imposed restrictions, while OpenAI recently promised to cover electricity and grid costs at future campuses it owns and operates.

Industry supporters see a bigger economic risk. BlackRock CEO Larry Fink warned last month that a construction moratorium could trigger a recession because so much investment now depends on AI infrastructure.

A federal construction freeze could delay the additional computing capacity that AI companies expect to bring online, along with the power infrastructure built to support it. Existing data centers would not necessarily shut down, but the scope of Hawley's proposed moratorium remains undefined.

Hawley's call does not freeze a single project, and he has not said whether he would support the Sanders bill. For now, the Senate is debating legislation to speed infrastructure approvals, not legislation to ban new data centers.

#4981. Andy Baraghani’s New NYC Restaurant is Full of Design Surprises

“I’ve always been obsessed with design, since I was a kid,” says Andy Baraghani. “I love how restaurant interiors can take you to a different place.”

At Ditto—the all-day café he is opening in New York’s Flatiron—the chef, cookbook author, and YouTube star has given that obsession free rein. With each design decision—from the vintage glass-prism Holophane pendants in the front window to the intense blue (tinted to match Benjamin Moore Buckland Blue) of the rear dining area’s Venetian plaster walls—he asked himself: “Would I actually do this in my home?” If the answer was yes, it passed the test.

The interior of Ditto, with Venetian plaster walls in a shade matching Benjamin Moore Buckland Blue, and Model S22 chairs from Galvanitas.

For Andy Baraghani, design is something of a family affair. His husband, Keith Pollock, is a former AD editor and now a West Elm executive. But opening a restaurant was never part of the plan. In fact, when Michael Silber, a restaurant investor behind New York hot spots like Borgo, Penny, and Bistrot Ha, approached him with the idea of a laid-back restaurant open for breakfast, lunch, and dinner, Baraghani admits his first reaction was: “I have no interest.”

It wasn’t for lack of experience. He had started his career in restaurants—from Chez Panisse in Berkeley, where he worked as a teenager, to Estela in New York—before becoming a food editor at Bon Appetit and winning a James Beard Award for his cookbook, The Cook You Want to Be.

But he bonded with Silber over their shared love of greenmarket cooking and decorating deep dives. When it came to Ditto, he says, he wanted the space to convey his personal style. It helped that he and Silber were on the same page. “Andy and I talked about styles ranging from Bauhaus, de Stijl, and classic modernism to 1970s and ’80s aesthetics from places like Italy and France,” Silber says.

They brought in like-minded collaborators: interior designer Eric Wink and architect Alta Indelman. Wink, a close friend of Baraghani who helped with the design of his and Pollock’s home, says the partners told him that New York’s much-missed City Bakery, which was two blocks uptown from Ditto’s 16th Street location, was an inspiration. “They wanted it to feel casual and approachable, with a bit of a New York diner quality,” Wink says.

Holophane pendant lamps hang in the restaurant’s front window.

The space, in a 1905 former factory (its last incarnation was an agnès b. store), is divided into three zones. In the front section, visitors can shop at a market area with hand-picked groceries. A glossy white counter with Bauhaus curves anchors the middle section; this is where customers line up, take a number, and either carry the food out or dine at laminate-topped tables under a trio of oversized custom fabric pendants. The menu, handwritten on a roll of butcher’s paper and updated daily, features dishes like pomegranate spoon salad and what Baraghani calls “the Platonic ideal of granola,” with tahini, maple syrup, and yogurt drizzled with fig-leaf oil.

The third zone, the one painted blue, features an open kitchen and café tables pulled up to a banquette that runs the length of the room. “There’s that good chaos in the middle and the back is a calmer moment,” says Baraghani.

Indelman, a veteran collaborator with New York restaurateurs like Andrew Tarlow and Gabe Stulman, was tasked with giving the space a utilitarian, high-tech ambience. She clad the walls and the open kitchen in patinated stainless steel and preserved industrial elements like structural arches and polished concrete floors.

But it was Baraghani and Silber who took the lead on honing the visual details of the interiors. “We went through 50 possibilities to find the right chair,” Silber says. The one they selected—a 1960s Dutch industrial chair, the Model S22 from Galvanitas—was sourced from Amsterdam Modern in Los Angeles.

Silber also spent hours on eBay bidding on midcentury ceramic bud vases. “They were each under $20 but put them together on a shelf and you have your own little Giorgio Morandi tableau,” he says.

White wall speakers and a French ceramic mask hang above the banquette, tables, and stools upholstered in Maharam’s Stripe It fabric.

The no-nonsense decor also features white wall speakers, created for the space by sound artist Tom Bloxam, and programmed with playlists created by Michael Scanlon of Chandelier Creative, which oversaw Ditto’s branding. But there are quirkier moments too. When it came to choosing fabric for the custom stools, Baraghani insisted on Maharam’s Stripe It, which he felt had a “Baja California” vibe. “We needed that twist,” he says. For his part, Wink specified his favorite stainless-steel toilet for the restrooms—Neo-Metro’s wall-mounted MiniLoo. “I added a custom wood top to soften it a bit,” Wink says.

Meanwhile, a pair of massive midcentury French ceramic faces that hang on a wall opposite the menu of the day are on loan from Baraghani’s own home. “They anchor the space and add a touch of craft,” he says. “I just knew they needed to be here.”

#4978. Congress Summons Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, Lutnick Over Chip Exports to China

Congress Summons Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, Lutnick Over Chip Exports to China

Sen. Tim Scott (R-SC), chairman of the Senate Banking Committee, has asked former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick to testify, widening a congressional inquiry that has moved from Huang's private life to U.S. controls on chip exports to China. "The American people deserve answers," Scott wrote in a tweet announcing the requests on Thursday evening.

In a letter dated November 12, Scott asked Huang to appear voluntarily at a public hearing on December 3 over public reports that "raise significant national security concerns." The committee has jurisdiction over export controls, "including their application to NVIDIA's exports of advanced semiconductors," he wrote. If Huang does not appear, the committee "is prepared to use all tools at our disposal, including subpoenas."

Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive U.S. technology.

I am requesting that NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick testify before the Senate Banking Committee.

The American people deserve… pic.twitter.com/Xu1kiNzkhW

— Senator Tim Scott (@SenatorTimScott) November 13, 2026

Scott asked Lutnick to testify on December 10, noting Commerce's "central role in issuing and enforcing export control regulations." A Lutnick spokesperson told NBC News that he had "proactively agreed to appear voluntarily," while the White House said President Donald Trump "maintains complete confidence" in Lutnick.

Separately on Thursday, the Republican chairs of the House Foreign Affairs, China and Intelligence committees, Reps. Brian Mast (R-FL), John Moolenaar (R-MI) and Rick Crawford (R-AR), wrote to Huang, Nvidia General Counsel Tim Teter and Jeffrey Kessler, the Commerce Department's under secretary for industry and security.

Huang was asked to sit for a closed-door, transcribed interview on December 1 and Teter on December 2, with each given until 8 p.m. on Friday to agree or face a subpoena. Kessler, whose bureau administers export controls, was invited to a hearing on December 3.

"As reports continue to come to light regarding Jensen Huang, NVIDIA, and MegaSpeed, there are clear national security concerns that need to be addressed immediately," the three chairs said in a statement. They called on Congress to add four chip security bills to the annual defense authorization bill.

It is their second request. A letter dated November 4 had asked Huang to brief the committees on November 18. Mast told reporters on Thursday that Huang had agreed to brief lawmakers, NBC News reported.

At the center of the inquiries is Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer. U.S. officials suspected the company of helping Chinese firms get around restrictions on advanced AI chips, The New York Times reported in October. Its sister company in Malaysia bought at least $4.8 billion of AI equipment containing Nvidia chips between June 2024 and February 2026, according to trade records cited by the Times.

Megaspeed told the Times it had hired outside counsel to conduct an internal investigation. Nvidia has said it complies with U.S. export laws, according to NBC News.

The Times reported that U.S. officials had concluded that Alice Huang, a former Megaspeed executive, was a Chinese intelligence asset. China has rejected that as a smear, and Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Thursday that the Justice Department had not reached a conclusion on whether she was a spy, according to BBC News.

Jensen Huang admitted on Sunday to being unfaithful to his wife and resigned on Thursday. Nvidia's board has said it has no reason to believe national security or company secrets were compromised.

Moolenaar told reporters that Huang had "been one of the strongest proponents for not having export controls," Roll Call reported. On Wednesday, Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA), the Banking Committee's ranking member, and Sen. Chris Coons (D-DE) asked Lutnick, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller to preserve records on Nvidia chip exports to China.

China's foreign ministry warned on Friday that tighter U.S. export controls on semiconductors would threaten the stability of global supply chains, Al Jazeera reported.

Nvidia shares closed down 4.3% at $166.19 on Thursday and were up 2.7% at $170.63 in premarket trading early on Friday, according to Yahoo! Finance. The company has lost almost $500 billion in market value since the close on Friday, November 6. It reports quarterly earnings on Tuesday, the first under interim CEO Colette Kress.

#4977. Gwyneth Paltrow Is Launching a New Podcast—Take the First Look Inside the “Goop Guide” Studio

Viewers tuning in to Gwyneth Paltrow’s new podcast, The Goop Guide, will be forgiven for assuming the show was filmed in a room inside her serene and stylish Montecito home. The setting for her new venture (launching on Netflix and Spotify November 11) upends the paradigm of a podcaster on a sofa next to a potted fern.

The Goop Guide is indeed recorded inside a studio, but Paltrow approached the space with just as much intention as her own home, enlisting interior designer Marie Carson to bring it to life. “I wanted it to feel like a beautiful, elevated nook of somebody’s old stone house somewhere,” Paltrow tells AD of the space.

“If we can’t be recording in my home, I want to give guests the feeling of comfort and coziness,” Paltrow says of her goal for the studio’s design.

The catch? Carson only had mere days to turn the sterile studio into something far cozier and conducive to the wide-ranging conversations Paltrow hoped to conduct during the show, which launches with Zoe Saldana as the first guest. “We started to decorate remotely and I looked at it and thought, This is not the direction,” Paltrow says of the space’s first iteration. “And so I called Marie and said, ‘We’re filming in, like, a week and I need you to come and fix this.’”

It wasn’t the tight timeline that initially gave Carson pause. “At first I said, ‘Oh my gosh, I’ve never done a set! What does that mean?’” recalls the designer. “But when Gwyneth said she wanted it to feel like a beautiful room in her home, I thought, Well, I can do that.” Previously, the two had collaborated on a space at Paltrow’s Brentwood property, which meant there was already a creative shorthand between them—incredibly helpful when they had only days to outfit the space before shooting commenced. “Gwyneth has such a strong direction of what she wants and such excellent taste,” Carson says. “So I was able to take her notes and run with it.”

The focal point for the studio’s design is a late-18th-century Flemish tapestry. “It added life to the space,” says Carson.

The Goop Kitchen founder wanted the focal point of the design to be a late-18th-century Flemish tapestry, whose landscape imagery features a palette of muted greens and taupes. “I thought it would be beautiful because of what they’ve lived through and what they’ve seen,” Paltrow says of tapestries. “I love them because you think about all the life going on in front of these things for hundreds and hundreds of years.” To complement the hanging, Carson added a 19th-century Belgian trestle table from Meg Turner Gallery, antique chairs reupholstered in Loro Piana brushed cashmere, and a handwoven hemp and silk rug sourced from Mansour. “Marie’s execution is flawless,” Paltrow says. “She has a real knack for finding the perfect thing that has the right dimensions and warmth.”

Paltrow commissioned a painting of magnolia from artist Rob Reynolds. The sconces are from Goop’s collection with CB2 and the wicker stools are from Galerie Provenence.

A close-up of one of the podcast chairs: an antique sourced from a shop in Pasadena and reupholstered with Loro Piana cashmere.

In the handful of shows Paltrow’s filmed so far, it appears her guests (which include Rick Rubin, Ke Huy Quan, and more) feel similarly at home. “Everyone who walks in is like, ‘I’ve done so many podcasts and I’ve never seen one like this! It feels like a real room and I feel so at ease.’’’ she says. Considering Paltrow will be filming dozens of episodes inside the space, she’s happy it’s comfortable for her as well. “I would definitely live in this room,” she says. “I think about this room all the time now when I’m not in it because I’m so obsessed with it.”

#4976. Trump nominates Hester Peirce successor among SEC, CFTC picks

The White House also named a former CFTC commissioner and two CLARITY Act veterans as nominees to fill vacancies at the SEC and CFTC.

US President Donald Trump has nominated Steven Levine to replace former Securities and Exchange Commission Commissioner Hester Peirce, one of the agency’s most prominent crypto advocates, as part of four nominations to fill vacancies at the SEC and Commodity Futures Trading Commission.

The White House’s latest announcement on Thursday named two nominees each for the SEC and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, agencies whose decisions help shape US cryptocurrency regulation, among other functions. All four nominations require Senate confirmation.

The nominations would replenish several commissioner roles. The SEC currently has only two of its five commissioner seats filled, while CFTC Chairman Michael Selig is the sole member of his agency’s five-seat commission.

The nominations come weeks before Democrats are set to take control of the Senate in January, giving a narrow window for Republicans to confirm Trump’s picks.

Related: House Finance panel chair says regulator actions on crypto ‘fall short’ of CLARITY bill

Levine, a Republican, was nominated for an SEC term ending June 5, 2030, replacing Peirce, who left the agency in October. Peirce was a leading advocate for clearer crypto rules at the SEC, helping shape its approach to digital assets as the former head of its Crypto Task Force, while Levine’s views on crypto regulation are less publicly established.

Trump also nominated Democrat Kristin Johnson, a former CFTC commissioner, for the SEC seat held by Caroline Crenshaw, whose term has also expired. Johnson’s proposed term ends June 5, 2029. Johnson has advocated for stronger customer protections and conflict-of-interest rules for crypto firms in her time at the CFTC.

For the CFTC, Trump nominated Republican Nathan Anonick and Democrat Matthew Rowland, both of whom worked on the Clarity Act. CFTC Chairman Mike Selig called them “two strong bipartisan picks” for CFTC commissioners and said he looked forward to working with them upon their confirmation.

The nominees would typically appear before the Senate Banking Committee for the SEC positions and the Senate Agriculture Committee for the CFTC roles. Following committee consideration, their nominations would go to the full Senate, where confirmation requires a majority vote.

#4974. Bitcoin Drops to $69K On 'Concentrated Liquidation' and 'Weakening' Demand

Bitcoin Drops to $69K On 'Concentrated Liquidation' and 'Weakening' Demand

Leading cryptocurrency Bitcoin has dropped 5.6% on the day, and is currently trading at just over $69,300, per Decrypt data. It comes after the latest consumer price index, or CPI, results came in "hotter than expected," experts said, with the drop was triggered by a "concentrated liquidation."

Bitcoin's plunge took it to a 24-hour low of $67,922, its lowest level since mid-August, when it boosted from $63,000.

"Bitcoin’s drop below $69,000 was directly triggered by a concentrated liquidation of leveraged long positions," Tim Sun, senior analyst at Hashkey, told Decrypt. "Though the deeper issue lies in steadily weakening market demand."

Sun highlighted U.S. Bitcoin spot ETFs recording six consecutive days of net outflows, according to CoinGlass. He also pointed to stablecoin supply shrinking and MSCI removing digital asset treasury firm Strategy from its funds.

"ETFs, stablecoin liquidity, and corporate treasuries have served as the primary capital pillars supporting this Bitcoin rally," Sun said. "With all three coming under simultaneous pressure, the market is losing the marginal buying power that previously propelled prices higher."

Meanwhile, other major cryptocurrencies have also taken a hit. Ethereum has dropped 4.6% on the day to $1,953, BNB has slipped 4.7% to $639.46, and Solana is down 3.1% to $80.47, according to Decrypt data. The global crypto market cap has dropped 4.6% over the past 24 hours to $2.39 trillion.

It follows U.S. CPI rising 0.4% in October, which is "hotter than expected, even if the headline monthly rate eased to 0.4% from September’s 0.6%," according to CoinShares head of research James Butterfill. "More troubling was the composition," he added. "Food rose 0.5%, services remained firm, and core CPI increased 0.3%, with services excluding rent, a gauge of domestic price pressure the Fed watches closely, rising 0.5%. The inflation problem is therefore no longer easily dismissed as an oil story."

The consumer price index, or CPI, is a way of measuring inflation that tracks the average price changes of everyday goods and services over time. Central banks tend to use CPI to inform whether they'll cut or hike interest rates, meaning higher-than-expected CPI signals a potential Federal Reserve hike.

Polymarket predictors put a 97% chance on the Fed raising interest rates this year, up from 75% at the start of the week. The CME FedWatch tool, meanwhile, sees an 88% chance of a 25 basis point hike, with 12% anticipating a 50 basis point rise.

On top of the CPI, Bitcoin's downward move comes following Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned from the company, after reports of multiple affairs including with a suspected Chinese spy and a senior company executive spread.

Huang's departure has sparked "severe volatility across tech stocks, further dampening market risk appetite," Sun told Decrypt. "This has prompted broader portfolio de-risking, amplifying the downward pressure on Bitcoin," he added.

For a crypto rebound to happen, Sun said that Bitcoin needs "long-term allocation capital" to flow back into crypto after ETFs, stablecoins, and digital asset treasury companies have taken a hit in recent weeks.

#4972. Nvidia Employees Push Board to Bring Back Jensen Huang: Report

Nvidia Employees Push Board to Bring Back Jensen Huang: Report

Some Nvidia employees are calling on the company's board to bring back Jensen Huang, days after he resigned as CEO following reports of extramarital relationships, according to internal correspondence reviewed by Wired.

An internal "suggestion box" post, submitted by a longtime engineer prior to Huang's resignation, urged the board to take employee sentiment into account before acting, Wired reported. It has since drawn more than 1,600 comments, several of which asked the board to find a way to reinstate Huang. "If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss," one anonymous commenter wrote.

The post also called on the board to share the findings of its investigation with staff and to formally seek their views on Huang continuing as CEO. One employee went further, sending a companywide email demanding transparency about Huang's exit and declaring: "I stand by Jensen."

In the hours following Huang's departure, some two dozen Nvidia employees posted supportive messages on LinkedIn, while Wired said it could not find any employee posts backing his departure. The engineer behind the suggestion box post declined to comment, and Nvidia and its new board chair, Harvey Jones, did not immediately respond to the outlet.

Huang resigned as Nvidia's president, CEO and a board member on Thursday, ending a 33-year tenure at the company he co-founded. Chief financial officer Colette Kress is serving as interim CEO, and Jones, a former CEO of Synopsys, became the board's first chair. Nvidia did not give a reason for the resignation.

Huang told staff on Sunday that he had been "unfaithful" to his wife, without naming anyone.

The New York Times had reported in October that surveillance of Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, indicated that she had developed a "close relationship" with the Nvidia CEO. Per the Times, U.S. officials concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset, while the Financial Times reported that a U.S. memo said the two had been romantically involved. China has denied that she is a spy.

Alice Huang was CEO and chair of Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer, and was investigated by Singapore police over possible false declarations relating to the company's server purchases, the country's Ministry of Home Affairs said on Thursday. She was not investigated for espionage, it said.

Police found insufficient evidence that she had committed any offenses, but concluded she had knowingly given them inaccurate information to downplay her role at Megaspeed, according to the ministry. She was issued a stern warning, left Singapore in February and was barred from returning. Investigations into Megaspeed and six other people are ongoing.

Alice Huang is now living in Beijing with the knowledge of security officials, Bloomberg reported, citing a person familiar with the matter. Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Thursday that the Justice Department had not yet concluded whether Huang was a spy, and that the FBI was investigating the reports, according to Reuters, which was unable to reach her for comment.

On Wednesday, the Times reported that Jensen Huang had also had a relationship with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of health care, who reported directly to him. Powell has not commented.

On Thursday, Reuters reported, citing two people familiar with the matter, that Huang had an intimate relationship with a second Nvidia employee, whom it did not name. Nvidia declined to comment to Reuters, and Huang and a representative for the board did not respond. Huang has not commented on the reports since his Sunday email, according to Wired.

The calls inside Nvidia echo criticism of the board's decision from tech and crypto figures. Binance co-founder Changpeng "CZ" Zhao called it a mistake for Nvidia to let Huang resign, while Elon Musk said hiring him would be "an honor."

Huang still faces scrutiny in Washington. Congressional committees have asked him to sit for a closed-door interview on December 1 and to testify at a Senate hearing on December 3, as lawmakers examine Nvidia's chip exports to China.

He and Nvidia also face a proposed shareholder class action over his alleged ties to Alice Huang.

Nvidia reports quarterly earnings on Tuesday, the first under Kress.
