#4971. 15 Best Doormats That Are Durable and Design-Forward (2026)

Displaying a welcoming doormats at your home’s threshold is a crucial opportunity to make a strong first impression on visitors. But it’s not just about wooing your guests upon arrival—they should also have some staying power against the elements and an onslaught of muddy boots this winter.

Thankfully, we found plenty of heavy-duty, high-quality doormats that are both handsome and durable. Many of these options feature natural fibers made from coir, which is mildew-resistant, absorbent, and can survive any kind of weather conditions and foot traffic it endures. Eco-friendly jute is another natural material common among doormat designs—favored for its organic look and its ability to trap dirt while still looking clean. Our top picks include doormats we’ve tested in our own homes as well as a few exciting options from brands we trust, so we don’t suggest (figuratively) dragging your feet on these great designs.

Ruggable’s entire door mat catalog is machine washable, which feels like a non-negotiable for an item literally made to trap dirt. Senior commerce editor Nashia Baker found a kindred match in this pick, telling us that “the balance of whimsical lines and shapes makes walking through the door a harmonious experience.” She also noted that it’s “incredibly durable,” and after months of high-traffic it “can handle any wet or muddy shoes that land on it without getting waterlogged.” Non-slip backing keeps it (and you) from sliding around.

Lean into a modern nautical vibe (think: this contemporary Long Island escape) with Serena & Lily’s chunky mat that looks like it was woven from stolen sailboat rope. The natural/coastal blue colorway offers just a hint of color, while monochromatic options that weave in multiple shades of blue and white make a more vibrant statement.

Sometimes all you need to make a statement is a simple pattern. This honeycomb design is subtle, mildew-resistant, shed-free, and fade-safe. A rubber backing means there won’t be any slipping and it’s designed to hold water—the raised border design helps contain up to 1.5 gallons of water per square yard.

Quilt-like lines feed into a larger geometric pattern in this doormat design that still feels simple but very modern. The grid of black rectangular lines reads as a neutral, and the elongated shape covers more area than some of its peers on this list, so we’d recommend it for an entry hallway or wider doorsteps.

This Heymat doormat is a durable option for covered porches and entryways. Not suitable for true outdoor use (on an uncovered patio or anywhere susceptible to the elements), this Heymat doormat is highly absorbent and soft underfoot. Shopping market editor Kate McGregor’s family owns a few mats from this brand, which they use as an oversized entryway mat in the mudroom for muddy boots and dog paws, as well as a bathmat in the guest bathroom. “This multi-colored mat has held up so well against summer mud and dropped birdseed, despite its pastel grid design,” she says. “Even after five years, the colors have stayed vibrant, and the mat never gets soggy.” Plus, whether sprayed down or shaken, she notes that the cleanup is very easy. “I might protest if my mom ever decides to replace it.”

A departure from the traditional rectangular doormat shape, this Toast alternative offers colorful texture in one compact oval shape that’s fully reversible. This one is crafted from woven and braided jute strands that are ideal for trapping loose dirt and debris. For those looking for an alternative to standard brown jute styles, these alternating patterns are pretty mesmerizing.

If you’ve got more floor space to fill in your greeting room, smaller sizes of The Citizenry’s jute rugs could be exactly what you’ve been looking for. At 24” x 36,” this hand braided mat has a refined look thanks to the contrasting border design, and it’ll be a nice surface to station wet or damp shoes so you’re not damaging the floor.

In our doormat quest, we also found this woven gem from Matilda Goad’s MG&Co. with looped fringing that looks like scalloped edges. The deep burgundy tone will help hide visible mud stains (though the jute weave can also be easily hosed down when it’s time for a clean). It’s also available in a longer, thinner silhouette to help cover sprawling patios.

Floral without feeling overly whimsical (no shame if that’s your vibe, though), Anthropologie’s sweet, embossed doormat is a simple option for anyone with an already-decorated entryway. With an abundance of five-star ratings, lots of reviews praise the design's versatile look and durable material makeup.

The raised, slatted design of Crate & Barrel’s unfinished teakwood doormat keeps moisture, grime, and dirt away sans-bristles. It’s also great for indoor use, perhaps as a minimalist bath mat. Be mindful that teak patinas over time due to weather and regular wear and tear, shifting from a golden wood to a grayish color.

The bold tulip motif on this doormat has a cheerful Scandi-folk style that can brighten your front entry even in the dead of winter. When it starts to look a little worse for wear, the mat easily lifts off its non-slip base for machine washing. In a spot that receives near-constant foot traffic, the ability to refresh it from season to season will be a godsend.

Danish brand HAY is known for their lively color combinations, and their doormats are no exception. With interlocking strands of navy and pink rope, bookended by charming red trim, this pick welcomes guests in with a jolt of maximalist energy. The vegetable fiber strands offer a strong and durable natural alternative to plastic: Shake it out and spray it down with water to clean it and keep the colors fresh.

A stylish coir braid gives this under-$30 doormat a higher-end look. The thick weave creates an almost padded texture, designed to expertly track dirt as your shoe treads catch on the material. With its lattice-like appearance, we envision this doormat at the front door of your Cotswolds-inspired farmhouse, or perhaps a coastal home that could use another natural texture to contrast the white and blue color palette.

Leave it to Sarah Sherman Samuel to create a great doormat. The AD100 designer, arguably most known for Mandy Moore’s Altadena home, offers a twist on the traditional rectangle. Uneven stripes with cutouts give your entryway a modern edge. The coir is dependable, and the black accents help to disguise the inevitable mess.

CB2’s doormat selection is predominantly composed of versatile Chilewich designs—like this monochromatic pattern that feels like a fashionable take on television static. Designed with functionality first, the shag mat uses antimicrobial yarn to keep itself tidy. It also has a no-slip backing to keep it fixed near your doorstep, even when your shoes need a serious wipe down.

What should you look for in the best outdoor doormats?

Although design is important, you also want to make sure that your doormat is low profile enough to fit under your door and also has a non-slip backing to keep your guests from busting it before they make it into your home. (Talk about a first impression!) Materials like PVC with rubber backing are key for the latter, while 100% coir doormats give your entryway a more natural look and are more ideal for trapping dirt and debris.

The answer is a tentative yes. You’re always going to want to see what the manufacturer recommends and it, of course, depends on where you live. Doormats are always made for outdoor use, but various climates will wear down the materials differently. Frequent rain and snow might soften jute while constant sunshine will fade most textiles. An easy solution, if you aren’t sure what your mat can withstand, is to drag your doormat into the mudroom or somewhere with a bit of shelter whenever there’s big weather on its way.

#4969. Bitcoin slides below $70K amid thin liquidity, ETF outflows

Bitcoin fell below $70,000 as crypto sentiment hit its lowest level in more than four months, with analysts warning of institutional outflows, thinning liquidity and elevated leverage.

Bitcoin fell below $70,000 on Friday, extending its recent decline as investor sentiment deteriorated amid institutional outflows, tightening liquidity and broader macroeconomic pressures.

Bitcoin (BTC) is trading at $68,607.23 at the time of writing, down 9.2% in the last week, according to CoinGecko.

The decline coincided with a sharp decline in market sentiment. The Crypto Fear & Greed Index fell to 20 on Friday, its lowest reading in more than four months, putting it in “extreme fear.” The index last reached that level on July 8.

Bitcoin’s seven-day price chart. Source: CoinGecko

Vikram Subburaj, CEO of crypto exchange Giottus, attributed Bitcoin’s weakness to institutional outflows, tightening crypto liquidity and expectations of a December Federal Reserve interest rate hike. “Investors should avoid chasing short-term dips or using excessive leverage,” he said.

In a Friday report, 10x Research highlighted Bitcoin’s divergence from US equities. Between Nov. 5 and Nov. 12, Bitcoin declined 5.9% while the Nasdaq 100 gained 6.4%, suggesting the cryptocurrency was responding more to rising Treasury yields and a stronger US dollar than to technology stocks.

The research firm said Bitcoin was increasingly trading alongside gold, which fell 5.5% over the same period, as the US 10-year Treasury yield climbed above 5%.

Meanwhile, institutional trading platform Talos also warned that thin liquidity and elevated leverage could amplify market volatility. Its weekly report showed Bitcoin order-book depth across major exchanges fell to $72 million on Nov. 9, roughly 33% below normal levels.

“Liquidity remains thin while leverage stays elevated, leaving the market vulnerable to sharp moves in either direction,” said Tanay Ved, senior research associate at Talos. “A rebuild in order-book depth and a turn in capital flows would provide a firmer foundation for recovery.”

The selloff follows sustained withdrawals from US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs). BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded more than $2 billion in net outflows across nine consecutive trading sessions through Wednesday, while broader funds shed nearly $1.44 billion over five sessions.

Preliminary Farside Investors data showed another $47.7 million in net outflows on Thursday, although figures for BlackRock and Fidelity were still pending.

#4966. 8 Best Hotel Mattress Picks for a Five-Star Staycation (2026)

The hotel mattress probably isn't on your vacation itinerary, but it can make or break the trip. A lumpy, unsupportive bed can derail even the most carefully planned getaway. A really good one, though, might become an unexpected highlight—the kind that has you looking forward to bedtime and quietly checking the mattress label before checkout.

Recreating that experience at home doesn't require another plane ticket. Some of our favorite properties, including design-forward waterfront retreats and charming bed-and-breakfasts, put just as much thought into their mattresses as they do their interiors. Many turn to brands like Saatva, Ostermoor, and Casper to outfit their guest rooms; others, like the W Hotels, have signature mattresses you can purchase directly. We've rounded up our favorites, such as options with plush pillow tops, supportive coils, and pressure-relieving foam, to help you bring home the best part of your stay. The room service, unfortunately, is still up to you.

For consistency, all the prices reflect queen-size mattresses.

A stay at the Ritz-Carlton is typically reflected by its refined, elegant bedrooms. The property’s eponymous bed invites the same feeling. At 14 inches, it’s a higher-profile mattress that can help make the bed the centerpiece of the room. One of the standout qualities is its coil system, as it includes 900-plus individual springs for great edge support to help keep the sides from sagging as well as promote motion isolation—a necessary factor for couples who don’t want to sense each other’s every move.

Serta is one of the most well-known mattress manufacturers for high-end hotels, such as the Waldorf-Astoria. While many luxury properties feature custom beds from the namesake brand, it doesn’t mean you can’t shop at popular retailers such as Amazon. You’d never guess it, but this premium bed is priced under $500 (making it the most affordable on our list). While Serta offers memory foam and hybrid versions of its beds, this pillow-top innerspring mattress has been a favorite in our shopping writer Julia Harrison’s space, and she says it lives up to its “Five-Star” name.

It’s filled with three zones of coil support that keep her free of back and neck problems, and the quilted cover gives her something soft to dream about each night. “I’m really not one for pomp and frills in my mattress—I’m a firm believer that a good mattress has very little going on besides a somewhat plush top to keep you from feeling any sort of spring mechanism going on inside,” she says in her mattress review. “I instantly slept well on this mattress I think because it’s quite simply: a great mattress.” Bonus: It’s a mattress-in-a-box, so it can fit easily through narrow doors and hallways once it’s delivered, and inflate to its full size in less than a day’s time after unboxing, according to Harrison. Just in case you’re on the fence, it comes with a 100-day sleep trial so you can really see if it’s right for you before committing.

The Six Bells Countryside Inn opened its doors in the bucolic Hudson Valley in 2025, and it’s already earned two Michelin Keys. In each of the 11 carefully and whimsically designed bedrooms at this retreat, you’ll find handcrafted mattresses and bedding from New York-born brand Ostermoor. Dating back to 1853, Ostermoor’s mattresses have a storied history. The beds have innovated to foam and pocketed coil inner layers and designer covers in recent years, and we can prove they’re worth the splurge.

“From the moment I plopped down on the mattress, I was amazed by how comfortable and supportive it was,” shares contributor Zoë Sessums. “It strikes a perfect balance between spring and cushion—just the right amount of give without ever feeling too soft. I consistently wake up feeling rested and supported.” Firm without feeling rigid, this mattress looks as good as it feels, too. “The tufted top, the blue and white ticking pattern, the hand-stitched details—it feels like a piece of luxury furniture, not just a place to sleep,” Sessums adds, later writing, “I would recommend this to someone who values design and craftsmanship as much as comfort.”

Saatva has found its way into charming bed-and-breakfasts and boutique retreats across the country, including Pasadena's historic Bissell House (an 1887 Victorian filled with antique furnishings) and the Cornell Inn in the Berkshires (known for its colorful, individually designed guest rooms). You can bring that same inviting comfort home with the Saatva Classic, which comes with a plush three-inch Euro pillow top, a responsive dual-coil system, and targeted lumbar support. “It’s firm (which I prefer), regulates temperature well, and is extremely durable,” our tester Erin Kaplan, vice president of communications at Conde Nast, says. “I alternate between sleeping on my side and sleeping on my stomach, and this mattress is great for both positions.”

Choose from three firmness levels—plush soft, luxury firm, or firm—, eight different sizes, and two mattress heights to find your ideal fit. Feeling extra? Saatva also offers two adjustable bases that elevate your head and feet at the touch of a button, including a premium Plus model for an even more indulgent breakfast in bed. A 365-night trial gives you plenty of time to sleep on your decision before committing.

At 1 Hotel Brooklyn Bridge, even the beds have a view. Sweeping views of the Manhattan skyline set the scene, with reclaimed wood floors, rich leather headboards, and locally crafted details like crate-inspired closets and Fresnel glass light fixtures bringing warmth and character to the rooms. With custom Keetsa mattresses in every room, you might find yourself hitting snooze despite the spectacular view.

Back at home, the Keetsa Pillow Plus offers its own take on hotel-worthy comfort. It's something of a Goldilocks mattress: not too soft, not too firm, but just right. A three-inch pillow top combines body-contouring memory foam with responsive comfort foam to help cushion pressure points, and a soft, moisture-wicking cover designed to keep overheating at bay. Individually wrapped coils provide cushioning and support while minimizing motion transfer, so even if your bedmate tosses and turns, you can stay blissfully unaware.

When husband-and-wife duo Ely and Danielle Franko transformed a dated Catskills ski hostel into The Henson, a 16-room boutique hotel, they had a pretty good idea of what makes a great night's sleep. The couple painstakingly restored the property themselves, filling its rooms with vintage furniture, locally sourced finds, and hand-applied wallpaper. But when it came to mattresses, they didn't have to look far for inspiration: They chose the same Casper Dream Hybrid they sleep on at home. In fact, they loved it so much that they insisted on king-size mattresses in every room, even the smallest ones. The hybrid design combines responsive springs with breathable foam and three zones of support that help keep your spine aligned. Its medium feel is meant to offer a little give without leaving you sinking into the mattress, making it a crowd-pleasing choice for your own guest room. After all, the best hosts know a good night's sleep is part of the hospitality.

Hot sleepers, here’s a mattress for you. The Four Season’s signature mattress features a couple of core cooling bed qualities: proprietary cooling gel foam layers with channeled designs that promote airflow throughout the mattress, as well as a patented DualCool top layer that draws heat and moisture away from the body and keeps the bed fresh with its antimicrobial properties. The cooling mattress is designed for sleepers who need extra structure on their bed. This posh number has targeted support for the lower back area and zeroes in on alignment regardless of your sleep style.

The W Hotels are widely popular because of the bold design across the properties worldwide. To mirror this aesthetic, you can cover the brand’s mattress with colorful bedding to let your personal style shine. When it comes to the bed itself, it is defined by a plush top quilted damask design to ease side sleepers into a peaceful slumber. It’s made for people who need extra support too, though, including an individually-wrapped coil system, five-zone support system, and comfort layers for added cushioning and pressure relief.

Which Kind of Mattress Do Five-Star Hotels Use?

Most luxury hotels use hybrid mattresses in their suites to strike the right balance of softness and support, thanks to pocketed coils and layers of foam. These are also typically medium-firm mattresses since a plush mattress can be uncomfortable for some sleep positions like stomach sleepers. Ultra-firm beds are often too stiff for side sleeping and may not align with guests' expectations who are looking for soft mattresses to sink into on their vacations.

If you want to refresh your mattress without the hefty price tag of buying a new one, a mattress topper or featherbed could be a solid transitional pick. The Ritz-Carlton and W Hotels promote one made with white duck feathers, Mandarin Oriental’s exclusive topper comprises white goose feathers, Waldorf Astoria beds feature a down-alternative fill, and Marriott’s selection is polyester.

There’s no one-size-fits-all here, so it really comes down to your preferred loftiness and weight. Duck and goose feathers are heavier than others fills, though duck is slightly less fluffy and won’t trap heat as well. Down-alternatives, like polyester, are more allergen-friendly and “a good option for those who prefer to be vegan in their home,” Danielle Trotter, the former textiles technical designer at West Elm, previously shared.

What Is the Thickness of a Hotel Mattress?

Hotel mattresses usually average 12 inches in thickness. This makes it easier to hop in and out of bed, especially when using a box spring that can add extra height. Plus, it prevents the need for deep pocket sheets.

How Can I Recreate the Hotel Bed Experience at Home?

We’ve explored the entire topic on how to make your bedroom feel like a hotel, and aside from finding a new bed with a medium-firm feel, we’ve learned that percale sheets and down pillows are pretty much a must to complete the setup. When it comes to the former, hotel sheets are usually made with organic cotton percale because the fabric looks and feels crisp, giving that refined aesthetic a boutique stay is known for. Usually a 200 thread count is a good rule of thumb to look out for since this means there’s plenty of airflow between the threads for a breathable feel. Hotel pillows are usually filled with down feathers since they’re so lofty and soft, but a down alternative is just as good in case you’re an allergy-prone sleeper (just look for materials like hypoallergenic gel fibers since they’re known for being squishy without falling flat, just like a feather-filled pillow).

What Is the Difference Between Memory Foam and Latex?

Traditional memory foam has a body-contouring effect that allows your body to sink into your mattress, reducing motion transfer (a must for bedmates!). Meanwhile, latex foam offers more responsiveness (or bounce) and overall support. As Neslihan Ozdemir, founder of Girl on the Mattress, previously told AD, latex also holds up well over time and is temperature-regulating. Latex foam mattresses (and some memory foam options) are certified organic, making them eco-friendly foam picks, too.

Known as the most common mattress type, innerspring mattresses feature a bed of coils. There’s quite a range to these mattresses: Basic versions have a grid of steel coils, while premier options have a pillow top and pocketed coil technology, meaning each coil is individually wrapped to help with motion isolation. Ozdemir added that innerspring is best for people who sleep on the edge of the bed or are heavier in weight. If you prefer a more plush mattress, but still want the support of those coils, consider a hybrid mattress, which is layered with soft foam.

#4965. Microsoft 365 outage hits Outlook email and Teams calendars worldwide

Microsoft began a wider patch rollout Friday as the outage disrupted government email and Microsoft 365 connectors in ChatGPT and Claude.

A global Microsoft 365 outage continued into Friday, disrupting Outlook email and some Teams features.

Microsoft said in a 7:25 am UTC update that a patch had improved stability on some servers and that it was working to deploy the fix more widely. The update, republished by the University of Pennsylvania, gave no timetable for restoration.

The outage began Thursday, leaving some users unable to access their Exchange Online mailboxes. Microsoft said a process that handles secure connection became overloaded, causing affected servers to stop processing requests.

The company said it attempted to restore services by restarting infrastructure, redirecting network traffic and expanding server capacity. Microsoft said there was no indication of a security breach.

The outage also affected features in Microsoft Copilot and Defender for Office 365. In Teams, users had trouble scheduling meetings and sending invitations. Microsoft said most Teams calls and chats continued to work.

Businesses and government agencies worldwide use Microsoft 365 for email and scheduling. Microsoft reported more than 450 million paid commercial seats for the suite in an October filing. Exchange Online hosts the mailboxes and calendars those organizations use, and outside apps, including AI assistants, rely on the same service to retrieve that data when users connect their work accounts.

OpenAI reported errors in ChatGPT’s Microsoft 365 connector. Anthropic said the outage caused most email, calendar and search requests through Claude’s Microsoft 365 connector to fail.

The US Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit said its clerk’s office could neither send nor receive email for much of Thursday. Japan’s Finance Ministry said it was unable to receive email on Friday and Singapore’s Government Technology Agency told CNA that government email was among systems affected.

#4963. ‘Roadmap is Complete,' Cardano Founder Charles Hoskinson Says

‘Roadmap is Complete,' Cardano Founder Charles Hoskinson Says

Charles Hoskinson said that the Cardano "roadmap is complete" and that everything he said would be built has finished. It came during a YouTube livestream Saturday where Hoskinson sparred with disgruntled investors as ADA sits down 94% from its 2021 high, per Decrypt data.

One viewer, named shevilkid1738, pushed Hoskinson's buttons by claiming he'd promised Cardano would overtake Bitcoin. The Cardano founder clapped back, saying there was no evidence he ever said that.

"I never promised that. I said I'd love for it to happen, and I believe that Cardano is a better store of value than Bitcoin is for a variety of reasons," Hoskinson said. "I don't promise price appreciation, or people overturning things. I say it's absolutely possible."

Shevilkid1738 responded over an hour later with a quote where Hoskinson said he "thinks" Cardano is "probably" going to become the biggest cryptocurrency in the world. Hoskinson highlighted the word think and said that he has "taken it as far as I can," explaining that the network is now decentralized and doesn't rely on him as a founder.

The viewer wasn't happy and called it "insane" for a founder to no longer have a responsibility to those who invested in their project.

"Under what conditions does that obligation end?" Hoskinson said, "The roadmap is complete. Everything I said is going to be built has been built. It reached four times the value of Honda. There's millions of people that have used it, and millions of people continue to use it. It is self-determining and self-governing, with its ability to pick and choose its own leaders without my consent."

Cardano’s roadmap was split into five eras: Byron, Shelly, Goguen, Basho, and Voltaire.

Byron launched the network in September 2017, Shelley added staking in July 2020, and Goguen brought smart contracts via the Alonzo hard fork in September 2021. Basho, the scaling era, came via the Vasil upgrade in September 2022, before the Chang hard fork kicked off Voltaire, the governance era, in September 2024.

The final piece to the puzzle occurred in January 2025, when the Plomin hard fork gave ADA holders "real voting power" over the blockchain’s future. This marked the transition to "full decentralized governance," and apparently the end of the final roadmap era.

"The goal was simple. There's a roadmap. Byron, Basho, Shelley, et cetera, et cetera, and it's done," Hoskinson said. "I have no power over this ecosystem."

Ultimately, though, Hoskinson says the issue that investors have is financial.

"You want me to pump your bags. That's what you're really saying. The job's not done till it's pumped," he said. "But guess what? It's never done then, because somebody comes in at $3 ADA, and then expects it to be five. And then somebody comes in at $5 ADA and expects it to be seven. If you retire at five, you've defrauded the person at five. If you retire at three, you've defrauded the person at three. There's no winning."

ADA hit an all-time high of $3.09 in 2021 and has been on a downward slide ever since. By 2023, it hit a low of $0.25 before pumping 384% to a high of $1.21 by the end of 2024. Since then, its price has tanked 86% to $0.17.

"Apparently, I have to go pump your bags, and if I don't, I'm a scammer," Hoskinson said. "Would you sign up for that deal? Would any human being sign up for that? I thought slavery was made illegal."

#4960. Crypto Laundering a 'Material Risk' for UK Ahead of FATF Review: RUSI

Crypto Laundering a 'Material Risk' for UK Ahead of FATF Review: RUSI

Britain's new crypto rules will still be in transition when global anti-money laundering assessors arrive next year, and the country will be judged on whether it can enforce against crypto laundering, a taskforce convened by the Royal United Services Institute has warned.

The report, published on Friday, sets out the issues the London-based defense and security think tank's taskforce expects to dominate the UK's 2027 evaluation by the Financial Action Task Force (FATF). The Paris-based body sets global standards against money laundering and terrorist financing, and its peer reviews, known as mutual evaluations, grade countries on whether their rules work in practice.

The UK's report is due to be agreed at FATF's February 2028 plenary. The taskforce, made up of experts from the private sector, law enforcement and civil society, met in September; its discussions are not attributed to individual members.

This will be the first UK evaluation in which FATF considers crypto, and the taskforce was clear that crypto is a "material risk" for the UK. The City of London Police assesses that 66% of investment fraud in the UK involves crypto, according to the report, while the UK's 2025 National Risk Assessment rated crypto money laundering high risk, up from medium in 2020.

The UK brought crypto firms under its money laundering rules in 2020, and legislation passed in February created a statutory crypto regime. The Financial Conduct Authority set out which crypto activities it will regulate in April and finalized its rulebook in June, but the mandatory regime does not take effect until October 25, 2027.

The taskforce doubted the UK would be able to show adequate implementation by the time FATF visits in June, and said the UK had moved more conservatively than Hong Kong, the EU and Singapore.

Members were unanimous that assessors would examine this, and pointed to over-the-counter brokers, which the National Crime Agency estimated in the 2025 risk assessment are likely to launder hundreds of millions of dollars a year.

The taskforce cited the Metropolitan Police's £5.5 billion (about $7.3 billion) crypto seizure, linked to a Chinese professional money launderer sentenced in 2025, as an encouraging sign of what the UK criminal justice system can do when it has the capacity. But one member said the success had not been repeated in enough cases, and that the UK had barely scratched the surface of crypto laundering. FATF is expected to test whether results match the risk rather than rest on one headline case, the report said.

It also warned that because the UK's regime, which resembles the EU's Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation, focuses on identifiable crypto firms, it risks pushing the highest-risk crypto outside the FCA's perimeter. That would leave law enforcement to trace assets the regulator cannot reach, such as Tether's USDT, with the taskforce pointing to a July 2025 Treasury report that flagged the stablecoin as having been "extensively exploited" by Russian-speaking laundering networks. Members doubted law enforcement had been given the capability and resources to keep pace.

The taskforce did praise UK international cooperation, including Operation Destabilise, which targeted Russian-speaking networks converting street cash into crypto. But it flagged resourcing of the UK Financial Intelligence Unit and law enforcement's economic crime capacity as likely vulnerabilities.

FATF has been pressing countries on crypto enforcement more broadly. In July, it said nearly 93% of jurisdictions it surveyed had yet to apply its standards to qualifying DeFiplatforms.

#4958. Jane Street boosts Fidelity Bitcoin ETF holdings nearly 8x, trims IBIT

Jane Street ended Q3 with 19% fewer shares in BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF, and a higher share price lifted the stake’s value to $957 million.

Jane Street’s share count in Fidelity’s spot Bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) rose nearly eightfold in the third quarter, while its holdings in BlackRock’s fund fell 19%.

The trading giant ended September with 7.01 million shares of Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC0, up from about 887,000 at the end of June. The position was worth $509 million on Sept. 30, according to its second-quarter and third-quarter filings.

Jane Street’s holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT) fell to 20.22 million shares from 24.88 million. A higher share price lifted the reported value of its stake to $957 million from $828 million.

Jane Street was the largest reported institutional holder of BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF at the end of June after boosting its share count by 324% in Q2, according to 13F Pro. IBIT is the world’s largest spot Bitcoin ETF with $56 billion in net assets as of Thursday.

The firm also added nearly 9 million shares (about $249 million) of the ARK 21Shares Bitcoin ETF (ARKB), while more than doubling its holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Ethereum Trust ETF (ETHA) by 7.83 million shares (about $158 million).

JPMorgan Chase increased its IBIT holdings by 9.6% in the third quarter, adding about 1 million shares to reach 11.41 million. The US banking giant’s reported IBIT position rose to around $540 million.

In contrast, JPMorgan nearly erased its direct holdings in BlackRock’s ETHA, reducing its position from 1.17 million shares at the end of June to just 399 shares.

#4957. Joseph Dirand Joins Forces with Peter Brant and Stephanie Seymour Brant to Reimagine a Historic 1920s Palm Beach Mansion

It began with a chance encounter and a phone call. The French architect Joseph Dirand was walking into a restaurant in New York when he ran into an old friend, the art dealer Tico Mugrabi. Dirand mentioned that he was flying to Palm Beach the next day and his friend lit up. Mugrabi pulled out his phone and made a call. “Peter, my friend Joseph is coming to Palm Beach tomorrow,” Dirand recalls him saying. “You have to meet this guy.” The following day Dirand was sitting across from Peter Brant, the fabled industrialist and publisher, and his wife, Stephanie Seymour Brant, one of the defining supermodels of her generation, in their new home. The Brants had owned the house for only a few days, and it marked the opening of a new chapter for the couple.

The Brants in front of Warhol’s White Disaster (White Car Crash 19 Times), 1963, in the great room. Jean Prouvé coffee table; sofa and chairs by Pierre Jeanneret.

Marc Newson’s aluminum Lockheed Lounge, a Tower Cabinet by Ettore Sottsass, and a Trapeze table by Jean Prouvé all stand in the loggia.

A Végétale mirror by Claude Lalanne reflects Untitled (Richard Prince and Cindy Sherman), 1980, and a 1930s settee by Jean-Michel Frank.

A pair of bespoke Philippe Anthonioz floor lamps Flank Jonathan Horowitz’s Self-portrait in “Mirror #1” (Kevin), 2012.

Brown Jordan chaise longues under umbrellas by Business & Pleasure Co. stand poolside.

There is something quintessentially American in how quickly it all unfolded, and for Dirand, it meant meeting a man he had long admired. As an inquisitive young designer he had pored over photographs of Brant’s earlier homes, and one image had stayed with him: It showed a Jean Prouvé desk beneath a Jean-Michel Basquiat painting, captured at a time when almost no one thought to set French postwar design against contemporary art. “For me, Peter was a kind of collector’s hero,” says Dirand.

Brant’s eye for art and design was formed early. His father loved classical art and considered even Picasso “way too modern,” but on family ski trips to Switzerland the teenage Brant got to know the Zurich dealer Bruno Bischofberger, who subsequently introduced him to Leo Castelli, and the two titans of the art world became the young Brant’s mentors. Brant then bought his first Andy Warhol painting in 1966, and by the time Warhol’s business manager, Fred Hughes, introduced them in November 1968, he already owned about seven of them. Warhol and Brant would go on to become close friends for the remainder of the artist’s life.

In a corner of the Great Room, Roy Lichtenstein’s The Refrigerator, 1962, faces a 1960 copper hanging cabinet by Paul Evans. a 1934 plaster vase by Alberto Giacometti sits on A Joseph Dirand Edition Iseo Gold coffee table. 1950s Hirondelles wall light by Jean Royère; pair of 1958 Diego Giacometti armchairs.

Warhol’s 1975 portrait of Murray Brant (Brant’s father) commands a wall of the paneled study. Direction armchair and Présidence desk by Jean Prouvé; Ettore Sottsass hanging lamp.

Artworks in the entrance hall include, from left, a Massangis stone lion-head fountain feature by Harumi Klossowska de Rola, 2022; Andy Warhol’s Self Portrait (Green), 1964; a Klossowska de Rola wolf sculpture; and Richard Prince’s Nurses’ Dormitory, 2002. The sanded Ceppo di Gré stone modular console is by Dirand.

In the primary bedroom, George Condo’s 2004 portrait of Stephanie Seymour Brant hangs above a 1946 Charlotte Perriand sideboard on which stands an enameled earthenware lamp by Roger Capron. A Jean Prouvé Bridge armchair joins an Ours Polaire chair and sofa by Jean Royère around Prouvé’s Tripod coffee table.

An exterior view of the 1926 Addison Mizner residence

Few people then saw Warhol’s work the way Brant did. When he hung his first Marilyn at home, visitors were shocked: “People would come to my house and think, This is the worst thing I’ve ever seen. In the 1960s, he says, you could count the serious collectors of Pop art “on three hands.”

His rule, he says, is to collect “something that was in style that’s out of style,” though never as speculation. Between their holdings and those of the Brant Foundation, which he established in 1996 to promote the education and appreciation of contemporary art and design, the collection now numbers more than 3,000 works, and he has never bought for a particular wall. What he misses in many newer collections is any sense of the person behind them. “You can see right away that this guy had a great adviser,” he says, “but there’s no madness in the collection. Every collection should have some madness.”

Carlo Mollino’s Tipo B side chair rests beneath Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Per Capita, 1981.

A painting by Glenn Ligon and a stool by Shiro Kuramata in Brant’s bath

Seymour Brant and Brant in the great room

Brant has shared much of his collecting journey with Seymour Brant, whom he married in 1995. She has built a collection of her own: Her passion is clothing rather than paintings, and she has pursued it with the same scholarship and the same sharp eye as her husband. She was a close friend of the legendary couturier Azzedine Alaïa and has one of the most significant holdings of his work, alongside historic couture that Dirand describes as extraordinary. Where Brant is the storyteller, Seymour Brant is the quieter presence, though Dirand is clear that she was integral to every decision. Her collecting has even changed the way Brant sees his own. “It’s not a dress in a closet,” he says. “It’s a piece of art, and it mixes with art.”

Brant has spent some 50 years in the Palm Beach area, first as a polo player. He and Seymour Brant had no need for a new home there—they loved the one they had as their children grew up. But when Collado Hueco, a home by Addison Mizner, the architect who defined 1920s Palm Beach, came on the market in 2020, the collector in him took over. “It was a piece of history,” Dirand says. “For him, there was no choice.” Designed in 1924 for the American businessman Paul Moore, it is one of Mizner’s boldest and most unusual houses.

In a hall, a 1974 Diego Giacometti bench sits in front of Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Untitled (Sugar Ray Robinson), 1982. The curtain rods are by Julian Schnabel.

Warhol’s The Kiss (Bela Lugosi), 1964, and a 1947 Alexandre Noll mahogany armchair in a corner of the primary bedroom. Philippe Anthonioz hanging light; Jean Prouvé tripod coffee table; Jean Royère Ours Polaire sofa.

John Currin’s Constance Towers, 2009, presides over Seymour Brant’s dressing room. Furniture includes an Ambassador chair and Éléphanteau sofa, both by Jean Royère.

In the great room, a plaster bas-relief, Support, and a carved-stone console by Alberto Giacometti are flanked by a pair of Éléphanteau chairs by Jean Royère. In the foreground is a Fondation Maeght chair by Diego Giacometti.

The house presented Dirand with a real puzzle. Asymmetrical, it spreads across multiple wings, varying from one to three levels. The main entertaining rooms had retained their original character, while the entrance and stair hall were dark and enclosed. Dirand set out to let the sunlight that moves across the house reach every part of the interior as much as possible. The collection shaped Dirand’s approach too. With so many treasured works coming into the house, the team wanted to ensure they would be protected against anything the unpredictable Florida weather could bring. What began as a renovation became, in Dirand’s words, “a reconstruction saving the historical.” Rather than demolishing and starting again, the team kept the historic walls standing, reinforced every one of them, and dug beneath to take the foundations deeper. New hurricane-proof windows were set into concrete frames, the roof was removed and rebuilt, and the original tiles and the patina of the plaster fireplace were carefully preserved. The project took about four years. Throughout, Dirand immersed himself in Mizner’s doors, ceilings, and patterns so that the new rooms would be a true homage rather than a copy.

Early on, Brant handed Dirand two thick binders, one of artworks and one of design pieces. “This is what you can use,” he said. A few days later, on holiday with his family, Dirand spread every page across the floors. “I was walking on the paper,” he says. It reversed the usual order in which the architect designs the rooms and the art arrives afterward. Here the collection came first, though never rigidly, because “a collection is also something that is alive.”

A Diego Giacometti bench and A Jean Royère Liane six-branch floor lamp in a hall

A chair and floor lamp by Diego Giacometti stand next to the fireplace in the great room.

Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Per Capita, 1981, commands a wall of the loggia. Ours Polaire chair and Yo-Yo side table, both by Royère.

A family photo and fragrance bottles line up on a windowsill in Seymour Brant’s bath.

Warhol’s Big Campbell’s Soup Can with Can Opener (Vegetable), 1962, hangs in the stairwell, which also features a Jacques Adnet wall light.

At the heart of the home are two main spaces, the great room and the loggia, which are perhaps the spaces that both Dirand and Brant are most proud of. The great room is centered around a monumental fireplace that has the grandeur of a castle. Commanding one wall is a rare carved-stone console by Alberto Giacometti, nearly three meters high and crowned with a bas-relief of a female figure, made for Jean-Michel Frank’s important 1938 commission for the Born family in Argentina. It was one of a pair, and Dirand says no one knows where the other is today. Beside the fireplace hangs Warhol’s 1963 White Disaster (White Car Crash 19 Times), one of Brant’s most recent and important acquisitions; he had wanted a Car Crash painting since the ’60s. Prouvé furniture from Brazzaville, weathered by the Congo climate, sits amid Native American and African objects, and at the center of it all is a mirror polish copper table Dirand designed himself to reflect Giacometti’s bird vase and the fireplace behind it. Brant calls the table a masterpiece.

Art in the main entrance includes, from left, pieces by Jean-Michel Basquiat, Richard Prince and Cindy Sherman, and Brice Marden. Furniture includes an Ours Polaire Chair and sofa and a kidney-shaped coffee table, all by Jean Royère, and a Charlotte Perriand stool. a 1936 Jean-Michel Frank settee stands in the background.

A glimpse into the primary bedroom, where a Jean-Michel Frank table lamp sits atop a bedside table.

A wall niche holds a 2023 ibis sculpture by Harumi Klossowska de Rola

The loggia is lighter and more playful, and its original black, yellow, and turquoise tiled floor and Egyptian-style column capitals offer a glimpse of Mizner at his most adventurous. One of the most prominent works hanging in the room is a monumental 1981 Basquiat painting, Per Capita, which Brant bought from Annina Nosei’s memorial show for the artist. At the center of the room stands an Ettore Sottsass Tower Cabinet, designed in the early 1960s, which Dirand admits he didn’t understand at first, until Brant showed him exactly how to place it. Now he can’t imagine the room without it.

Upstairs, Dirand took down walls so that the primary bedroom runs the full width of the house, capturing light from sunrise over the ocean to sunset over the garden. In Seymour Brant’s bathroom, Estremoz marble walls are carved to evoke curtains, and beyond it lies her dressing room, an intimate boudoir. Brant’s bathroom, by contrast, is a marquetry of white and gray marble built around a pair of Glenn Ligon paintings. Dirand calls the room “minimalist ornamental.”

A 2023 flamingo sculpture by Harumi Klossowska de Rola (one of a pair) stands on the terrace.

In Seymour Brant’s bath, François-Xavier Lalanne’s Singe II, 1999, sits between a stool by Diego Giacometti and a bespoke bench by Philippe Anthonioz.

Every piece in the house comes with a story, and Dirand heard many of them over years of breakfasts, lunches, and dinners with the couple. “Every single piece could be a book,” he says. During those conversations, the collaboration between Brant and Dirand grew into a close friendship. Brant’s other great passion is horse racing, and his formidable stable has included Sottsass, the 2020 winner of the Prix de l’Arc de Triomphe, Europe’s most prestigious race. One day, Brant called Dirand with news: Sottsass had a son, a young thoroughbred already winning races of his own. He had named him Dirand.

#4954. DOJ moves to seize $1.2B from Binance hack, disrupts FaceStealer scam

Authorities froze over $2.7 billion in Binance hack proceeds, while the separate FaceStealer scam stole over $400 million in 18 months.

The US Department of Justice said Thursday it had obtained court authorization to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion of the $6.3 billion in cryptocurrency stolen from Binance.

The authorization covers part of the more than $2.7 billion in stolen assets that US authorities and private-sector partners have frozen since the Oct. 29 hack. Investigators are still working to recover additional funds, the department said in its announcement.

The FBI attributed the Binance hack to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group in a cyber alert. Kim Yo Jong, the sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, denied North Korea’s involvement.

Binance said it had covered the affected assets with its own funds and that a new proof-of-reserves snapshot showed at least one-to-one backing for customer assets, according to its Thursday post on X.

In November 2025, the DOJ announced a separate enforcement action targeting about $15 million in cryptocurrency linked to North Korean hackers known as APT38.

Related: Crypto hack losses equal 57 cents per $1 earned onchain: Ark’s Valente

In a separate case, US authorities seized cloud infrastructure used in FaceStealer, a North Korean social engineering campaign that stole more than $400 million in cryptocurrency over the past 18 months, the DOJ said.

The hackers used secretly recorded footage of previous victims and AI-generated deepfakes of prominent crypto figures to gain targets’ trust on video calls. They used bogus technical fixes to trick targets into installing malware, the DOJ said.

The campaign targeted people working in crypto and Web3. The hackers also increasingly targeted open-source software developers and maintainers in attempts to gain access to their devices and software repository accounts.

The FBI seized more than 900 webcam videos of FaceStealer victims under a federal warrant. The bureau also published guidance on how to protect against the scheme and what to do after a compromise in a public advisory.

Redacted stills from webcam videos recorded by FaceStealer hackers. Source: FBI

The DOJ said the hackers behind FaceStealer are known publicly by several names, including Sapphire Sleet, UNC1069, Dangerous Password, CryptoCore, APT38, BlueNoroff and Stardust Chollima.

#4952. Bitcoin returns to 200-week trend line as analyst sees risk of $57.7K next

Bitcoin price action interacted with its 200-week simple moving average for the first time since mid-August as it fell below $68,000.

Bitcoin (BTC) fell to $67,500 on Friday, slipping below its 200-week simple moving average on its first test of the level in nearly three months.

TradingView data showed that Bitcoin last touched its 200-week simple moving average (SMA) on Aug. 19.

BTC/USD one-day chart with the 200-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The 200-week SMA has served as a reference point for Bitcoin’s cycle lows, acting as support or resistance during bear markets. Bitcoin traded around the average from March to October 2023 and again from June to mid-August this year. Decisive moves back above it on the weekly chart have preceded sharper rallies.

In his latest analysis, Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, flagged the 200-week SMA as having confluence with a resistance/support flip level on Bitcoin’s relative strength index (RSI).

Weekly RSI has returned to 41.5, a level that it established as support in late July after it spent several weeks attempting a breakout.

“Losing 41.5 on Weekly RSI opens the door to test the cycle low at $57.7k,” Alan wrote Friday on X, noting the bullish implications that successfully defending the support zone would have.

BTC/USD one-week chart with 200 SMA; RSI data. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

BTC price downside accelerated on Friday as Asia markets reacted to the resignation and ongoing controversy around Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. Hong Kong’s Hang Seng finished the day down 1.55%, while South Korea’s KOSPI Composite Index ended 0.98% lower.

Related: Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO, exits board; shares plunge

US stock-market futures fell slightly, with the Nasdaq 100 falling 0.2% as analysis eyed a redistribution of capital into Nvidia’s competitors, preserving the AI trade.

Nasdaq 100 futures one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

“What you’re seeing in markets now is a bit of a rotation into other advanced chip design companies, on the assumption that this will enable other companies effectively to catch up,” said Mark Franklin, head of multi-asset solutions for Asia at asset manager Manulife Investment Management, told CNBC.

Nvidia stock was 0.7% higher pre-market, meanwhile, amid reports of growing support for Huang’s return. An internal post quoted by Wired drew more than 1,600 comments from employees.

Nvidia futures one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#4951. How One Creative Couple Found Harmony in Their 1920s Los Angeles Craftsman

Nothing about where interior designer Harrison Soll is now was part of the plan. Five years ago, he was focused on his work, and he didn’t want any distractions. But when he met Rodney Burns, a stylist and owner of the legendary Los Angeles vintage gallery, Church Boutique, it set everything on a new path. They met in the fall; by New Year’s they were a couple. “I didn’t think it was going to be a relationship,” Soll says. He credits their shared interests for bringing them together: “We always found a throughline with design.”

They now live in a 1925 craftsman house in Laurel Canyon. Soll took the lead decorating the nearly 1,400-square-foot home in a combination of his and Burns’s styles with many pieces coming from Burns’s eclectic collection of vintage art, furniture, and sculpture. Burns has worked with Lenny Kravitz as a stylist and creative collaborator for the past 15 years, and the singer says he admires what Burns and Soll have created together. “The mix of Rodney’s early- to midcentury style mixed with ethnic expression next to Harrison’s bold minimalist modern approach seamlessly blend together,” Kravitz tells AD.

The backyard of Soll’s and Burns’s Laurel Canyon home is a bricolage of funky, inherited stonework and vintage pottery.

The nail art above the fireplace has a cute backstory story: Burns and Soll had seen it at a vintage shop in Santa Monica and Burns regretted not buying it. When he went back to purchase the piece, it was marked as sold. But only because Soll had already covertly bought it as a Valentine’s Day gift for Burns.

The giant Sergio Bustamante giraffe bust in the foyer is a delicate topic. “The giraffe was one of our biggest fights in decorating this house,” Burns explains. “I was determined to have that giraffe live some place in this house because he’s just fantastic, and the scale of him, and the rarity of him.” Except, counters Soll, that the placement of the giraffe is treacherous because the front door swings right in its path. “You just have to be careful,” Soll says.

The foyer—which features a collection of masks and vintage paintings—was one of the most difficult areas to style, explains Burns. The couple was trying to be selective of what pieces to incorporate from Burns’s massive collection and at one point Burns suggested putting all of his masks in storage to save for another house down the line, or passing them onto Burns’s vintage boutique, Church. Soll dissented, saying, “No, let’s put them everywhere.”

The living room features two sofas—a Joseph D’Urso for Knoll piece reupholstered in leopard fabric and a leather De Sede Terrazza. The carpet is the Antilocarpa style from Stark. A trio of fiberglass shell art was sourced through Kelly Wearstler. The art behind the sofa is a manipulated canvas garment bag by the artist Roosevelt “Rip” Woods, the first black artist to have a solo show at the Phoenix Art Museum.

As renters, there were naturally limitations on what could be achieved. Soll got permission to retile a bathroom and install a new vanity. The doe-patterned wall-to-wall carpeting in the living room was an “ask for forgiveness” move, but his landladies ended up loving it. Burns, on the other hand, needed more convincing. “The carpet took many dinners of conversations between the two of us,” Soll says.

Soll describes himself as more of a minimalist while Burns avows, “I’m a maximalist to the umpteenth power.” The couple attempted to strike a balance while still filling the space with character. Some rooms nod to Soll’s leanings toward minimalism like an office with a midcentury desk and a 1970s tapestry and the dining room where a Sergio Bustamante table and a Chinese Art Deco rug are the main attractions.

When decorating their Laurel Canyon home, Soll and Burns had to figure out how to combine their opposing styles, with Soll being more of a minimalist and Burns being a maximalist. But, says Burns, “[Soll] has made me be able to take a pause and then also make me a little less attached to some of the things that I’ve collected  and given me an opportunity to maybe show a little bit more restraint.” In the corner, a brass Maison Jansen palm tree mingles with a Sergio Bustamante monkey.

Burns, who has been styling Lenny Kravitz for over a decade, says his approach to home design and fashion are one in the same: “Building an outfit, building a room: same thing in my opinion.”

Kravitz offers these kind words on Burns: “What I love about Rodney is that he is limitless in his concepts and always thinks outside of the box. His visual vocabulary enables him to mix a vast stew of eras and genres. He pays tribute to our ancestors and uplifts new innovators. Always soulful, expressive, and elegant.”

In the dining room, Soll placed a Sergio Bustamante table on a diagonal to make enough room for the couple’s large dog. “She’s a girthy girl,” says Soll, referring to the table, not the dog. The chairs are by Paul Evans and the Chinese Art Deco rug was sourced from Ariana Rugs. The Tom Greene chandelier was sourced from Burns’s boutique, Church.

Burns’s maximalism is on display in the living room, where a couch upholstered in leopard Scalamandré mixes with zebra-hide chairs and a sinuous De Sede sofa. (That animal print couch, Soll wants you to know, also features a block of leather upholstery: “I just think it would have been too maximalist to do a full Scalamandré sofa.”)

The living room is filled with brass sculptures (a Maison Jansen palm tree and a Sergio Bustamante monkey), bronze figures sourced from an estate sale in Arizona, and several pieces of sculptural artwork.

The bedroom mixes patterns and textures with a stainless steel bed from TTMM, Paul Evans “Cityscape” nightstands, striped bedding, and antique bronze table lamps with African mud cloth shades.

The 1970s textile art in the office is a vintage find of Burns’s that Soll loves. “I used to go to Rodney’s apartment when we first started dating, and I would be like, ‘Oh my God! Like, if we break up, I’m never gonna be able to find this tapestry,’” he says.

Soll got permission from his landladies to retile the bathroom and add a new vanity. The metal Curtis Jeré artwork comes from Burns’s collection.

The bohemian architecture of the home lends quirkiness to the design. The main bedroom isn’t a perfect square, making it seem like a stainless steel bed from TTMM is placed on a diagonal but is actually following the geometry of a very funky, angular bedroom.

The couple has taken their time tinkering and adjusting the decor, taking a considered approach to collecting and combining their belongings. Soll describes the overall vibe as having an Afrocentric theme that’s also a little bit Ralph Lauren. “Our visions have mingled,” Burns says.

Still, there is one last item the couple needs to complete the design: a blanket basket. But, says Soll, “We still haven’t found one we agree upon.”

The backyard is also home to a shed (not pictured), which functions as Burns’s closet.

#4949. Unocoin keeps deposits and withdrawals halted over suspect transfers

Unocoin said the transfers were signed and broadcast outside BitGo’s platform, and BitGo said the account freeze worked as intended.

Update (Nov. 13, 2026, 12:30 pm UTC): This article has been updated to include BitGo’s response and clarify that the account freeze applied to activity routed through BitGo’s platform.

Indian cryptocurrency exchange Unocoin has kept deposits and withdrawals suspended as it investigates suspicious wallet transfers and a breach of its own systems.

BitGo froze Unocoin’s enterprise account on Nov. 6 at the exchange’s request. Unocoin said the freeze appeared to have worked as intended and that the transfers were signed and broadcast onchain outside BitGo’s platform, according to its Friday update.

“Our review found that the transaction examined was signed using the user and backup keys held by Unocoin and broadcast externally onchain, without BitGo’s participation. BitGo’s systems were not breached in connection with that transaction,” BitGo said in a statement to Cointelegraph.

The company said the freeze applied only to activity routed through its platform and that the transfers were signed using keys Unocoin held outside BitGo’s systems.

Unocoin said it was investigating possible use of the wallet’s backup signing option and whether any cryptocurrency was lost. The exchange did not respond to Cointelegraph’s request for more information before publication.

Unocoin first detected unauthorized activity on Nov. 6 and suspended all services on Monday after detecting further activity. On Thursday, it disclosed outgoing transfers from an Ethereum address associated with its BitGo wallets and said its infrastructure had been compromised.

A Nov. 5 warning from India’s cybersecurity agency described an AI-assisted campaign targeting crypto exchanges and other financial firms. No connection to Unocoin’s incident has been established.

Related: India plans first tokenized bonds using wholesale CBDC: Report

#4947. Israeli police arrest five in $5M WhatsApp crypto trading probe

Police suspect the group drew up fictitious loan agreements to disguise crypto trading proceeds it transferred directly into bank accounts.

Israeli police arrested five people on Tuesday in a money laundering investigation involving about 16 million shekels ($5 million) in crypto transactions arranged through a WhatsApp group.

Investigators from the National Unit for Combating Economic Crime within Lahav 433 suspect the group provided crypto services without a license and failed to report transactions to Israel’s tax authorities. Police allege the group used fictitious agreements to make crypto proceeds transferred into bank accounts look like loans, according to The Jerusalem Post.

Authorities seized wallets, computers and tens of thousands of shekels in cash while searching the suspects’ homes. Investigators questioned the five on suspicion of money laundering, fraud and providing financial asset services without a license.

Israel’s Tax Authority announced two other arrests on Oct. 6 in cases involving suspected failures to report crypto activity. One involved trades worth tens of millions of dollars. In the other, an Ashdod resident allegedly failed to report income from about $13 million in crypto trades between 2019 and 2021.

A November 2024 audit by Israel’s State Comptroller found that about 500 people reported crypto activity each year on average from 2018 to 2022, out of at least 200,000 potential filers.

#4943. Nakamoto shares fall 6% premarket after Q3 revenue turns negative

A $5.8 million loss on Bitcoin options pushed operating revenue below zero, and higher Bitcoin prices lifted net income to $85.5 million.

Shares in Nasdaq-listed Bitcoin treasury company Nakamoto fell 6.5% in premarket trading on Friday after the company reported that losses on Bitcoin options pushed third-quarter operating revenue below zero.

Nakamoto’s shares traded at $5.40 at 12:02 pm UTC on Friday, according to Yahoo Finance. At that price, the stock was down about 62% year to date.

Nakamoto reported its third-quarter results after the market closed on Thursday. The company generated $3.9 million in revenue from its businesses. A $5.8 million net loss on Bitcoin options brought total operating revenue to negative $1.9 million, according to its quarterly filing.

Analysts had expected about $17.4 million in revenue, according to estimates compiled by Yahoo Finance.

Nakamoto reported $85.5 million in net income and a $15.8 million adjusted operating loss for the quarter. A $110.4 million gain from revaluing its digital assets, chiefly Bitcoin (BTC), drove the profit. The adjusted operating loss excludes that gain.

Nakamoto held 4,283 BTC worth $357.9 million as of Sept. 30, down from 4,467 BTC at the end of June.

#4941. Arthur Hayes ‘flattered’ as Coinbase backs US stock perps

Arthur Hayes said he was “flattered” by Coinbase’s defense of US stock perpetual futures, a decade after BitMEX launched perpetual swaps.

Arthur Hayes, co-founder of BitMEX, said he was “flattered” by Coinbase’s support for the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) in a US legal dispute over stock perpetual futures.

“When we launched the perpetual swap at BitMEX in 2016, Washington’s reaction was less than warm,” Hayes told Cointelegraph. “Ten years later, Coinbase is in federal court defending the right to offer them on US stocks. I’m flattered. Imitation is the sincerest form of regulation.”

Coinbase and five other companies submitted a brief on Monday opposing the alliance’s request to block trading in perpetual futures tied to individual stocks. The court accepted the brief on Tuesday.

The Capital Markets Alliance Association (CMAA), which brought the lawsuit, says the contracts should be treated as security-based swaps, which face tighter restrictions on retail trading.

Hayes was CEO of BitMEX when the crypto derivatives exchange launched its Bitcoin perpetual swap, which let traders take leveraged positions on Bitcoin without a fixed expiration date. The product helped make perpetual swaps a staple of crypto trading.

The US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) reportedly submitted a proposed court brief on Monday, opposing the alliance’s request to temporarily halt trading in the contracts.

The agency said it was reviewing the exchanges’ rules for listing the contracts. The brief left open whether the SEC would set those rules aside and whether the contracts qualify as security futures or security-based swaps.

Related: EU DeFi proposals could push users to riskier platforms, says Coinbase exec

In a Tuesday court filing, CMAA said that the CFTC had approved the contracts without adequately explaining its decision or addressing unresolved regulatory questions.

The group also cited about $375 million in reported 24-hour notional trading volume for Kalshi’s stock perpetuals shortly after launch, claiming the products were already diverting business from traditional stock markets.

Stock perpetuals let traders speculate on individual share prices without owning the underlying stocks. Unlike traditional futures, the contracts have no fixed expiration date.

On Nov. 3, US District Judge Valerie Caproni denied the group’s request for a temporary restraining order because it had yet to show likely irreparable harm. She found the alliance was likely to succeed on its claim that the CFTC exceeded its authority.

Wojciech Kaszycki, strategy adviser to BTCS SA, said the lack of a fixed expiration or delivery date could complicate the case for treating perpetual contracts as futures.

Kaszycki told Cointelegraph that the case “sale for future delivery.”

However, he warned that a ruling against the CFTC could push trading activity toward offshore and decentralized platforms.

“The product doesn’t die; it just changes address,” he said.

#4940. The Guy’s Guide to Adult Acne

We’re all stressed AF. The food we’re eating probably isn’t helping. Everyone told us we wouldn’t have to worry about our hormones going crazy after our teen years. What the heck is going on?

THE YEAR WAS 1997. I’d just made the varsity baseball team as a freshman, and I was ready to shoot my shot with every senior girl who’d give me the time of day. The only problem: I had also hard-launched my pepperoni face. I’m not talking surface-level New York slice stuff—these breakouts were Detroit-style-pizza deep. Every clogged pore had clocked in at the same time, and I was an absolute wreck.

For six months, I’d burned through everything the CVS aisles had to offer: Oxy Pads, Stridex, Noxzema, whatever stuff was hawking a promise of clear skin. Nothing worked. My face just got redder, drier, and more irritated. My mom (thank god for her) dragged me to the dermatologist, and I walked out with a prescription for isotretinoin, a.k.a. Accutane. Five months later, my skin was completely clear. My mini CVS stash went into the trash, and I thought that’d be the end of it.

I was wrong. I spent years assuming that because I took Accutane as a teenager, I’d paid my dues, but as a 44-year-old dude, I still get the occasional pimple or two—seemingly right before a big work meeting or whenever I have a lot of stuff on my plate. And it still messes with my head. Does my hygiene suck? Are my hormones to blame? Am I sweating too much? Apparently, I’m not the only one.

According to Christopher Bunick, MD, PhD, an associate professor of dermatology at the Yale School of Medicine, 83 percent of teen boys will deal with acne before they reach their 20s, which is often due to a surge in testosterone at the time. And while acne typically clears up as testosterone levels stabilize in our 20s, it can make a surprise comeback in our 30s, 40s, 50s, and beyond—with around 20 percent of men in their 30s dealing with breakouts, but only 12 percent seeking treatment from a medical professional. It’s not just a skin issue: Research shows that adult acne is consistently linked to a higher risk of developing anxiety, depression, and reduced self-esteem.

Acne (not to be mistaken for folliculitis, which can result from shaving) is mainly caused by a combination of excess oil production and dead skin cells that clog pores. This can create an environment where strains of Cutibacterium acnes—the bacteria naturally found on your skin—become imbalanced and trigger inflammation. This could look like blackheads and whiteheads, raised pustules and papules, or the deeper, more painful nodules or cysts that are more likely to scar. And despite what your instincts tell you, excessively scrubbing your face won’t help—it could actually harm your skin barrier. “You cannot wash away hormones,” says Dr. Bunick. Acne is an inflammatory condition, not a hygiene problem.

Scientists still aren’t sure exactly what makes some guys more susceptible to acne than others, but genetics and hormones are two leading factors. Research suggests about 80 percent of your acne risk is inherited. Your genes determine how sensitive your skin is to the hormones (i.e., androgens) that regulate the sebaceous glands and how aggressively your immune system overreacts to fight the inflammation. Then there are the lifestyle factors that play a smaller role but can collectively add up, such as diet (Dr. Bunick is a believer in the gut-skin axis—the idea that an imbalanced gut microbiome can drive systemic inflammation that can predispose you to acne), stress (this spikes cortisol, which also drives oil production and contributes to inflammation), sleep deprivation (same), and testosterone replacement therapy.

Though there isn’t a cure for acne yet, there are plenty of treatment options. “Untreated acne doesn’t just affect your skin. It affects how you move through the world,” says Dr. Bunick. “Acne impacts confidence, whether it’s making friends, having a social or dating life, or performance in work, sports, or other extracurriculars.” Earlier intervention means less of a psychosocial toll, less scarring, and (you heard the man) more confidence.

So if your bathroom still looks like mine did in the ’90s, it’s time to investigate what might be causing the chaos in your largest organ and learn how to treat it. The stories below will help you get your skin clear and, hopefully, keep it that way.

Bob Gearing is a New York-based writer covering men’s grooming from the perspective of a very real (and very sweaty) dude. By day, he’s a well-SPF’d tech sales bro; by night, he’s a red-light mask guy who definitely didn’t think he’d care this much about skincare. When he’s not testing the latest face serum, you’ll find him sweating through a round of golf or holding out hope that the Lions will make the Super Bowl before he dies.
