#4939. 9 Best Duvet Covers: Editor Sets to Style a Bedscape (2026)

Besides pieces like quilts and coverlets, I always say duvet covers are the best accessories to give a bedscape a bit more personality. Take it from Ariel Kaye, founder of juggernaut bedding brand Parachute, who has filled her eclectic LA home with a range of duvet covers that span vibrant, breezy linens to neutral, crisp percales.

To truly find the best of the best, I rounded up a group of writers and editors to test duvet covers from our favorite bedding brands—featuring muted, structured covers for minimalists and trendy colorways and patterns for maximalists. As for our top pick, we went with Boll & Branch’s Signature Hemmed Duvet Set since it’s organic, has a smooth and breathable feel, and comes in tons of prints and colors for easy styling.

If you’re still on the fence about what to go with for your bedroom, I also tapped AD PRO Directory designers to share what they look for in duvet covers, including materiality and design. Read below to create your dream bedscape this season.

For consistency, all prices listed reflect queen-size duvets unless otherwise noted, but up to king sizes are available for purchase from each brand.

Boll & Branch is one of AD’s favorite brands when it comes to bedding. With super soft sheets and other products made from 100% organic cotton, they have a heavy focus on sustainability and directly sourcing raw materials. The duvet cover follows suit, with long-staple cotton making for a durable piece of bedding that I’ve found is breathable enough for both hot and cold nights.

This organic-cotton duvet set can please pretty much anyone with a traditional bedroom. It has a tried-and-true cotton sateen material that gets softer with every wash, and designs—including subtle botanical prints—that are a must for anyone with classic taste. The earthy tones are great for a grounded bedroom too.

Bring the outdoors in with your styling by pairing one of Boll & Branch’s botanical prints with a textured linen sheet set.

Shop AD editors have stood behind Boll & Branch for years because of the brand’s sustainable business model and high-quality bedding. The signature cotton sheets have even earned a top spot in our best-of guides for the last five years. Just like these sheets, shopping producer Lauren Arzbaecher tells me that this Boll & Branch duvet cover’s sateen weave has a buttery soft texture. “The cotton is very smooth, and I maintained a comfortable temperature throughout the night even when bundled up underneath all my bedding,” she says. As a cold sleeper, Arzbeacher loves layering extra throws and blankets on top of her duvet, and she says this ultra-breathable material has all the “cuddly qualities” you’d want without making you overheat. Even better, the bedding is 100% organic, so you can rest knowing you’re not breathing in any harmful chemicals.

This sateen duvet cover also has a zipper closure, so it’s a lot easier for Arzbaecher to slip her Company Store down insert on and off than other button-closure covers she’s tried. “Their solid color range has a lovely mix of earthy hues, and the brand has been releasing more and more patterns, from stripes to florals,” for styling a bed, she adds.

I’m not sure what’s left to say about this bedding brand that hasn’t already been plastered across subway ads or Instagram feeds, but in short, Brooklinen is somewhat of a bedding celebrity. It has garnered cool-kid status by offering top-quality products using Oeko-Tex-certified products at reasonable prices.

The Classic Duvet Cover is made of percale, which can be compared to a crisp cotton button-down shirt. It’s soft and sophisticated in a matte, buttery finish that can be attributed to long-staple cotton fibers that are spun to make stronger but finer yarn.

We are not only big fans of Brooklinen products for their breathable, sustainable materials but also for their versatile designs. With 20 earthy tones, I recommend this duvet cover for any type of bedroom style—but I’d lean towards purchasing for a coastal design because of the soothing colors and prints.

Like Alpert, who has this duvet cover in her bedroom, I recommend styling it with textured linen sheets for a contrasting yet comfortable fabric combination and a throw blanket in complementary neutral and oceanic hues.

Not one but two AD writers love this Brooklinen cotton duvet cover all year long. Shopping writer Julia Harrison calls this super, super soft and very cool to the touch. “It stays cool even when I’ve wrapped myself all the way in it like a caterpillar so I’m never overheating, but I am staying comfy and warm,” she shares. Contributor Yelena Moroz Alpert says this is her favorite duvet cover of all time, hands-down, because of its unmatched airy and parachute-like quality. “The percale duvet cover has this smooth feel but at the same time it is remarkably light and crisp,” she says. “Apparently, it’s all because of the high-quality single-ply yarns, which are made from strong, long-staple cotton fibers that give it that buttery touch.”

The style potential is a plus here too. Alpert loves the “oyster” print since it strays away from more expected patterns like stripes filling up her linen closet. She got the matching sheets in the same print for this exact reason, saying the swirls remind her of “Hokusai’s waves and stand out in the sea of florals and plaids” in her linen closet.

From pajamas to sheets, Cozy Earth is a favorite of ours—and Oprah thanks to frequent appearances in her Favorite Things lists—for cooling bedding made of bamboo fabric. Its offerings also ring in at a budget-friendly price (especially with the brand’s frequent sales).

Many of the colors in Cozy Earth’s collection are grounded in the earth (to be expected). With this in mind, I see this duvet cover in a minimalist, bohemian, or coastal bedroom. Alpert’s peony pick is just as nice for a romantic bedroom or something seasonal for summer.

The delicate nature of the bedding (both in texture and color) makes it a striking contrast against crisp, patterned accents, like a percale floral sheet set.

When I asked Alpert what her favorite qualities are about Cozy Earth’s signature, silky duvet cover, there were almost too many to count. “This duvet cover is a trifecta of perks: It looks good, feels good, and won’t slip off the comforter,” she says. “But if I had to pick one, I’d say that I love the delicate pink hue and the equally delicate feel of the cover—just looking at my bed makes me want to crawl into it.”

The material (100% viscose from bamboo) has a breathable and smooth feel to it that’s nice for hot sleepers but also anyone who doesn’t want a more textured cover like linen. Even better, the soft, pastel-inspired color options like peony pink and laurel green make it a great fit for styling a bed during this blooming season. “The fabric is so delicate and dreamy that it reminds me of flower petals,” Alpert tells me. “Since I chose the peony colorway, it definitely fits the romantic profile.”

Even though it has a smooth texture, the comforter inside won’t slip around because of the secure snap closures. “Cozy Earth has 12(!) points of contact between the comforter and the cover, so there is no way anything is going to get loose, especially because these are snapped on, and not tied, like many other duvet covers,” she says. “I pair it with the bamboo viscose comforter with standard fill and the combination works great because the comforter has some weight and the duvet cover does not mess with it in any way.”

A darling of the 2010s direct to consumer movement, Parachute is a home brand best known for its bedding, though the company has also dabbled in furniture, mattresses, and other essentials. Its products, including the Linen Duvet Cover, are Oeko-Tex Standard 100–certified, meaning that every element (even buttons!) has been tested for substances damaging to human health. Parachute also doesn’t artificially soften their products. In the wrong hands, linen can be itchy or stiff until it’s been broken in, but that wasn’t the case here. This version, made of European Flax and made in the Guimarães region of Portugal, was inviting and oh so soft. The company also touts the material as naturally antimicrobial, which is a plus.

Another pick for a bohemian bedroom, this duvet cover has a lived-in look and several organic colors to choose from.

If you, in true minimalist fashion, like a monochrome bedscape, style this duvet cover with linen sheets in natural colors. If you want a slightly moodier bedroom, try a shade like cacao or indigo.

Contributor Lila Allen has nothing but good things to say about the feel of this linen and its nonchalant elegance: “I was pleasantly surprised at how soft this cover was—with linen, sometimes it can be a roll of the dice. This set had the best of both worlds: super supple but still casually elevated. The linen is a nice thickness, too; I can’t see any trace of the duvet inside of it.” Parachute’s linen has 175 GSM, and Quince’s linen that I mention down below has around 150 GSM. To Allen’s point, this means Parachute’s is more durable with a substantial feel and less opaque (the reason why she can’t see her duvet insert peeking through) with a lighter cover color like toast or sand.

Allen pairs her duvet cover with an all-season comforter from Riley, which she says stays secure through a button closure on the side and gives just the right amount of rumple together. The insert moves around a bit inside of the cover but mostly remains in place. “Best of all, I’ve been sleeping soundly with the set on hot nights as well as ones where the AC has been on overdrive, and so far it holds up in both situations—not too warm or cool.” Allen tells me that she shares a bed with her “hot-blooded husband and a large Maine Coon cat,” so her bed usually runs warm. This layerable, snuggly cover is perfect.

The home brand is headquartered in New York City, however, the materials are sourced from fourth-generation, family-owned textile mills in Portugal and other countries across Europe. The products span a range of bedding and bath products, namely duvet covers, sheets, towels, mats, and other accessories like pillows and robs.

Since all of the duvet covers come in neutral, unfussy colors, this is a great option for minimalists.

I style this simply with other neutrals like white or cream sheets. When I want to add more visual interest, I layer in butter yellow bedding or a quilt in soft, muted tones.

To put it simply, this duvet cover feels like a lightweight cashmere sweater. It’s not stuffy, but it’s not flimsy either. Riley Home’s somehow managed to make a cover that feels airy enough for nights when my apartment’s radiator runs overly hot and still warm enough when I want to curl up in bed after a cold commute from work.

Even though it feels soft, it’s not made of pure cashmere. In reality, it’s made of 95% cotton and just 5% cashmere, but I wouldn’t have noticed if I didn’t read the label so closely. The texture has a brushed quality to it that feels gentle and light on my skin. There are a few neutral colors on the site. I went with the oatmeal and have liked styling it with white sheet sets so far for a simple, not-overly-done-up bedspread. A couple of other thoughtful details are the interior ties and buttons to keep my comforter from shifting inside. The super-easy care sold me too. The brand doesn’t recommend dry cleaning—just a machine wash and dry with a gentle detergent will do. As someone who has lugged specialty cashmere bedding to NYC cleaners on foot through the years, this note in and of itself almost convinced me to give the bedding a try.

Quince’s entire deal is about making high-quality luxury products at an affordable price. They work only with factories that are both sustainable and pay a fair wage, which is something that many shoppers care enough about to pay a little extra for, but Quince cuts back on things like fancy packaging to keep costs down.

Quince is another AD favorite for its plethora of prints and solid colors. Try this duvet cover in your space—no matter its design style—for a lightweight addition to your bedscape that’s also budget friendly.

Since linen makes a statement on its own with its lived-in look, I recommend styling this duvet cover with Quince’s classic European linen sheet set and a fluffy duvet insert in moody neutral hues (think deep brown) for a cozy sleep oasis. It’s also neutral enough that you can add some pattern sheeting for extra personality.

I’ve had this Quince linen duvet for about a year and it’s been a mainstay since it’s substantial and textured while still having an airiness to it—even when I’m layering with a few throw blankets or quilts when it’s cold outside. Sometimes linen gets a bad rap for its rough texture, but this best seller has been gentle against my skin and only gotten softer with more routine wear and care. It’s not as soft as something like an heirloom-quality stonewashed linen, but I’m not too picky in the linen department. I think its Oeko-Tex certification gives it a leg up generally to some competitors in this fabric category too.

I chose this Quince duvet since there were so many color options to choose from. I love moody colors year-round, and the burgundy is true to the deep color I saw on the site and it hasn’t faded after months of washes. It has the relaxed look you know and love with linen materials. Steaming it knocks out wrinkles easily whenever I want a more put-together look in my bedroom, though.

At this price point and with the most colors and prints available in this guide, I think this comforter cover can give you a great, budget-friendly way to dress a bed. The button closures also help keep a duvet insert or comforter in place. The care has been simple. I wash it with similar colors on a cold cycle and tumble dry on low and have only noticed a loose thread here and there, but nothing too crazy.

As mentioned above in our top overall pick, Brooklinen is known as the “home of the internet’s favorite sheets,” so we find it to be a worthy destination for quality bedding—including this quilt-like duvet cover made with 100% cotton.

With a design that mirrors the appearance of a quilt, we think this duvet cover would dress a vintage-inspired space beautifully—like the bedding you’d find in a historic Victorian home.

Alpert chose the plum colorway because of its sophisticated look that anchors her bedroom. She shares that the hue looks exceptionally well-paired with the deep caramel of the Boll & Branch Grand waffle blanket, as the color almost works like a moody neutral.

Even though the Brooklinen website describes this duvet as “breezy,” Alpert says it feels more like a weighted blanket. “The ribbed side is certainly quilt-like, which is not surprising because each ribbon that runs through the duvet cover is padded ever so slightly,” she shares.

The cover has a two-sided design: one side with textured, ribbed matelasse and the other with a crisp percale fabric. Alpert thinks this material combo is genius since it keeps the duvet cover from feeling too heavy. “This is especially key for anyone who sleeps without a flat sheet—you’ll feel like you’re getting into freshly changed linens night after night,” she says. “On the flip side, the ribbed texture elevates the duvet cover, not only in how it looks but it gives your bedding a slightly cushy sensation.”

Rest assured that the feel isn’t as heavy as an actual weighted blanket. Alpert says it certainly has substance and structure, though. “When I sleep with it on, it feels as if I am very secure—no one will dare wake me up,” she notes.

Hommey is a relatively new Australian-based brand that got its start in 2020. It became popular for robes and nightwear in bold, fashionable stripes. The designs are now spread across the bedding category, including cotton and linen sheets and duvet covers.

Since the brand is known for more out-of-the-box colorways across its collections, this duvet color is a great option for maximalists looking to experiment with bold color-blocking.

For a moodier bedscape, I style this duvet cover with sheets in a deep tone like burgundy. Overall, the chocolatey hue acts like a neutral and pairs beautifully with lighter sheet colors like oatmeal, which can also complement the cream-colored trim.

I know we’ve talked about linen a couple of times already in this story, but Hommey’s duvet helps hit the trifecta for the category. The natural flax fiber felt smoother and more broken-in than my Quince version right out the package. My guess is that it’s because of the stonewashed finish, which is known to give scratchy linen a more silky quality. I won’t get ahead of myself and say it feels like pure silk, but I can confirm that it’s soft and breathable almost like an organic cotton duvet after a few washes. The duvet caught my attention even more because of all of the styles. There are over 30 fashionable colors and prints, like the oxford and plum reversible cotton duvet I want in a button-down shirt version. The linen offering has four distinct colorways, including the brown sugar with cream trim that I got for myself. I like deep colors for my bedscape, especially as the year starts inching towards fall and winter, and its rich hue hasn’t disappointed. It's the perfect complement to my autumnal color palette, especially when I pair it with burgundy linen sheets.

It is less expensive than the Parachute pick and has a plusher texture than the Quince, so I’d say this is the perfect middle ground for an approachable but high-quality linen duvet.

Garnet Hill was founded in New Hampshire in the mid-1970s by Pegge and Grant Dowse who built the brand on a commitment to sustainable materials, thoughtful craftsmanship, and responsible sourcing with products like organic cotton and flannel bedding. The bedding blends traditional styles with a modern touch to fit in a range of bedrooms.

Our tester says the Pear Garden print is completely unexpected. “As much as I love florals, stripes, and gingham, a girl needs a break…and these charming pears are just the right motif,” Alpert shares. “They signal a cottagecore moment without going over the top. Every time I glimpse the cheery yellow fruit, I crack a little smile. Plus, the colorway of bold yellow, sage green, brown, and cream are easy to coordinate with the rest of my bedding collection.”

Because the pear motifs are the statement, we think you can go with more subtle sheets like a white set with embroidered trim. If you’re more of a maximalist, you can lean into the pattern play with a gingham sheet set in a complementary muted tone like brown and extra layers like a faux fur blanket if you’re cozying up for winter.

According to Alpert, this duvet cover is synonymous with cozy. It’s the softest option on this list because of the brushed flannel fabric, and it’s also a great pick if you live in cold climates since it has a bit of heft to it. “It’s as if a layer of leaves, or in this case pears, is surrounding me during winter hibernation,” she says. “That’s in part because this specific flannel is woven to have a substantial six-ounce weight.”

Alpert’s had this for nearly one year and says it has a decadent texture, likening it to climbing under a layer of meringue when she pairs it with her fluffy Ostermoor down duvet insert. “The plush feel of the cover also makes me think that I’ve just scored an upgrade to a royal suite,” Alpert adds.

At this point, she hasn’t noticed any pilling, but she does note that the cover is a bit snugger than a typical king, so you can expect the duvet to feel a little loftier than a regular fit.

What to Look for in the Best Duvet Covers

A duvet cover is more than just a bedcover. This eye-catching textile has a large footprint in any bedroom and can instantly give your space a refresh—whether with unique colors and prints or something more modest—especially if you want to disguise an old duvet insert. With this in mind, I tapped AD PRO Directory designers to weigh in on what to look for in a duvet cover to elevate your bedroom.

Material: When it comes to duvet covers, everyone has their own personal preferences. For Nicole R. Fisher, CEO and founder of Nicole Fisher Interiors, it’s linen: “They’re effortless, lightweight, and airy. I think the simplicity of the linen transcends different styles and spaces,” she says. Fisher notes that the feel is a big factor when selecting a duvet cover.

Carmen René Smith, founder and principal designer of Aquilo Interiors, says that it also needs to feel soft to the touch, breathable, and cozy. “What’s the point of a beautiful duvet if you don’t want to snuggle into it?” she says. “Texture is key too—whether it’s silky, crisp, or nubby, it should add depth to the space.”

Comfort: All of the interior designers I spoke to for this story point to natural fibers, not only because of their quality but also their airflow and breathability. Shelbie Cox of Shelbie Cox Studio recommends natural fibers like organic cotton and linen and notes that polyester and other synthetic fibers are best to avoid since they can trap heat.

Design: With so many patterns and colors to choose from, it’s important to keep your bedroom’s design style in mind. If you like more of a minimalist bedroom, Cox suggests a fine stripe pattern (like Quince’s European Linen Duvet Cover) or a monochromatic, muted color palette (see the Cozy Earth Bamboo Duvet Cover) for your duvet cover set.

If you want to go bold for your bedroom, there are options aplenty. “We’re maximalists, so we say bump up the color and pattern no matter what your signature style is!” says Smith, adding that it’s important to still maintain balance. “If you have bold wallpaper, pull a color from it and go for a solid, textured duvet—like a rich velvet for drama or a soft linen for a more relaxed vibe. If you’re mixing patterns, think about scale—a bold, oversized print pairs best with a smaller, more delicate duvet pattern to give the eye a place to rest.”

If you don’t want to commit to one style just yet, rest assured that having a few duvets in rotation can help you keep your bedroom design fresh. “I tend to go simple on bedding and let the accessories be the pops of pattern and color that’s easily swapped out,” adds Fisher.

Maintenance: While these are great design accessories for a bedroom, they also need to be practical. Ashley Ross, founder and principal designer of Muse Noire Interiors recommends keeping these questions in mind when shopping: How does it fasten (zipper, tie up, or buttons)? Does it fit within your washer and dryer? This will help you know how to use it and how to care for it.

We tested the best duvet covers the same way we test products for the rest of our sleep content: by bringing the items in our homes and dressing them on our beds. This way, you’re guaranteed to get a real-life look at how the products perform after weeks and months of everyday use. We also wash them routinely to keep an eye out for any wear and tear. All of the brands we feature reflect a variety of price points, designs, and materials so you have the best options to choose from for your bedroom.

Jump to the “How We Tested” section of this article to watch an in-depth video about our testing methodology and some of our favorite products and brands.

You don’t necessarily need a duvet cover. It depends on how you like to dress your bed. In my opinion, it’s a great bed accessory. A duvet cover not only protects your duvet insert but it also gives you the opportunity to easily switch up your bedscape (instead of swapping comforters when you want a new look). That said, pay attention to the care instructions for your insert because some do not recommend washing or dry cleaning.

There are several ways you can put a duvet cover on, but according to TikTok experts, this method below is as foolproof as possible:

Start by turning your duvet cover inside out, top side down, with the duvet opener at the foot of the bed. Then, place your comforter on top and secure the corners with the attached ties to the duvet cover. Starting at the head of the bed, roll the duvet down towards the foot just like a burrito. Finally, fold the duvet opening over each end of the roll, and unroll the blanket back towards the

Always check the manufacturer instructions to find out how to wash your specific duvet cover. However, the most common way to clean a duvet cover is by machine washing it with like colors with a gentle detergent on a cold cycle. If the duvet cover has a zipper closure, it's usually helpful to zip it closed to keep it from snagging in the washing machine.

It's generally recommended to wash your duvet cover as often as you wash your sheets, which is about once a week. If you use a top sheet, though, you can usually extend another week and wash your duvet cover every other week.

Our editors selected top products in this story from tried-and-true brands on the market, from Brooklinen to Boll & Branch. These brands boast organic materials like 100% organic cotton and European Flax and bamboo for a sustainably made bed. Plus, many boast eco-friendly certifications like GOTS and Oeko-Tex so you know they are free of chemicals. As a design editor, I also made sure all of the products have a range of colors, patterns, and prints to complement a bedroom’s style.

#4937. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Jane Street increased its share count in Fidelity’s spot Bitcoin ETF nearly eightfold in the third quarter, US President Donald Trump nominated four new SEC and CFTC commissioners, including a replacement for prominent crypto advocate Hester Peirce and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang abruptly resigned.

Jane Street’s share count in Fidelity’s spot Bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) rose nearly eightfold in the third quarter, while its holdings in BlackRock’s fund fell 19%.

The trading giant ended September with 7.01 million shares of Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC0, up from about 887,000 at the end of June. The position was worth $509 million on Sept. 30, according to its second-quarter and third-quarter filings.

Jane Street’s holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT) fell to 20.22 million shares from 24.88 million. A higher share price lifted the reported value of its stake to $957 million from $828 million.

Jane Street was the largest reported institutional holder of BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF at the end of June after boosting its share count by 324% in Q2, according to 13F Pro. IBIT is the world’s largest spot Bitcoin ETF with $56 billion in net assets as of Thursday.

The firm also added nearly 9 million shares (about $249 million) of the ARK 21Shares Bitcoin ETF (ARKB), while more than doubling its holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Ethereum Trust ETF (ETHA) by 7.83 million shares (about $158 million).

US President Donald Trump has nominated Steven Levine to replace former Securities and Exchange Commission Commissioner Hester Peirce, one of the agency’s most prominent crypto advocates, as part of four nominations to fill vacancies at the SEC and Commodity Futures Trading Commission.

The White House’s latest announcement on Thursday named two nominees each for the SEC and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, agencies whose decisions help shape US cryptocurrency regulation, among other functions. All four nominations require Senate confirmation.

The nominations would replenish several commissioner roles. The SEC currently has only two of its five commissioner seats filled, while CFTC Chairman Michael Selig is the sole member of his agency’s five-seat commission.

The nominations come weeks before Democrats are set to take control of the Senate in January, giving a narrow window for Republicans to confirm Trump’s picks.

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as president and CEO and stepped down from the company’s board on Thursday, effective immediately. The board named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.

Kress will retain her role as CFO while Nvidia’s board considers internal and external candidates for the permanent CEO role. The board also appointed Harvey C. Jones its first chair and said it expects Nvidia’s operations to proceed without interruption.

Nvidia shares fell more than 13% shortly after the US market opened on Thursday, trading at $151.00 as of 2:31 p.m. UTC, according to Yahoo Finance. Bitcoin (BTC) fell alongside Nvidia shares, dropping as much as 3.5% to a multimonth low.

The shares later pared losses to trade at $165 at 4:24 p.m. UTC, down 5% on the day. The stock was down more than 26% over the previous month.

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

#4935. The Paths to Clear(er) Skin

With a little trust and patience, it’s possible to find a solution that works for you. These three guys are proof.

Treating adult acne can feel like one big science experiment—until now. This is the guy’s guide to adult acne. Read the rest of the stories here.

IT’S ONE THING to get advice from a doctor (still important and invaluable), but it’s another to hear from other guys who’ve been there. Guys who understand what it’s like to cancel plans because of a whopper of a zit. Guys who’ve also hesitated to take their shirts off at the pool for fear of being judged for their bacne. We wanted to hear about their struggles, and successes—what finally worked to help them send breakouts packing and regain their confidence. As you’ll learn from these three men, no two plans are the same. But with a little consistency and some experimentation, blemish-free skin is possible.

I like to take care of myself. I work out at least five times a week. I’m consistent with my diet. But my confidence takes a hit because of my acne. Since I was a teenager, I’ve dealt with deep cystic breakouts that have totally upended my life. I’ve canceled dates; I’ve canceled trips; I won’t leave the house when I have a flare. It’s improved as I’ve gotten older—since 2023 I’ve been using Winlevi, a prescription hormone-blocking cream, in the morning and at night, and Tazorac, a doctor-dispensed retinoid that I’ve been using since my 20s, at night—but I still deal with the occasional breakout. And it still messes with my confidence.

I went to Greece over the summer, and I could feel a big one coming under my skin. The one thing that really makes a difference is the La Roche-Posay Effaclar Duo+M treatment, which you can get for $25 at the drugstore. I apply it religiously every few hours as soon as I feel a zit coming on, and sometimes it helps it come to a head faster or stops it from forming altogether. I’ve found a routine that works, but acne is always something lingering in the back of my mind. —Derek Monacchio, 38, tax director

Back acne is something I’ve had my entire life, but honestly, I just thought it was something I’d always have to deal with. I couldn’t reach back there with a cream or a wash, and I didn’t want to go on Accutane or any prescription medications. Even though most people couldn’t see it, I’d get very self-conscious whenever I’d take my shirt off in public, which sucked because I take good care of my body.

That all changed when I went in for a laser hair removal appointment at a physician-led laser center, The Kaga Institute, about six months ago. The office staff knew my situation with the breakouts on my back and told me they had something that could help—a new laser called AviClear, which supposedly suppresses oil gland production. I was curious, so I gave it a try. After three sessions, the pimples on my back were gone. It was totally painless for me—especially when compared to the laser hair removal I’ve tried. Each click of the laser felt like a warm sensation for a millisecond, and I was able to continue with my laser hair removal at the same time. Now that I’ve finished my series of treatments, I was told the results should improve with time. It feels great to have found a solution that works. I’m ready for next summer already. —William Sebok, 42, construction company owner

There’s a perception that when you have acne, you find a solution and then it’s over. That has not been my experience. My first breakouts (a mix of whiteheads and inflamed red bumps) started just as I was hitting puberty, around age 11, and persisted well into my 30s. I’ve seen a few different dermatologists since I was 15 and was prescribed everything under the sun—from clindamycin (a prescription antibiotic) to Accutane. I would take the medications for a few months at a time, and some would help for a while, but once I stopped, my skin would inevitably break out again. Plus, I experienced some side effects from the Accutane, including significant lethargy.

After I stopped taking the medications, I started looking at the labels of the skin care products I was using and noticed that when the ingredient list was extremely long, there was a higher likelihood there would be something in there that would clog my pores. [Editor’s note: Look for the label “noncomedogenic,” a.k.a. not pore clogging, on your products.] For me, it became more about avoiding certain products and soothing the skin barrier. I also became much more careful about not overloading my routine with too many strong ingredients. I focused instead on making sure the products I was using were appropriate for my acne-prone skin. That was the game changer. Another thing that causes my skin to react is my diet. If I’m eating a lot of high-glycemic foods and meals that are high in sugar, I immediately break out. I also try to wash my face or shower immediately after every workout, and if I can’t, I spray on a hypochlorous acid spray to kill the bacteria. Less is more, and I’m proof of that. —Brute Choi, 37, content creator

Brian Underwood is beauty director at Women’s Health, where he oversees content strategy for the brand across all platforms, including digital, print, and social. Underwood previously served as beauty and wellness director at Oprah Daily and O, The Oprah Magazine. During his tenure leading beauty content for the Oprah brand at Hearst, stories Underwood commissioned were awarded the Skin Cancer Foundation Media Award and a Fragrance Award for Editorial Excellence (his second). He was the launch Beauty Director of Dr. Oz THE GOOD LIFE, and has held additional editorial positions at Fitness, Organic Style, Good Housekeeping, Life & Style Weekly, and Woman’s Day and has written for Self, Shape, Seventeen, Redbook, Cosmopolitan, and many more. Underwood previously served on the Skin Cancer Foundation’s gala committee and as partnerships director of the Trans Beauty Clinic, a New York-based charitable organization that provided beauty services and workshops to the city’s trans community.

#4931. 8 Best Holiday Wreaths for a Festive Front Door (2026)

Nothing gets us feeling warm and fuzzy quite like passing by a fully adorned front door. Whether lit or unlit, fresh or faux, a well-appointed Christmas wreath is a visual signifier to all who pass that the festivities have officially begun in this home. While a classic ring of evergreens will never go out of style, there’s no shortage of modern takes on traditional winter foliage that will earn you points for both holiday spirit and good taste.

If cleaning up pine needles isn’t your thing, fear not: Faux wreaths are getting closer to the real thing by the season, saving you a little maintenance during an already busy time of year. Choose one accented with velvet ribbons for a touch of nostalgia, or opt for something that looks as though it was plucked straight from a whimsical snowscape, complete with pinecones and berries. And if you’ve historically never considered yourself a “wreath person,” take this as your sign to make this the year you become one. Turns out, participating in holiday cheer is just as delightful as it sounds.

While you may turn to Quince for an affordable cashmere sweater or linen bedding, the unsung treasures might surprise you. Take this faux foliage wreath, for instance, which mimics the look and feel of a Norfolk pine so convincingly, you’d never know the difference from the street. Simple and full, its bare branches make the perfect backdrop for trailing red bows, tinsel, or whatever other accoutrements you desire.

This faux greenery features impressively lifelike cypress leaves, while the berries add a bold, seasonal color. It’s designed for indoor and outdoor use and has UV protection. In short, it’s designed with durability in mind to outlast wintry weather and beyond. It'll also keep you from sweeping a single pine needle off your front porch (a gift in itself).

If evergreens, real or fake, aren't your thing, we say go full color. This Lulu and Georgia wreath is made of velvety baubles, each with a plush texture and rich hue. It's the perfect size for hanging from a cabinet, chair back, or interior door when you want to add a simple but effective holiday touch.

Something to make your days merry and bright, literally. This faux wreath comes pre-lit, saving you the hassle of weaving your own lights through the branches (and sparing you any DIY allegations). With softly draping cedar branches and a warm white glow, it makes an inviting addition to front doors, mantels, and more.

There's something especially charming about holiday decorations that look as though they've been gathered on a winter walk. This Magnolia wreath captures that feeling with lifelike foliage and bright red berries that add just the right amount of color. Even better, it’s currently 25% off. Hang it on your front door or prop it on a mantel for a dose of old-fashioned holiday spirit.

A few perfectly placed pinecones can make a wreath feel understated yet exceptionally sophisticated. This Afloral wreath is the kind of arrangement that lets everyone approaching your front door know they’re about to enter a well-decorated home, though you’d never make a big deal of it. The real-touch greenery is convincing enough to have guests wondering when you found the time to forage a Noble Fir yourself.

If you’re craving a departure from the expected, this copper wreath offers a fresh take on traditional holiday foliage. Eucalyptus and assorted leaves are finished in shimmering shades of copper and gold, lending the arrangement a decidedly more glamorous feel. Think less woodland Christmas, more fancy dinner party.

If you’re dreaming of a white Christmas, this wreath will certainly do the trick. Covered in clusters of snowy white berries, it has a soft, wintry look that feels merry without leaning too heavily into traditional red and green. It's a lovely option for anyone who prefers a more pared-back holiday palette, and one that will look just as fitting long after the presents have been unwrapped.

#4924. Artists Helen Marden and the Late Brice Marden Refashioned a Decommissioned 19th-Century Ferry Terminal

Over the course of their more than 50-year marriage, artist Helen Marden and her late husband, the eminent painter Brice Marden, amassed an international collection of seriously eccentric—and deeply magical—houses, gardens, and studios, each designed around the double helix of art and family. They bought their first house, a cliffside cottage on the Greek island of Hydra, in 1973, and since then real estate and art have been entwined in their life, art facilitating the purchase of property, the properties both catalyst and carapace for the evolution of their art. An Italianate villa in upstate New York, a pair of traditional riads in Marrakech, a hotel set among the ruins of a 19th-century sugar plantation in the Caribbean, a second house by the port in Hydra, a Renaissance Revival town house in Manhattan, a stately manse on a sprawling estate in Pennsylvania—every one of their idiosyncratic properties has its own fascinating history, reflected in its interior. The homes are all documented in the upcoming book Brice & Helen Marden: Houses, Gardens, Studios (Gagosian), edited by this writer, with photography by Stephen Kent Johnson.

Helen Marden in the living room. Chair and side table by the Campana Brothers. The chimney is clad in Clé Tile. A watercolor by Henri Michaux rests on the mantel.

The living room includes chairs by Marcel Wanders (far left) and the Campana Brothers. Sculpture at right by Michael Wang.

In the dining room, a Paavo Tynell pendant hangs above a rustic farm table with chairs by Jonathan Olivares and Maarten Baas. Amazonia marble covers one wall.

The Mardens were instinctively drawn to residences with endurance, places that have seen a few things, not newly built pleasure domes devoid of texture and soul. Swan House, the riparian hideaway in the middle of the Hudson River, epitomizes the couple’s embrace of the strange and the enchanted. Accessed by a long, narrow causeway that leads off the shore of Saugerties, the structure was built in 1877 as part of Long Dock, a ship terminal and moorage for large vessels conveying people and goods the length of the Hudson. It was also a landing for ferries crossing the river between Saugerties and the village of Tivoli. One can see Swan House shimmering in the landscape from the edge of Rose Hill, the Marden family’s 19th-century Tivoli estate.

The Depression, along with the advent of more efficient modes of transport, scuppered the last of the large ships landing at Long Dock in the early 1930s, but ferries continued to make the two-minute trip across the water until 1938. After that, the structure was decommissioned from professional service and sold, becoming the only privately owned peninsula on the Hudson. The Mardens purchased the property in 2011, but it took a full decade before they engaged designer Daniel Sachs and architect Kevin Lindores of the AD100 firm Sachs Lindores Architecture, Interiors to execute a full-scale renovation.

The primary bath features custom zellige tiles, a vintage Vistosi chandelier, an antique marble sink, and a tub by The Water Monopoly.

Sachs Lindores remodeled the entry with a windscreen of glass brick. The chair is by Gaetano Pesce.

Yellow zellige tiles meet a pale pink Aga range beneath a custom zinc hood in the kitchen. The pendant light is by Adolf Loos from WOKA.

Brice Marden’s For Helen – Valentine 2019 (2015–18) hangs on the black-tiled fireplace tower that mediates the bedroom and living room.

A custom bed is flanked by an antique French console table and a Charles Rohlfs hall chair. Lighting includes a Franz West chandelier and a floor lamp by Achille and Pier Giacomo Castiglioni.

“Early on we demolished a few walls to open up the views, but it was pretty raw. We’d just go over and watch the boats go by,” Helen recalls. “You have a completely different relationship with the river when you’re sitting in the middle of it.”

Given the famously cultivated, idiosyncratic tastes of the homeowners, the collaboration between Sachs Lindores and their clients was unusually fertile. Together, they reimagined the derelict building as a trick box for trapping the natural light that bounces off the water on all sides, with reflective, lead-coated copper siding and a zinc roof on the exterior and fields of luminous plaster, tile, and marble within. “Helen never shies away from a bold gesture. At first, she told us to paint the house red, but when we presented the idea of a glistening metal-clad box with light dancing on the skin, she immediately went for it,” Sachs recalls. “Brice and Helen were very interconnected in their aesthetic tastes and decision making,” Lindores adds. “As you might imagine, they studied every paint color and trim detail.”

Ruins of the 19th-century dock remain alongside the house.

A guest room is outfitted with a Pierre Jeanneret daybed, lounge chairs by Monika Mulder for IKEA, a vintage Kevin Walz standing lamp, and a Mathieu Matégot magazine rack. Painting by Helen Marden.

Sigmar Polke’s Untitled (1998) hangs above a vintage Walter Knoll sofa in the first-floor sitting room.

While retaining the shape of its original architectural envelope, the house was essentially rebuilt, with new window configurations, open circulation, and an exposed ceiling, all in service of maximizing watery vistas and ease of use. On the lofty upper level, the existing chimney, newly revealed in its full floor-to-ceiling height, was clad in glossy black brick tile. The monolithic form now feels like a high altar in a temple of light. Custom zellige tiles imported from Fez cover the walls of the kitchen and baths, where Helen’s genius for juxtaposing colors and materials comes into high relief.

The polyglot ensemble of furnishings hews closely to the signature Marden mix: Moroccan rugs; bold contemporary designs by the likes of Gaetano Pesce, Marcel Wanders, and the Campana Brothers; humble American folk pieces; 20th-century classics by Paavo Tynell, Adolf Loos, and Pierre Jeanneret; and a few Continental antiques to leaven the brew. “I just pick things that I like, things that make me happy,” Helen says, pooh-poohing the idea of some grand decorative vision. Her designers would demur. “She has an incredible eye,” Sachs insists. “The more crazy Helen would get, the more Brice liked it.”

#4923. Tesla drops car referral rewards, now pays owners in FSD for referrals

Tesla has launched a new referral program in the US that pays owners in Full Self-Driving (Supervised) instead of rewarding car sales. A referred owner gets one free month of FSD, and the referrer gets a free month or a $99 Tesla Credit if that person starts paying.

At the same time, Tesla’s Refer and Earn terms no longer list any vehicle as a qualifying purchase for buyer or referrer rewards. Buy a Tesla with a friend’s code today and neither of you gets anything.

To share a link, you need to be paying for FSD (Supervised) monthly or own it outright from before Tesla stopped selling it, according to Tesla’s FSD referral support page. Owners who got FSD through a one-year grant can’t participate. The link lives under “Refer and Earn” in the Tesla app, version 4.62.0 or later.

The person on the other end has to own a registered Tesla with compatible hardware, can’t have paid for FSD in the past 12 months, and can’t have bought it outright. There’s also a catch for recent buyers: anyone who got a free FSD trial with a new car is locked out of referral links until 12 months after that trial ends.

The bigger change is buried in Tesla’s Refer and Earn page. Buyer benefits now apply to solar panels only. Referrer benefits cover solar, first demo drives (up to 30 over an account’s lifetime) and the new FSD referral.

Model 3, Model Y and Cybertruck only show up under loyalty benefits, for existing owners buying another Tesla product. Model S and Model X aren’t listed at all.

Tesla didn’t announce the change. The page is stamped “Updated February 24, 2026,” so it’s unclear exactly when vehicles came off the list.

That’s quite a turn for a program that was always about moving cars. When Tesla brought the referral program back in August 2024, a buyer got $1,000 off a Model S, X, Y or 3, and the referrer got $500 in credit. Tesla has killed and revived the program several times over the past decade, but cars were always at the center of it. Not anymore.

That’s the exact metric in Elon Musk’s 2025 CEO Performance Award, which includes a milestone of 10 million active FSD subscriptions. It’s the reason Tesla started disclosing the number in January in the first place.

The free month a friend receives doesn’t count toward that total. A paid conversion does, and that’s precisely the event the new program pays for.

The only way I would ever pay for FSD is if Tesla covered full comprehensive insurance for my vehicle and accepted all liability. Otherwise, it's a gamble with my (and others') life.

That they seem to prioritize this sham service (Just call it Assisted Driving for honesty sake?) versus improving the cars or lowering prices, tells me enough not to look at Tesla for my next vehicle.

The recipient has to already own a Tesla, so the program doesn’t add a single car to the fleet. It’s built to mine the existing fleet for subscriptions. Tesla had sold about 8.9 million vehicles by the end of 2025, and only 1.74 million are paying for FSD, so there’s plenty of room. At Q3’s pace, we estimated 10 million subscriptions is still about eight years away.

So Tesla stopped paying for referrals that require selling a car, and started paying for the ones that move the number in Musk’s pay package. We think that tells you a lot about where the company’s priorities are right now.

If you’re driving an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #4919. Where Does Joe Burrow Live? Unpacking the Bengals Quarterback’s Real Estate Portfolio

He signed a record-breaking contract with the Cincinnati Bengals in 2023, but where does Joe Burrow live when he’s not on the field?

The NFL quarterback was born in Ames, Iowa, in 1996. His family moved to Ohio when he was eight, and he has resided mostly in the Buckeye State ever since. Burrow played for Ohio State University from 2015 to 2017, then transferred as a graduate student to Louisiana State University, where he won the Heisman Trophy shortly before graduating in December 2019. He was drafted into the NFL the following year by his hometown Bengals, with whom he inked a $36.1 million, four-year contract—plus a $23.9 million signing bonus. Three years later Burrow signed a $275 million five-year extension, making him the highest-paid player in NFL history at the time, according to ESPN.

It’s unsurprising, then, that Burrow has put down roots in the Buckeye State. Below, we’re examining the quarterback’s real estate portfolio.

In May 2020, a month after being selected first overall in the NFL Draft, Burrow revealed that he was spending the pandemic lockdown in his old bedroom at his parents’ house in The Plains, Ohio, just outside of Athens. Interior design apparently wasn’t at the forefront of his mind; the room still had his childhood Star Wars curtains and posters on the wall, though he had given the bed a king-size upgrade in high school, replacing the Star Wars comforter that once rounded out the room’s theme. According to The Sun, the 1997-built residence sits on a cul-de-sac and has four bedrooms and three bathrooms.

The Bengals play at Paycor Stadium, formerly known as Paul Brown Stadium.

In June 2020, Burrow spent $835,000 on his first house: a 2,900-square-foot Cincinnati dwelling just five miles from the Bengals’ home stadium. Located in the city’s historic Columbia-Tusculum area, the two-story property was built in 2014. Further details on the dwelling are scant—including whether or not Burrow still owns it. However, its location would make it a convenient place to stay during the NFL season.

#4917. BYD reveals a flashy new feature on its upcoming luxury EV

BYD is moving upmarket with its new flagship EV, featuring suicide doors, a gemstone grille, and possibly solid-state batteries.

Yangwang is like BMW’s Rolls-Royce and Mercedes-Maybach, sitting at the very top of BYD’s brand hierarchy.

The ultra-luxury brand offers BYD’s most high-end and technically advanced vehicles, including the U7 sedan, the U8, a hardcore off-road SUV, and the U9, an electric supercar.

The executive sedan is expected to debut around November 5. Prices are rumored to start in the 2 million yuan range, or about $300,000.

Yangwang’s current electric sedan, the U7, starts at 658,000 yuan ($98,000). The extended U8L, on the other hand, is the brand’s highest-priced mass-produced vehicle, starting at 1.45 million yuan ($217,000).

#4916. This 4-Week Workout Program Uses Just 1 Dumbbell to Help You Get Stronger

Build muscle and fry fat over this month-long workout plan.

YES, YOU CAN work every muscle in your body with one piece of gear. We love kettlebells, med balls, and resistance bands, but if we had to choose one piece of equipment, it would be a single dumbbell.

The game plan: Three times a week, you’ll grab the simplest tool in the gym and use it to build muscle and banish fat in a super-dense half-hour workout. It’s the most effective way to fry body fat, and it’s the perfect way to train for the holidays. It’s also exactly how Hollywood-based trainer Ben Bruno, C.F.S.C., guides time-strapped A-listers like Justin Timberlake and NBA star Klay Thompson. “They want quick, efficient workouts that hit everything,” Bruno says. “So we need to move fast.”

It doesn’t get more efficient than this light-speed, one-dumbbell workout.

Stand holding a dumbbell at your left shoulder, feet shoulder-width apart, knees slightly bent, glutes squeezed, and core tight. Keeping your chest level, straighten your arm to press the dumbbell overhead (b). Pause, then slowly lower the dumbbell back to your shoulder. That’s 1 rep; do 8 per arm.

Stand holding a dumbbell at your chest. Tighten your core and step back about three feet with your right leg. Keeping your chest up and the dumbbell high, bend your knees and lower your torso until your left thigh is parallel to the floor. Your right knee should be a few inches from the floor. Pause, then push off your right leg and stand back up. That’s 1 rep; do 10 per leg.

Eb says: Squeeze your glutes when you stand up. This will help you avoid lower-back pain when doing lunges.

Set a bench to a 45-degree angle and position yourself so your chest is against it; squeeze your abs and glutes. With your left arm out at shoulder height, hold a dumbbell in your right hand, arm hanging naturally. Squeeze your shoulder blades. This is the start. Keeping your shoulders and hips steady, row the dumbbell to your right hip. Pause, then return to the start.That’s 1 rep; do 10 per arm.

Eb says: This is more than a back move; your glutes and abs should be into it, too. Tighten them up so your torso doesn’t tip down to the side of the weight.

Lie on your back, holding a dumbbell in your left hand, arm straight, directly over your chest. Press your lower back into the floor and lift your legs slightly off the floor; lift your shoulder blades slightly, too. This is the start.Bend at the elbow and shoulder, lowering the dumbbell to your chest without losing torso position. Press back to the start. That’s 1 rep; do 8 per arm.

Eb says: Struggling with the position? Bend your knees to make it easier.

Lie on your back, knees bent and feet flat on the floor. Lift your right foot off the floor and tuck your right knee toward your chest. Tighten your core. Driving your left heel into the ground, push your lower back off the floor; squeeze your left glute as you do this. Pause, then return to the start. That’s 1 rep; do 10 per side.

Set up in pushup position, glutes and abs tight, right hand gripping a dumbbell. Squeeze your shoulder blades. Pull the dumbbell off the floor, rowing it toward your rib cage but keeping your hips and shoulders square. Pause. Return the dumbbell to the start. That’s 1 rep; do 8 per arm.

Eb says: Focus on flexing the oblique on the rowing side as hard as you canto keep your hips level.

This article was originally published in 2019.

Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S., is the fitness director of Men's Health and a certified trainer with more than 10 years of training experience. He's logged training time with NFL athletes and track athletes and his current training regimen includes weight training, HIIT conditioning, and yoga. Before joining Men's Health, he served as a sports columnist and tech columnist for the New York Daily News.

#4915. Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: Lung Cancer Can Look Like Anyone

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Picture someone with lung cancer. Now picture a 24-year-old soccer player in the prime of his life. A filmmaker in her early 40s with a camera in her hand, doing what she loves best. A busy mom of two, with a shoulder so sore she can’t pick up her newborn, yet struggling to get a diagnosis.

The gap between the picture in our heads and the people actually being diagnosed is costing time. And time is the one thing with lung cancer you cannot get back.

We know that lung cancer is the most common cancer worldwide and the leading cause of cancer death. And while lung cancer incidence rates are falling in many countries as fewer people smoke, lung cancer in people who have never smoked is on the rise globally, both as a proportion and in absolute numbers.

According to the CDC, as many as 20% of lung cancers diagnosed in the US are in people who have never smoked. Data suggests that non-smoking lung cancer is now among the most common causes of cancer-related deaths worldwide.

The numbers are stark. Lung cancer can affect anyone. And that includes the young – like Alex, one of the subjects of a powerful new short documentary commissioned by the charity EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK for their Our Lungs: Time to Breathe campaign.

Alex was a 24-year-old soccer player who died just weeks after receiving a diagnosis of Epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) positive lung cancer, following several misdiagnoses born of the fact that no one suspected lung cancer in one so young, fit, and with no smoking history. The film was made by filmmaker Sarah Li, who herself was diagnosed with Stage 4 EGFR+ lung cancer in April 2024, just a month after Alex died from the same mutation of the disease, and charts her journey with survivor’s guilt, what an earlier diagnosis may have meant for Alex, and what that fact means for her own future.

Ahead of Lung Cancer Awareness Month in November, I sat down with my friend, Gini Harrison, to talk about the film, her own journey, and why catching lung cancer sooner matters so much. Gini is a Professor of Psychology at The Open University, a trustee of EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK, and is living with stage 4 EGFR-positive lung cancer. She’s also the mom at the top of this piece, whose sore shoulder was initially attributed to breastfeeding posture.

This interview has been lightly edited for clarity and length.

Gini Harrison: I’d had my son, Michael, in February 2021 and was on maternity leave. My daughter, Emily, was five. I think the shoulder pain started around March, but that was it. No cough, no breathlessness… nothing that made me think about my lungs.

Initially, they gave me painkillers, which helped a bit. But the pain kept getting worse. Eventually, I started losing movement and strength in my arm, and it got to the point where I couldn’t pick up my son anymore.

This was all happening during COVID, so my appointments were over the telephone. Eventually, after months without being examined face-to-face, I paid to see a shoulder specialist. As soon as he touched my shoulder blade and I practically leaped out of the chair, he knew something wasn’t right. He ordered an MRI, which showed a tumor in my shoulder blade and another in my upper right lung. In December 2021, I was diagnosed with stage IV lung cancer.

Gini Harrison: I wasn’t thinking, “This must be cancer.” I was just thinking, “Why isn’t this getting better?”

And I think that distinction matters. Patients shouldn’t have to identify their own disease before they can get help. You should be able to say that something is wrong and be listened to.

Gini Harrison: It was devastating. So many of my first questions were about the children: “How are we going to tell them?” “Am I going to be here to see them grow up?” “Will they remember me?” Suddenly, there was a question mark over a future I’d taken for granted.

I was also completely shocked by the type of cancer. If someone had said breast cancer, I don’t think I would have been anywhere near as surprised. But lung cancer?! I’d always associated it with smoking, and I’d never smoked.

I hadn’t imagined that this particular cancer could ever happen to me. So yes, I had some assumptions that I now challenge. I understand how deeply rooted they are because I had them too.

Gini Harrison: People are often shocked, and one of the first questions is whether I smoked.

And they often look healthy. That doesn’t mean everything is easy, or that there aren’t side effects. It just means you can’t tell what someone is dealing with by looking at them.

I think we’ve given lung cancer such a narrow public image that people outside it can become almost invisible.

Gini Harrison: Oh, yes! Once, I spoke to a doctor about a breathing problem after radiotherapy. He asked, “How much did you smoke?” When I said I’d never smoked, he replied, “Well, how did you get lung cancer then?”

On another occasion, I went to a hospice for help managing side effects, and someone there asked, “How are you still alive?” That’s quite a thing to hear when you’ve come looking for support!

When people ask about smoking, I try to explain that I’ve never smoked and I’m certainly not alone. Never smoking doesn’t make you immune. But I’m also very clear that nobody deserves lung cancer, whether they’ve smoked or not. We shouldn’t be creating a hierarchy of who deserves compassion.

Smoking history matters medically. But in a personal conversation, “How are you doing?” is a much kinder place to start.

Gini Harrison: Lung cancer isn’t one single disease. Two people can both have lung cancer, but different changes in their cells may be driving it.

Genes contain the instructions that tell our cells how to work, including when to grow and divide. Changes in those genes (called mutations) can cause cells to keep dividing when they shouldn’t, forming a tumor. In my case, there’s a mutation in a gene called EGFR.

EGFR-positive lung cancer is a type of non-small cell lung cancer caused by a genetic mutation in the EGFR gene, which keeps the EGFR receptor switched “on,” leading to uncontrolled cell growth and enhanced cancer cell survival.

Biomarker testing looks for these changes, as well as other features of the cancer that can guide treatment. If we know what’s helping the cancer grow, there may be a medication that specifically targets it.

The word “genetic” can sound scary, particularly when you have children. But these changes usually develop in cancer cells over a person’s lifetime. They aren’t necessarily inherited.

Gini Harrison: Please get it checked. And if it persists, worsens or doesn’t improve with treatment, go back. Explain what’s changed and how it’s affecting your life. Ask, “What happens next if this doesn’t get better?”

The symptoms don’t always look like lung symptoms. Mine was shoulder pain.

Most symptoms will turn out to be something other than cancer. But when a symptom continues to worsen, the original explanation needs to be reconsidered. Lower risk doesn’t mean no risk.

And I don’t want all the responsibility to fall on patients to keep pushing. Reassurance should come with a next step.

Gini Harrison: Once I had recovered from my initial treatment, I realized that I wanted to help others in my situation, so I became a trustee of the charity EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK. Through this work, we produced a film, Our Lungs: Time to Breathe. At the heart of it, it challenges the perception of what someone with lung cancer looks like.

The hardest part is how close to home it feels. Alex was so young, and that worked against him. Watching his family talk about what happened is heartbreaking. It’s just so unfair.

Gini Harrison: Sarah’s story shows life continuing alongside lung cancer. She’s a filmmaker with ambitions, people she loves, and things she wants to do.

Through Alex’s family, you see the enormity of the loss. I hope people feel that, rather than simply hearing another statistic.

Gini Harrison: For me, the biggest thing is that my children are growing up and I’m here to see it. Michael is now five (the age Emily was when I was diagnosed), and Emily is about to turn ten. I can’t tell you how grateful I am to be here with them.

It wasn’t about big bucket-list things. It was about being here every day. Seeing Michael start school. Seeing Emily reach double figures. Those milestones are enormous when you’ve wondered whether you’d get to see them.

After chemotherapy and radiotherapy, I’ve had no evidence of disease since May 2022, and my scans remain stable. But a big change has been seeing the progress in available treatment. It gives me more hope about what might be available if I need treatment again.

The public image of lung cancer still needs to catch up. We need better recognition of symptoms, access to appropriate testing and treatment, and support for the whole person. As a psychologist as well as a patient, I’m particularly aware that psychological well-being and family life need attention alongside the cancer itself.

Gini Harrison: I’d love for people to share the film and help get it in front of those who wouldn’t normally think lung cancer has anything to do with them. And take something from it into everyday life: book the appointment you’ve been putting off, ask your doctor about testing, or respond with kindness when someone tells you about their diagnosis.

Just being willing to rethink your assumptions could make a difference.

Gini Harrison: “You don’t have to put a brave face on with me. How are you really doing?”

When you look well, or your scans are good, people can assume you’re “fine now”. But I’m not sure the fear ever completely goes away. Every scan is terrifying. It feels like there’s a sword of Damocles hanging over my head, and part of me is waiting for the bad scan. I also still have lasting side effects from treatment, and probably always will.

My life has fundamentally changed. Some of that has brought good things, but it doesn’t make the difficult parts disappear. It helps when someone gives you room to be honest about both, without expecting you to reassure them or turn everything into a positive story.

Gini and I have been friends for nearly 30 years, and, while she’d probably cringe to hear me say it, I remain in awe of the way she approaches life with cancer. I am so grateful for her sharing her experiences with me for this column. What Gini asks of us is simple: look again, ask the question, listen, and act on your instincts. Our Lungs: Time to Breathe is where that starts for her. You can learn more about the Our Lungs: Time to Breathe campaign and the wider work of EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK by clicking the links. — Robin Hough

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#4914. Honda has dominated motorcycles for decades. Electric bikes are changing that

Honda has spent decades building one of the most dominant positions in the global motorcycle industry, selling millions of gasoline-powered two-wheelers each year. But in markets where electric motorcycles and scooters are becoming increasingly accessible, that dominance is starting to face a serious challenge.

Vietnam offers a particularly interesting case study, where rapidly growing electric motorcycle sales are beginning to reshape a market long dominated by Honda.

According to newly released nationwide sales figures, Vietnam saw more than 3 million motorcycle and scooter sales during the first nine months of 2026, representing 25.9% growth over the same period last year.

But the real story is in the electric segment, where sales surged 90.5% to reach 973,247 units. That means electric two-wheelers now account for approximately 32% of all new motorcycle and scooter sales in the country.

And the biggest beneficiary isn’t Honda. It’s actually Vietnamese electric vehicle manufacturer VinFast.

While Honda remains Vietnam’s largest motorcycle manufacturer overall, with sales growing 8.4% this year, VinFast has surged into second place with a staggering 152.1% increase in sales. Yamaha now sits in third place, followed by electric two-wheeler manufacturer Yadea in fourth.

Of the top four players, half are electric, and it’s precisely that half that is experiencing meteoric sales growth.

It’s an impressive reshuffling of a market that has traditionally been dominated by Japanese gasoline motorcycle manufacturers.

Vietnam’s transition hasn’t happened in a vacuum, either. Government efforts to reduce urban air pollution, including plans to restrict gasoline-powered motorcycles in parts of Hanoi, have helped create an increasingly favorable environment for electric alternatives.

Too bad for Honda but I'm thrilled to read about increasing sales of electric motorcycles. I don't live in or near Vietnam but the pollution from small gasoline engines affects everyone where the wind blows it.

And with companies like VinFast offering an expanding range of electric scooters and motorcycles, riders now have more practical alternatives to gasoline-powered models than ever before.

To be clear, Honda isn’t exactly struggling. Its motorcycle sales are still growing, and the Japanese giant remains the undisputed market leader in Vietnam. But its overall share of the market is shrinking, and its relatively modest growth compared to the exploding electric segment suggests that the market’s center of gravity is beginning to shift.

Vietnam may be just a single example, but it’s an important one of what can happen when electric two-wheelers become viable mainstream alternatives to gasoline motorcycles.

Honda has spent decades perfecting the gasoline motorcycle. The question now is how quickly it can adapt to a market that is increasingly interested in something else.

#4913. The Six Bells Madewell Collection 2026

Whimsical design flourishes, cozy interiors, and close proximity to upstate antiquing all make The Six Bells Countryside Inn one of the best weekend escapes in the Empire State. Now, thanks to Madewell, you never have to check out. Launching today, the two brands have joined forces on a 28-piece holiday capsule of heirloom-worthy wardrobe and home essentials inspired by The Six Bells’ vintage aesthetic. Pieces specifically pay homage to the hotel’s interiors through references to archival patterns and wallpaper prints found throughout the Inn’s rooms and communal spaces.

“We fell in love with [The Six Bells’] distinct, whimsical world—the mix of patterns, the nostalgic sentiment, and the personality in every detail,” says Madewell creative director Steven Cateron. During initial planning for the clothing brand’s holiday collection, the Rosendale, New York-based hotel became an unexpected source of inspiration for Cateron and his team because of the shared appreciation for heritage and storytelling. “Each piece has intention, whether it’s to wear day-to-day or for a celebration, a gifting moment for a loved one or hostess, or pajama pieces that lean into cozy season,” he says.

Sock slippers from the collaboration and Madewell’s classic denim make a perfect pairing.

Founded by Audrey Gelman in 2025, who designed the hotel in collaboration with interiors firm GrecoDeco, the Countryside Inn has quickly risen in popularity—even earning a spot in AD’s list of Great Design Hotels last year. “This project is so rooted in a shared love of the decorative arts and global human history,” designer Adam Greco previously told AD. Design-wise, maximalism dominates the interiors. Picture: rooms wrapped in fretwork wallpaper and deep cinnabar paint, with pistachio window shades and striped seating.

A cheeky message makes this classic needlepoint pillow pop.

At the same time, the collaboration was an opportunity to venture into Madwell’s less-explored categories—specifically the home space. The collection’s interior goods are a practice in curated nostalgia: There are holiday ornaments you’ll reach for year after year, scented candles and soaps to integrate into your daily winter routine, and cozy pajamas inspired by vintage sleep silhouettes. “If it’s something we don’t currently offer, we want to work with people that do the category best,” says Cateron. “The Six Bells team were the perfect partners to bring this collection’s home accessories to life.”

Still, versatility isn’t lost in the assortment. In designing the clothing, Cateron made sure to include “pieces to wear upstate, in the city, or for day-to-day celebrations.” Classic denim jackets are neutral staples that you can reach for any day of the week, while fair-isle wool knits are seasonally appropriate while still feeling adaptable enough to wear after the holidays. “There’s a real point of view behind every detail [at the Countryside Inn], from the interiors to collected vintage pieces,” says Cateron. “This collection captures that sensibility in a way that felt authentic to both brands.”

The collection is available to shop on Madewell.com and in Madewell stores starting today, November 12.

“There’s so much character in every detail at The Six Bells, and we wanted to bring that sensibility into each piece,” says Madewell creative director Steven Cateron.
