#4911. How to Find Out If You Got the Measles Vaccine as a Kid

The US may lose its measles elimination status after multiple outbreaks in the past two years.

THE US is likely to lose its measles elimination status this week when the Pan-American Health Organization, part of the public health arm of the United Nations, meets to review data from the multiple outbreaks that have occurred nationwide in the past two years.As of early November, there are 5,182 confirmed measles cases in 47 states. This is more than double of cases in 2025—the CDC reported 2,289 measles cases in 45 states. And a far cry from when measles were practically eliminated in the US with a record low of 86 cases two decades ago.

Of all the US cases this year, 96 percent come from people who are unvaccinated. Since most of us got the MMR (measles, mumps, and rubella) vaccine as a kid, it wouldn't hurt to double-check that you're protected against one of the most contagious infectious diseases in the world.

Now, there's no central repository of childhood vaccine records in the United States, says Brian Labus, PhD, MPH, assistant professor of epidemiology and biostatistics at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas. So you'd need to find your copy of your vaccination records or get them from your pediatrician's office—which may or may not still be operating. There are a few other places to do that detective work (more below).

In lieu of that, some people are getting blood tests to check if they have antibodies for some of the diseases children are vaccinated for, but there are pros and cons with those. Here's what to know about making sure you're protected against measles and whether you got other vaccinations on the childhood immunization schedule.

IF YOU WERE born before 1957, it’s assumed that you were exposed to measles as a child and don’t need to be vaccinated, Labus says. If you’re around 45 or older, you were likely exposed to chicken pox and don’t need that vaccine, adds Thomas Russo, MD, chief of the division of infectious diseases at the University at Buffalo. There aren’t specific age cutoffs for other diseases, though.

But there is a blood test, called an antibody serology test, that can tell you if you have antibodies against certain diseases, including measles, mumps, chicken pox, hepatitis, tetanus, HIV, mononucleosis, and others.

These blood tests can suggest whether you’ve been previously infected with these diseases or vaccinated against them, Dr. Russo says. “The presence of antibodies indicates that your body mounted an immune response against a pathogen either due to a natural exposure or to a vaccination,” Labus explains. “It can also give you an idea about the level of protection you have in your body.”

While that generally means that you’re immune, it’s also not a guarantee, says Georges Benjamin, MD, executive director of the American Public Health Association. Also, antibodies decline over time.

Starting with an antibody test to determine your vaccination status might make sense for some people, but Labus says there are some practicalities to consider.

For one, the tests can be expensive, costing around $250, Dr. Benjamin says. Your insurance may or may not cover them.

It can also take several days to get the results. Labus says if you’re using the test to determine if you need a vaccine quickly, you’re potentially not protected during that wait. “While this doesn't matter for routine situations, it could be the difference between getting measles or avoiding it if you live in a community experiencing an outbreak,” he says.

If you're not sure you were vaccinated, Labus and the other doctors we spoke to suggest just getting the MMR, polio, DTap (diphtheria, tetanus, and acellular pertussis), and other shots just to be safe (more about that below).

But if you're not sure you want to get jabbed, your pediatrician retired long ago, and you have a moment to invest in detective work on your records, here's where to look:

• Old records you or your parents have at home, like a baby book.

• Any schools you attended. Many states require certain vaccines before you start kindergarten, including MMR, polio, DTap, and varicella, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. If you were in the military, you would have been required to get certain vaccines.

• The health department where you grew up. Some state health departments keep immunization records, Dr. Benjamin says. But whether yours are there may depend on your age. “State immunization registries are a relatively new development, and those systems were not likely in place when you were young if you are more than 25 or 30 years old,” Labus says.

TALK TO YOUR doctor about your concerns and uncertainty about your childhood vaccination status and level of protection against diseases, and they can suggest whether just getting the vaccine for the first time or a booster is appropriate, Labus says. “Most vaccines can be given to adults without any problems.”

Getting the shot is usually cheaper and faster than an antibody test, he adds. Dr. Benjamin points out that boosters are a good idea to consider especially if you have chronic diseases, immune system dysfunction, or work in the healthcare industry. Also consider it if you plan to travel to parts of the world with higher instances of diseases like measles, such as countries in the Middle East, Dr. Russo adds.

And by the way, even if you did get all your vaccines as a child, you still need to get a DTap or Tdap booster every 10 years as an adult for protection against diphtheria and tetanus, Dr. Russo says.

For the other childhood vaccines, “receiving an extra dose is going to boost your antibody levels even if you have previously been vaccinated, which is a good thing,” Labus explains. Dr. Russo agrees: “The path of least resistance is when in doubt, there’s no harm in getting a booster shot.”

#4910. I imported a $2,000 electric truck from China. Five years later, here’s the truth

Five years ago, I did something that seemed pretty ridiculous at the time. I bought a $2,000 electric mini-truck from China, had it shipped across the oceans to the US, and put it to work on my parents’ property in Florida.

The little white mini electric truck became something of an internet sensation, with my original unboxing and testing video quickly racking up over 10 million views, plus several million more from the follow-up videos.

It also attracted plenty of skeptics who were convinced that a $2,000 Chinese electric truck would fall apart within months, rust into oblivion, or otherwise prove to be a spectacular waste of money. “Good thing it has a bed,” was a common refrain. “So you can collect all the pieces that fall off along the way.”

I gave an update after the first year, showing that the truck was doing great. But to be fair, one year doesn’t tell us much.

Well now it’s been over five years, and I recently returned to Florida to see how the little truck has held up after five trips around the sun on my parents’ homestead. And I’ve got to say, the results are pretty surprising.

So here’s the truth about how the truck is doing five years later. Oh, and if you want to see it in action, you can check out my update video here. Trust me, you’ll want to.

First, it’s important to understand that this truck hasn’t spent five years sitting in a garage as some kind of novelty vehicle. It’s been a genuine workhorse on my parents’ 10-acre homestead.

Over the years, we’ve used it to haul everything from mulch and sand to lumber, tree branches, logs, yard waste, and everything in between.

Its hydraulic dump bed makes it particularly useful for moving bulk materials around the property, like hauling mulch and then dumping it in their raised planting beds.

My dad even uses it as a mobile irrigation truck during Florida’s dry season. I built him a solar-powered pump system that fills a water bladder in the truck’s bed with water from his pond, allowing him to drive around watering his trees and plants.

By now, it’s been involved in practically every major landscaping and construction project my parents have tackled.

We’ve also made some improvements over the years. Early on, my dad and I replaced the ridiculously stiff factory suspension springs with softer ones, making the ride considerably more comfortable. We added a custom tow hitch and swapped the original street tires for aggressive off-road tires that work much better on the property’s sandy and muddy terrain.

The suspension upgrade did reduce how much weight we can comfortably carry, though. Load more than around 500 pounds (220 kg) of sand or other bulk materials into the bed, and the rear end starts squatting enough that our homemade tow hitch threatens to touch grass.

But for most of the work we do, it’s been an excellent little utility vehicle.

And before anyone asks, no, this isn’t a street-legal truck. It was never really designed for American roads, and we use it almost exclusively on private property. Sure, it’s hit the pavement a few times since they live out in the boonies and its normal to see tractors and other non-street-legal vehicles on country roads, but it’s not the most ideal vehicle for long distance transport at 25 mph (40 km/h).

Here’s the part that surprised me most: Almost everything still works.

The electric windows still zip up and down with the speed of a guillotine. The electric door locks work. The headlights work. The backup camera works. Even the air conditioning still blows impressively cold air.

I actually checked that last one with a thermal camera during my latest visit, and the air coming out of the vents was around 55°F (12°C). Not bad for a five-year-old air conditioning system in a $2,000 truck!

The windshield wiper still operates, but its range of motion has decreased significantly. It now stops around the middle of the driver’s field of vision, which isn’t ideal when Florida’s famous afternoon thunderstorms roll through.

The rearview mirror has also developed an annoying habit of falling off. We’ve glued it back on multiple times, and it looks like another round of adhesive surgery is coming soon.

Inside, there’s some minor upholstery damage on the driver’s seat, including a few small tears and stressed seams in the cheap faux-leather material that you can see in the video.

The passenger seat, which sees much less use, still looks nearly new. In another few years, I might just swap the two seats to get another half decade or so out of them before we need some upholstery repair.

One decorative plastic panel around the driver’s door handle has popped off, though I still have the piece and could probably snap it back into place with a bit of effort. It’s just aesthetic though, so it’s been a low-priority repair.

And that’s pretty much the extent of the mechanical and electrical problems we’ve encountered.

There haven’t been any major drivetrain failures, the hydraulic dump bed still works, and the truck continues to start up and drive just as reliably as it did when it was new.

It still gets used for lots of bulk jobs, material hauling, and sometimes just random transportation around the property.

One of the biggest predictions from critics five years ago was that this cheap Chinese truck would quickly dissolve into a pile of rust.

To be fair, there is some rust. But nowhere near as much as you might expect.

Most of the corrosion is concentrated in areas where the paint has been scratched or chipped, especially around the tailgate and cargo bed.

The rustiest spot is definitely the top lip of the tailgate, where we usually have long objects hanging out of the bed, such as shovels, ladders, lumber, etc. The paint has worn through on that edge, and the rust has inevitably started in those few spots.

The truck is stored outdoors under a lean-to roof that I built between two shipping containers, but we have a skid-steer in front of it and so the rear of the truck sticks out and gets rained on several times a week (or several times a day during the summer).

We applied a paint-on bed liner during the truck’s first year, which helped protect the metal for quite a while.

After four years of hauling dirt, rocks, branches, and other abrasive materials though, that coating has started wearing through in a few places.

Every time I’m shoveling mulch out of the bed and I hear that scrape of metal on metal, I cringe just a bit before remembering that the truck cost me $2,000 and doesn’t owe me anything at this point.

Speaking of being parked outdoors with the rear hanging out for the world (and the UV) to see, there’s clearly an effect on the truck’s wear.

You can absolutely see the difference in the paint and plastic components. The front headlight lenses are still crystal clear, while the rear taillight lenses have become noticeably cloudy from UV exposure.

Despite that, the truck’s basic structure remains solid, and the rust is mostly cosmetic and limited to the high-impact areas that tend to get beaten up repeatedly, as you’d expect in any self-respecting work truck.

Considering that Florida combines high humidity, frequent rain, and intense sunshine, I’d call that a pretty impressive result for a vehicle that cost less than many electric bicycles.

Since the truck lives at my parents’ property, my dad has actually spent far more time using it than I have.

And when I asked him how he felt about the purchase after five years, he was surprisingly enthusiastic.

He described the truck as an incredibly functional piece of equipment that has become an essential part of maintaining the property.

In fact, he pointed out that the truck has been involved in virtually every significant project they’ve completed over the past five years.

It isn’t the only electric utility vehicle on the property anymore. My parents also have a Kandi electric UTV, which has taken over some of the smaller daily chores thanks to its better suspension and more convenient dump bed operation (the bed also lowers under power) which helps my dad lift the trash and recycling bins and drive them down the long private road to the street on pickup day.

But when it’s time to haul bulky loads, long branches, or large quantities of dirt and mulch, the Changli is still the vehicle of choice.

My dad also pointed out something that tends to get lost in discussions about cheap Chinese EVs: the operating costs have been almost nonexistent.

A full charge costs a little over a dollar in electricity, and the truck has required remarkably little maintenance over the years.

Even after five years, he says it still puts a smile on his face every time he drives it.

Yes, the truck cost me $2,000. Or technically, the price was $2,080.00, and that’s what I paid to the factory. At that point, it was mine.

Of course, it was still in China though, and so I had to pay an international freight company to have it sent on a cargo ship to the US (nearly $2,000 by itself, if I recall correctly).

Then I had to pay the US Treasury a bunch of tariffs to help make sure American automotive companies don’t have to compete with these types of vehicles.

And I also had to hire a customs broker to handle the import documentation for me, etc.

So there were several thousand dollars of additional fees on top of it, but I still think it was worth it based on the incredible amount of use it has given us over the years.

Of course, there’s an important caveat here for anyone considering getting a vehicle like this.

Remember, this is a very basic, low-speed electric utility vehicle without the safety equipment, crash protection, or regulatory approvals you’d expect from a modern full-size pickup truck.

It’s not something I’d recommend as a replacement for a road-going vehicle.

This is a work truck for us. It moves material, tows trailers and boats, carries the trash cans a quarter mile to the street each week, etc. In that way, as a private-property work truck, it’s been fantastic.

But even though I’m calling it simple, that doesn’t mean it wasn’t made pretty well. The truck has hauled countless loads, survived five years of rough Florida weather, and required very little maintenance. Even its cheap interior and basic electronics have held up better than I expected.

I also have to give some credit to the modifications we made early on, especially the suspension and tire upgrades, which turned a quirky little imported vehicle into something much more practical for off-road work.

The biggest unknown is how much longer it will last. Unfortunately, the truck doesn’t have a proper odometer, so I can’t even tell you how many miles we’ve accumulated over five years of driving around the property.

I also can’t tell you anything about battery degradation, since we don’t know how far it has driven (due to the lack of odometer), and the fact that even on 10 acres, you just can’t get that far from an outlet. We generally charge the 6 kWh battery every couple weeks just to keep it topped up, but range was never an issue and hasn’t become one, at least not for our use case. If you needed to drive this thing to the next town over each day, it could be another story.

Nothing like being forced to drive massive four-door trucks that weigh as much as four cars because your country is protecting monopolist manufacturers and only allows their land yachts for sale.

Basically, there’s no indication that the mini-truck is nearing the end of its useful life.

My dad thinks it could easily serve another five or even ten years, and after seeing how well it’s survived its first five, I’m inclined to agree that there’s plenty of life left in it.

When I first bought this thing, plenty of people told me it was a terrible idea. Five years later, with the truck still hauling dirt, carrying branches, and generally earning its keep, I’m feeling pretty good about that decision. It’s done a lot more in its five years than many “real” pickup trucks do in their entire lives.

Not bad for a funny little electric truck from China.

#4908. Wellness Apps, Medically Tailored Meals Help Slow Weight Gain After GLP-1s

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Weight regain is common after stopping GLP-1 medications such as semaglutide (Wegovy, Ozempic) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro, Zepbound).

In fact, previous research has shown that most people regain at least half of their lost weight within 1 year of stopping treatment, with weight regain often accelerating during the first 3–4 months.

However, a new study suggests that structured support, including a weight management app or medically tailored meals, may help slow weight regain after discontinuing GLP-1 medications.

The small study, published October 7 in Obesity Science & Practice, found that participants who received either intervention regained significantly less weight over four months than those receiving usual care.

The study included 39 adults with an average age of 50, an average weight of 189 pounds, and an average body mass index (BMI) of 31.4.

All participants had lost more than 10% of their body weight while taking GLP-1 medications and had stopped treatment within the previous 30 days.

All participants also completed questionnaires at the beginning and end of the study and used Bluetooth scales to track their weight at home throughout the four-month period.

Researchers randomly assigned participants to one of the following three groups:

Participants continued to receive care from their healthcare providers, which may include nutrition, exercise, and lifestyle counseling from a clinician or registered dietitian. The type and amount of support varied depending on the provider’s recommendations and available resources. The researchers did not provide any additional intervention.

Participants received a subscription to Noom, an app that uses behavioral strategies to support weight loss and maintenance. These include tracking food intake, exercise, and weight, as well as educational lessons on the psychological, physiological, social, and environmental factors that influence eating behaviors.

The app also provides tools to help users recognize and manage triggers that may lead to overeating. Participants were encouraged to use the app for at least 15 minutes daily, including completing lessons, logging food, and tracking their weight.

These are typically nutrient-dense meals developed by registered dietitians to meet the nutritional needs of people with specific health conditions, such as chronic kidney disease or uncontrolled type 2 diabetes.

For this study, participants in this group selected 10 portion-controlled, “weight wellness” meals each week from more than 70 options, which were delivered to their homes.

After four months, participants who received either medically tailored meals or access to the digital weight management app experienced significantly less weight regain than those receiving usual care.

The estimated weight regain was 10.75% of body weight in the usual-care group, compared with 3.6% in the Noom group and 3.58% in the medically tailored meals group.

Although participants in all three groups regained weight, the findings suggest that structured nutritional or behavioral support may help limit weight regain after stopping GLP-1 medications.

“These findings highlight the benefits of ongoing support for patients on a weight loss journey,” said Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA. Ali wasn’t involved in the study.

Denise Pate, MD, a board certified internist with Medical Offices of Manhattan who was also not involved in the study, told Healthline that the findings are significant “because they show that obesity management is multifactorial, not only medication, but the combination of types of meals and behavioral change all play a role.”

However, because this was a small, four-month study, larger, longer-term trials are needed to determine whether these approaches can help people maintain weight loss over time after stopping GLP-1 medications.

Ali noted that research suggests GLP-1 medications are most effective for weight management when taken long-term.

“Obesity needs to be recognized and treated as a chronic condition, similar to diabetes or hypertension,” he said.

Pate agreed, emphasizing that obesity is not always recognized as a chronic condition that may require ongoing treatment.

“I feel that the medical community, insurance, and the population as a whole have yet to fully accept obesity as a chronic medical condition, which it most definitely is for a large part of patients. For this reason, I think patients should stay on these medications long term if needed,” she said.

“GLP-1 medications are highly effective tools for transitioning to a healthier diet and lifestyle, which for most people may require long-term use,” Ali added.

Pate explained that one of the goals of GLP-1 medications is to help people eat smaller portions by recognizing when they feel full and stopping when they do.

“I tell my patients from the start, this is the goal,” she said, adding that she hopes patients will retain those habits after stopping treatment.

Ali recommended maintaining a balanced diet rich in protein and vegetables while limiting added sugars and refined carbohydrates.

He added that tracking apps and regular follow-up appointments with a healthcare professional or registered dietitian may also help people maintain their progress.

“Patients need to understand that the GLP medication is not a miracle drug, although with the dramatic weight loss, it can be perceived as such. It is only a tool to be used in conjunction with an exercise routine as well as a healthy diet,” Pate said.

#4907. This Guy Lost 54 Pounds and Got Absolutely Shredded in 6 Months

At 46, Austin Cowburn wanted to overcome injuries and get back to feeling his best. Here’s how he did it.

At age 46, Austin Cowburn felt his training was "directionless," and he started putting on weight. This former runner and marathoner wanted to overcome injuries and get back to feeling his best. Here’s how he did it.

RUNNING HAS ALWAYS been my go-to exercise; as a younger man I joined several running clubs, and even finished as even finished pretty high up in the 1995 New York Marathon. I spent weeknights and Saturday mornings on the track or in a swimming pool. Cardio is familiar to me, and as I grew up, I stuck with it. Looking back, I realize means I didn’t have the understanding and technique to really benefit from weight training.

After a couple of years of directionless training, plus two injuries and rehab, and a poor diet, I put on weight. That inflamed other, niggling injuries, which made me feel like I just wasn’t at my best. I felt sluggish; my favorite clothes didn’t fit as well, and the new ones seemed to be smaller than I expected. I wanted to get back into marathons, but to do that I need to get fitter, stronger, and healthier. So, I approached Ultimate Performance Manchester for help.

We started with fundamental stuff: monitoring and being consistent with my diet. I went from eating a lot of rich, calorie-laden food, eating it at all times of the day, to something much more sustainable. I’d enjoy a blended berry smoothie in the morning with protein, and three scrambled eggs with spinach in mid-morning. Mid-afternoon and evening, I’d eat lean-meat chili, mainly turkey mince, with lots of vegetables thrown in, including peppers and tomatoes, as well as rice. I also enjoyed a low-fat, high-protein yogurt in the evening mixed with banana, honey, or chia seeds. On the weekend, I could indulge in a nice steak.

We often forget how big an impact diet can have on mood, energy levels, and your ability to train hard and get the best from yourself. Changing what I ate really reminded me of the importance of a foundational diet.

I trained three times a week in the gym, usually from 8 a.m. to 9 a.m. I loved getting it done first thing in the morning, which really set me up for the day. I would also run four times a week. I really got my love for running back during this time: my distances increased, and I had no injuries. I didn’t even have aches and pains afterward. Training, losing weight, and fueling my body correctly really helped me regain my fluidity of motion.

It also helped with my energy levels and sleep. I felt mentally sharper, more productive and more creative. People told me that my skin was glowing. Everyone was extremely positive and kind in and out of the gym, complimentary, and really encouraging. My family was very proud and surprised with the results, and I felt like the very best version of myself.

In six months, I lost 24.6 kg (54 pounds), bringing my body fat percentage from 28.2% to 11.6%. I dropped from an extra-large to a medium. I’ve continued training, and now I’m eating more, and building muscle mass. I’m looking to sustain my gains and also increase the number of exercises I have in my toolbox.

It’s great to be able to say that more, better training has helped me avoid injuries. The more I trained, the more I wanted to, and my stamina has gone through the roof. I’m happier, more relaxed, and more motivated; I just feel better about myself.

I think anyone can do this, and if you’re on the fence about it, just go for it. Set clear goals and look for help, whether that’s a professional trainer or just a friend who’ll hold you accountable. You don’t have to change all at once; I’d recommend not putting too much pressure on yourself from session to session. Enjoy your exercises; make sure you rest and refuel correctly.

#4905. Nickey Kehoe’s New Collection With The Lacquer Company Takes Its Cues From Beloved Antiques

In 2024, AD heralded the return of lacquer. In our April 2026 issue, cover star Athena Calderone was pictured leaning over an enormous burgundy-lacquered kitchen island, and in May, cover star Mark Grattan showed off his parrot green lacquer walls. Today’s collaboration between AD100 design firm Nickey Kehoe and The Lacquer Company brings the glossy finish down to a slightly more manageable scale, applying it to coffee tables, desks, trays, and tissue boxes. Nickey Kehoe founders Todd Nickey and Amy Kehoe designed the 30-piece collection with The Lacquer Company founder James Lowther, drawing on antique furniture and objects the three have collected over the years.

The Bonne Nuit nightstand in Cassis staged among various Nickey Kehoe textiles and an upholstered headboard.

The Florin Tray in Carreaux & Marine Blue is styled with all small pieces from the Nickey Kehoe collection.

Many of the pieces, as is often true of Nickey Kehoe’s in-house catalog, draw on the founders’ personal vintage collections. The Pastis side table is based on a bamboo folding table that belongs to Nickey. The Hugo desk is a Louis XVI–style writing desk, with tapered, fluted legs, brass details, and three soft-close drawers in a bright red lacquer. The colorways sound like a very civilized, French late lunch: cassis and lettuce green, carreaux and marine blue, khaki and chocolate brown. The Jules catchall tray comes with a matching tissue box cover for the powder room.

Lacquer trays, tissue boxes, side tables, and the collection’s coffee table are staged together here to show the full line.

The Pastis side table references a vintage style with the lacquer and bamboo combination reminiscent of Regency Chinoiserie.

Lowther bought his first piece of lacquer, a burnt-orange betel box, while traveling in Burma in 1999, and it’s still one of his favorite things. He later found the craftspeople he was looking for in Saigon, and every piece from The Lacquer Company is still made there in Sơn Mài lacquer, a Vietnamese art form that goes back centuries. The process is lengthy: Wood is first sealed with a resin called Sinh. It’s then coated repeatedly with natural lacquer mixed with ground mountain rock, alluvial soil, and cashew nut shell oil. Each coat is wet-sanded until it’s smooth before the next coat is applied. “There is no prescribed drying time, no set number of layers,” according to the company. That makes the eight- to 12-week lead time on larger pieces easier to forgive.

Nickey Kehoe’s goal is for its furniture to feel “as if it was acquired, not purchased.” With these pieces so rooted in vintage styles and techniques, they make good on their promise.

The collection is available to shop on NickeyKehoe.com.

#4902. Michael Strahan Shares the Words That Helped His Daughter Through Brain Cancer Treatment

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

For years, Michael Strahan appeared in commercials for St. Jude Children’s Research Hospital, delivering a message he never imagined would become so personal: “No parent should have to hear that their child has cancer.”

But in 2023, the beloved TV host and former NFL player faced that reality when his daughter, Isabella, was diagnosed with brain cancer at 19 years old.

“[That] line that I used to deliver, usually at the end of the commercial…here I am actually filling that line in my life in real time,” Strahan told Healthline.

Strahan believes his longtime connection with St. Jude was more than a coincidence. After years of advocating for families affected by childhood cancer, he found himself on the other side of that experience.

“When I needed that family…they were there for me,” he said. “I’m just appreciative, and I think things come back to you; give out good, good comes back.”

In October 2023, Isabella began experiencing vomiting, headaches, and balance issues during her freshman year at the University of Southern California (USC). Tests revealed that she had medulloblastoma, a fast-growing cancerous brain tumor located in the cerebellum. She underwent emergency brain surgery, radiation therapy, and four rounds of chemotherapy.

While medulloblastoma typically occurs in children between the ages of 3 and 9, it can also affect young adults like Isabella.

“[The] biology in adolescents and young adults can differ meaningfully from pediatric cases, requiring specialized expertise at centers of excellence for this disease,” Arnab Chakravarti, MD, director of the Brain Tumor Program at The Ohio State University, told Healthline.

Medulloblastoma is sometimes confused with glioblastoma, the most common type of malignant primary brain tumor in adults. Glioblastoma is particularly aggressive, with a median overall survival of just 15 months and a 5-year survival rate of less than 10%.

However, Chakravarti explained that medulloblastoma has a significantly higher cure rate than glioblastoma.

“[This is] a point of genuine hope that deserves to be communicated clearly,” he said.

Radiation therapy is a cornerstone of medulloblastoma treatment. Isabella received proton therapy, which uses precisely targeted radiation to treat tumors while limiting exposure to surrounding healthy tissue.

“For medulloblastoma, this physical precision is transformative,” Chakravarti said. “Proton craniospinal irradiation dramatically reduces [collateral] exposure — preserving cochlear function and hearing, reducing cardiovascular risk, protecting the thyroid, and most critically, sparing developing brain tissue in ways that translate into measurably better neurocognitive outcomes years after treatment.”

He added that multiple prospective studies have shown that children and young adults treated with proton craniospinal irradiation experience less cognitive decline and better quality of life compared to those treated with conventional radiation.

Today, Isabella is back at USC, embracing the everyday routines she once had to put on hold.

“I am back at USC, so…it’s been great, in the midst of exams, so it’s a little stressful, but I actually [am] inviting the stress in because at one point I didn’t have exams, so I’m happy to be back,” Isabella told Healthline.

Along with attending classes, Isabella is enjoying activities such as working out and cooking. She continues to undergo MRI scans of her head and spine every three months, but said that after another year, the scans will become less frequent, occurring every six months.

“But really…I don’t have a lot of doctor’s appointments,” she said.

“It feels like a brand-new life, but it’s a good one. And that’s wonderful,” Isabella said.

Isabella said her family’s support played an important role in helping her through cancer treatment. Spending so much time together in the hospital brought them closer as they watched movies and played cards and board games to pass the time.

“[It] really, really bonds you to people, and just having my dad and my whole family there was something that [you] can’t beat,” she told Healthline.

Michael said that despite the difficult circumstances, he cherishes the time he spent with his daughter. Before her diagnosis, Isabella was a typical teenager who spent much of her time at school or with friends.

“They say you spend the most time with your kid for the first few years of their life, and then once school starts, you don’t get a chance to spend as much time as you would like with them,” he said.

While Michael said he and Isabella already had a close relationship, her diagnosis brought the family even closer and helped them reflect on what mattered most.

“[What] was really important was family, and relationships, and the love that you have for each other, and the support you have to give each other,” he said. “I think that our relationship now is better than ever.”

Today, Michael makes an effort to visit Isabella at USC whenever he’s in Los Angeles during the NFL season.

“Some weekends she [has time for me]. Some she doesn’t,” he said. “But just to have that relationship with your child, where they want to hang out with you, is a good feeling, and that’s the relationship that we have.”

Michael and Isabella are sharing their story to raise awareness about brain cancer and offer hope to other families facing similar challenges.

They recently participated in a fireside chat in New York City hosted by Real Chemistry and SMAC, where they discussed their family’s experience navigating Isabella’s diagnosis, mental health, caregiving, and the impact of sharing their story.

Throughout her treatment, Isabella also documented her experience through a series of YouTube vlogs and the ABC primetime documentary, “Life Interrupted: Isabella Strahan’s Fight to Beat Cancer.”

For Michael, helping others is a value he learned from his parents. He recalled how his father used his transportation business in Germany to support people in need after retiring from the military.

“[He] would take donations, and use his money and his trucks, and drive stuff to Yugoslavia and Czechoslovakia, and drop it off there at local churches and the shelters for people, who he had no connection with,” said Michael. “So for me, I think I do what I feel is on my heart and what’s important to me.”

For parents caring for children with cancer, Michael emphasized that it’s OK to feel overwhelmed or emotional. He encouraged them to show their children love and support while helping them maintain hope for the future.

“I think positivity is the main thing because it was so tough on her at the time when she was [saying], ‘I can’t do it. I can’t do this. I can’t do that,’” said Michael.

Michael said he made a conscious effort to replace words like “can’t” and “if” with “when” while encouraging Isabella during treatment.

“Anytime she got down on herself, I’d say, ‘when you go back to college, when you do this, when you do that,’” said Michael. “And I just think the positive attitude and the love that you can give and the confidence that you can display towards your loved one [or] your child who’s going through something like this is the only thing you can do because you’re at the mercy of medicine.”

Now in her senior year at USC, Isabella is looking ahead to life after graduation.

“[There’s] a lot of interesting opportunities and things that I want to pursue,” she said.

Michael has no doubt she will find her way.

“She’s so talented, and this experience, in my opinion, has opened up my eyes to more talent that she has that I didn’t realize…the talent to connect people, and be in front of a crowd. I always thought she’d be nervous because I get nervous, but she’s better than me,” he said.

#4901. How One Designer Brought a Discerning Eye to Her Deeply Personal Manhattan Apartment

Ask an interior designer about their most difficult client, and there’s a good chance they’ll name themselves. Dani Dennis spent more than a decade at Mark Cunningham Inc. before founding her own New York–based practice, Danielette Dennis Interiors, translating other people’s tastes into rooms. “I’m such a decisive person,” she says of her approach to projects ranging from a traditional Brooklyn Heights townhouse to a sleek Park Avenue pied-à-terre. But when it came to decorating her own Gramercy Park apartment that she shares with her husband and two daughters, Dennis had no client brief to answer to—and no one to make the final call but herself.

When the family moved in three years ago, the apartment had plenty going for it: tall windows, handsome moldings, and an airy living and dining area. Dennis left the architecture alone, putting furniture, textiles, and her growing art collection to work instead. Benjamin Moore’s Steam keeps the walls a warm white, though the rooms are anything but hands-off. “I almost wanted it to feel like a gallery,” she says. “But, obviously, it’s lived in, and I have kids, so nothing is too precious.”

A portrait by Patrick Eugène, sourced via Mariane Ibrahim, anchors one end of the living room. Nearby are an Alfred Grenander chair upholstered in Toyine Sellers fabric, a black mushroom side table by Emma Donnersberg, and Christian Woo’s Noga Cube side table.

A portrait of a young girl by Jerrell Gibbs hangs near a Pierre Augustin Rose Cadillac ottoman upholstered in Rosemary Hallgarten’s alpaca bouclé. Vintage Saint-Louis crystal, sourced at the Paris flea market, sits atop the credenza.

The living room’s opposing sofas encourage conversation. The sofa (right), upholstered in Pierre Frey wool and mohair, is by Marked NYC. The ceiling fixture is a reedition of Pierre Chareau’s Quart de Rond lamp, and the rug is by Scott Group Studio.

Joseph-André Motte chairs surround the dining table, while a 1960s Italian burlwood credenza from Naga Antiques sits beneath an abstract painting by Carmen Neely. The dining rug is by Crosby Street Studios.

A first-generation American of Liberian heritage, Dennis has built a collection of art that includes contemporary Black artists from across the diaspora. A painting of girls playing in a circle takes her back to her school days in Côte d’Ivoire. Others show Black women in moments of rest and repose. “I want my kids and my family to be surrounded by reflections of us,” she says. Her clients’ collections tell their own stories; this one tells hers. Black joy runs through the collection, but Dennis doesn’t shop by theme; she listens to her gut. “I buy what I love,” she says. “I don’t buy for decoration.”

Dennis is equally at ease with an English-country chintz and a graphic geometric. In her own home, she borrows a little from everywhere, favoring clean lines and unexpected combinations. A custom sofa in Sanderson velvet faces a curved Marked NYC design in Pierre Frey wool and mohair. Camel-colored lounge chairs play against the sharp geometry of a Roger Sprunger coffee table; a 1906 Josef Hoffmann side table and an early-20th-century Alfred Grenander chair join the mix. “When the harmony comes from shape and proportion and feeling, I think that’s what makes spaces dynamic,” she says.

An expressive Ferrari Sheppard painting, acquired through Massimo De Carlo in Milan, hangs above a custom sofa upholstered in Sanderson’s indigo Quartz velvet. Camel-colored chairs wear Holland & Sherry fabric. A glass-and-metal coffee table by Roger Sprunger joins a modernist stool by Charles Vandenhove and JM Szymanski’s slender Table No. 3.

Dennis took a moodier approach in the library, where the family gathers. The walls and ceiling are painted the same dark hue, creating a cozy retreat for her two daughters, who prefer sprawling on the soft rug to sitting on the sofa. Artwork includes a floral painting by Alexandria Tarver and Hazel Mphande’s Stone or Lavender (2021). The striped pillows are in Fortuny fabric.

The dining table seats 10. Dinner guests, however, are still forthcoming. “I haven't had people over yet, because in my head it’s not done,” Dennis says. Joseph-André Motte chairs surround the table, while a 1960s Italian burlwood credenza and a Crosby Street Studios rug anchor the dining area. The living room sofas were positioned for entertaining too, although the family’s day-to-day use of the space is less formal. For Dennis’s daughters, 10 and seven, the mohair-and-wool sofa comes with ground rules: “You guys can sit on it, you can perch, but if we want to play, let’s do it elsewhere.”

The library, on the other hand, is fair game. The girls can sprawl out with a book or watch TV, though they rarely bother with the sofa. “They’re always lying on the rug,” Dennis says. She painted the walls a deep olive-green, carrying it across the ceiling for an enveloping effect. The art also takes a moodier turn, with a floral painting by Alexandria Tarver and a black-and-white self-portrait by Hazel Mphande. The primary bedroom, meanwhile, provides a brighter, quieter escape. “I always wanted to be in a cloud,” the designer says of the neutral palette. The upholstered bed, 1970s steel bench in alpaca wool, and leather-bound nightstands supply the texture.

Since the photo shoot, Dennis has switched out accessories and rearranged objects. When she recently swapped a lamp in the living room, even her husband took notice. “Another one?” he asked. At work, Dennis knows when to call a room finished. At home, she’s beginning to wonder why she should.

“I always wanted to be in a cloud,” Dennis says of the primary bedroom, where texture takes precedence over color. A vintage 1970s steel bench with alpaca upholstery sits at the foot of the bed, while leather-clad nightstands add another tactile element. The ceramic lamp is by Christiane Perrochon, and the understated artwork above the bed is by Carmen Neely.

#4898. Hormone Therapy May Help Improve Menopause Brain Fog, Mood Changes

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Menopause hormone therapy (MHT), also known as hormone replacement therapy (HRT), helps manage various menopause symptoms.

Currently, MHT is approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to help relieve hot flashes, night sweats, vaginal dryness, and dyspareunia (pain during sex).

However, recent research has found that MHT may also help improve mental symptoms like “brain fog” and mood changes.

Other research also found that MHT does not affect dementia risk. This research followed the FDA’s removal of the black box warning on these products, which suggested they may increase the risk.

Together, these studies show that MHT may help people experiencing menopause in ways not previously realized and that it is generally safe to use.

Research published in Scientific Reports on September 1 suggests that an estrogen hormone treatment that combines estriol and progesterone may help ease the symptoms of brain fog during menopause.

Brain fog may involve memory lapses, difficulty concentrating, and slower thinking.

Just about two-thirds of women experience cognitive difficulties, like brain fog, during the menopausal transition. However, there is currently no Food and Drug Administration (FDA)- approved treatment for these symptoms.

This study was an observational case series that followed 20 menopausal women with an average age of 53. Each participant was given a baseline assessment for cognitive symptoms prior to treatment.

They were then treated for 12 months with a customized regimen of estriol and progesterone.

Estriol is a natural form of estrogen that occurs during pregnancy and has been used in Europe and Asia to treat other symptoms of menopause.

After 12 months, the participants reported significant reductions in:

“Estrogen plays a well-documented role in protecting the brain, yet there are still no approved type and dose of treatment that specifically targets the cognitive symptoms so many women experience during menopause,” Rhonda Voskuhl, MD, neurologist, member of the Comprehensive Menopause Center at UCLA Health, and senior study author, said in a press release.

“Women are often told to simply live with brain fog, when in fact there is a neuroscience basis for it, and potentially a way to address it. That gap in care is what motivated us to look more closely at estriol,” Voskuhl said.

This was a small, preliminary case series without a placebo group or randomization. More research is needed to confirm the benefits for cognitive symptoms.

A study published September 9 in Menopause, the journal of the Menopause Society, showed that hormone therapy may help improve mood changes in women in midlife.

The researchers noted that mood disturbances, such as depression, irritability, anxiety, and mental exhaustion, affect up to 68% of women during menopause.

“The erratic changes and loss of estrogen associated with this hormonal transition can lower serotonin and dopamine levels, which [are] associated with depression and cognitive changes, including poor concentration, brain fog, and memory loss,” said Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

The study included 260 individuals with an average age of 52 years. Just over half of the participants had a history of anxiety or depression, and 24% were using an antidepressant medication at the start of the study.

Each participant completed the Menopause Rating Scale (MRS) both before and after initiating hormone therapy for FDA-approved indications, such as hot flashes and night sweats.

The average follow-up during the study was 4.5 months. Between the baseline and the follow-up, there was a significant reduction in the MRS psychological scores. The percentage of participants with severe mood symptoms decreased from 62.3% to 24.6%.

Treatment response didn’t appear to differ by age, menopausal stage, psychiatric history, or antidepressant use. These findings suggest that hormone therapy may help improve mood symptoms in midlife, especially for those with higher severity of symptoms before treatment.

“This study supports the evidence that starting hormone therapy for the typical disruptive symptoms of menopause may bring collateral benefits, including improvement in mood,” Ross said. “Communicating with your healthcare provider is always best to manage your symptoms and prepare yourself properly for this next stage of life.”

For decades, women have been warned about the serious potential risks associated with menopause hormone therapy (MHT). This included an FDA “black box” warning citing complications like cardiovascular disease, cancer, and dementia.

However, a recent meta-analysis commissioned by the World Health Organization (WHO) that included data from more than a million women, and was published in The Lancet Healthy Longevity, found that MHT neither increased nor decreased dementia risk.

“The World Health Organisation currently provides no guidance on MHT and dementia risk, leaving a critical gap for women, clinicians, and policymakers. Our work will help inform upcoming WHO guidelines on reducing the risk of cognitive decline and dementia,” Melissa Melville, a PhD candidate and aging researcher in the Department of Clinical Health Psychology at the University College London, and first author of the study, told Healthline in a previous interview.

In the same interview, Melville stressed that the importance of this work is twofold: for clinicians, there is an urgent need for higher-quality research, and for patients, decisions about MHT should be guided by symptoms and well-established benefits and risks.

#4896. 7 Best Cheese Boards for Entertaining Centerpieces (2026)

The calendar pointing toward the holidays also means that cheese board season is here. The platters function as a key part of any festive gathering, serving as the meeting point for people to nibble and mingle before they take a seat. But it’s much more than a way to display finger foods. Often situated in a central location at a party, a cheese board works as a decorative design piece, and when done well, can even be a conversation starter. In fact, some boards themselves read as a still life, even after half the Brie is gone.

The most unique cheese boards combine material and shape, offering something distinct and memorable to house the bites (or function as a surface for slicing a baguette). Designers are creating beautiful centerpieces by carving wood into soft, stonelike forms or cutting marble into asymmetrical ovals. Ahead, find seven cheese boards to invest in now, each one pretty enough to steal the spotlight at any event.

Sarah Sherman Samuel is known for blending character with clean modern lines in her interiors, and her cheese board trio for Lulu and Georgia brings that same sensibility to the table. The set includes two marble boards and one made of acacia, allowing you to layer textures; situating soft cheeses on cool stone and bread on wood. The three sizes allow you to extend your spread down a long table for a crowd or cuddle everything close on the counter.

These amorphous-shaped boards by Danish brand Ferm Living are inspired by cairns, the stacks of stones that hikers leave as markers on the trail. Each is carved from solid ash that’s certified by the FSC—a third-party organization that confirms responsible sourcing of wood—and is given a carbonized finish that turns the wood a deep espresso shade. When you’re not using the boards to display cheese, you can stack them on a counter where they’ll look like a sculptural piece.

For a more artful approach to your cheese board, check out this tortoiseshell option from CB2. Portuguese artisans hand-finish each piece in a reactive glaze, so no two boards come out identical. The amber and chocolate swirls highlight both white cheeses and green accoutrements, such as green grapes and Granny Smith apples, for an especially striking display. And at roughly 10 by 18 inches, the long board runs neatly down the center of a table.

Serena & Lily has made this wave shape its signature, commonly appearing on its crested mirrors and nightstands, and this board brings that coastal edge to the table. The scalloped surface is the relaxed, beach-house counterpoint to the more architectural boards on this list. At 18 inches, the small size suits a cozy night in for two, while the large, 30-inch board option has room to house a full spread with all the accoutrements.

Red marble has been having a moment, showing up on bathroom vanities and living room floors. This tray is a low-lift way to incorporate the material in your space. Cut from deep red stone into a sleek oval with a thick rim, the color of this CB2 cheese board is inherently festive but will look elevated and at home in your kitchen year-round. Because it’s solid marble, the veining and color will vary from tray to tray.

At 13 by 18.5 inches, the generously sized Romie has room for the full spread, with space for several cheeses, cured meats, a cluster of grapes, and a stack of crackers that won’t slide off the edge. Its classic oval shape is made from acacia that’s stained a deep chocolate, bringing out the grain and hiding knife marks. It’s an Anthropologie exclusive and a beautiful board to reach for when you’re feeding more than a few people.

The artist Neil Cohen designed this 15-inch acacia block for the New Mexico–based brand Nambe, and the piece’s best feature is hidden in plain sight. The wood is carved with nooks that hold the knife and spreader flush with the surface, so the tools stay with the board instead of getting lost in a drawer, which is a pretty great feature. The clean shape is modern, making it an easy hostess gift.

#4895. Bigger, badder Section 179 tax deduction could POWER UP fleet electrification [update]

With national diesel prices inching towards $7/gallon and Tesla’s $290,000 Semi trucks finally making their way to paying customers, it’s worth revisiting how Trump’s One Big, Beautiful Bill Act (OBBA) helped beef up the Section 179 deduction, and look again at how it could help your fleet ditch diesel sooner rather than later.

At the core of this thinking is the fact that the IRS does not distinguish between fuel choices, so there’s no separate depreciation category for electric- vs. diesel-fueled commercial trucks. The relevant classifications, then, are the vehicle’s use and property classes, and under the IRS’ depreciation guidelines, tractor units designed for over-the-road use are classified as 3-year properties and fall under the 20-year recovery threshold for 100% bonus depreciation, Tesla’s electric Semi can benefit from 179 just like their diesel-powered competitors. But don’t get me wrong – this isn’t just great news for Tesla Semi buyers. Electric garbage trucks, electric vocational trucks, battery electric reefer trailers, and even some big electric forklifts can potentially benefit from the same accelerated depreciation rules, since the IRS lists these as, “specialized business vehicles,” with no distinction between electric, diesel, natural gas, hydrogen, magic pixie dust, anything else.

There’s been some additional movement on the tax front since our original article, too. Earlier this year, the IRS issued Notice 2026-11, providing additional guidance on the permanent restoration of 100% bonus depreciation for qualifying property acquired after January 19, 2025. That means that eligible businesses might be able to combine Section 179 expensing with bonus depreciation when ordering qualifying fleet equipment – and ordering is the key word. The IRS has specific rules for determining when equipment is considered acquired under a written, binding contract, and your tax professional and truck dealer can probably help you figure out a way to make those rules work in your favor.

You can check out the original article (with a few corrections sprinkled in to align with the latest guidance from Notice 2026-11 and Publication 946) below. Give it a read, read the disclaimer twice, then let us know what you think in the comments.

After the Commercial Clean Vehicle Credit (Section 45W) expired on September 30, the “experts” rushed out predictions of an EV sales slowdown in Q4. But, with over 6,800 pages in the Internal Revenue Code still in play, a turbocharged Section 179 tax deduction could still power a strong year for commercial EVs.

The One Big, Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) of 2025 gutted America’s energy independence goals and ensuring its auto industry would fall even further behind the Chinese in the EV race, but the loss of Section 45W wasn’t the only change written into the IRS’ rulebook. Section 179, an immediate expense reduction that business owners can take on depreciable equipment assets, has been made significantly more powerful for 2025.

The section 179 expense deduction is limited to such items as cars, office equipment, business machinery, and computers. This speedy deduction can provide substantial tax relief for business owners who are purchasing startup equipment.

The revised Section 179 tax credit (or, more accurately, expense reduction) allows for a 100% deduction for equipment purchases has doubled to $2.5 million, with a phase-out kicking in at $4 million of capital investments that drops to zero at $6.5 million. That can be applied to new and used vehicles, as well as charging infrastructure, battery energy storage systems, specialized tools, and more (as long as they’re new to you).

“But wait,” as they say. “There’s more!” A revised Section 168(k) also allows for bonus depreciation on eligible equipment and property, accelerating depreciation for a reduced tax burden.

Section 179 and bonus depreciation aren’t mutually exclusive, either, which means that businesses don’t have to exceed the Section 179 spending cap to be able to take advantage of both. A fleet operator can elect to expense a portion of a qualifying vehicle or equipment purchase under Section 179, then apply bonus depreciation to the remaining eligible basis.

With 100% bonus depreciation restored for qualifying property acquired after January 19, 2025, that combination could allow businesses to deduct the entire qualifying business-use cost of an electric truck or other commercial equipment in the first year, subject to applicable eligibility requirements and limitations.

It’s a bit convoluted (what good tax code isn’t?), but these tax incentives are great for businesses looking to buy enough electric equipment assets to exceed the Section 179 spending limit – and, given the new Uber/Tesla semi truck purchase plan, the continued growth of the electric terminal tractor market, and the willingness of several utilities to incentivize both electric commercial vehicles and the deployment of smart EV charging infrastructure, that number may be bigger than you think.

Tax law is weird. Not only are there Federal tax laws and rules that need to be followed, but state and even local county and city rules, as well. As such, you want to make sure they don’t get you the way the got Capone.

Even worse, your favorite journalist (Hi!) is probably an idiot. Get a certified accountant and tax law expert to help walk you through the dirtier details of your specific scenario – but don’t let the complexity of human interaction slow you down, either. The really rich guys you know pay pennies on the tax dollar compared to you and me, because they’re not afraid to ask their accountants for help.

As a tax CPA, I cringe when I see "Sec 179" and the word "credit" put together. It is grossly inaccurate to say that Sec 179 is a tax credit.

Sec 179 and bonus depreciation are nothing new. OBBBA just raised the 179 maximum up, but the deductibility, specifically for heavy vehicles like semis, is unchanged.

The TL;DR version, though, is this: there’s still plenty of incentives out there for fleet operators looking to electrify their operations.

Inside the shoes and layers the endurance athlete relies on for steep climbs.

RÉMI BONNET MAKES running up mountains look easy. The Swiss endurance athlete holds the world record for the vertical kilometer, having climbed more than 3,200 feet in just 27 minutes and 21 seconds. It’s the equivalent of running nearly three Empire State Buildings straight up in less than half an hour.

That's why Tudor, Rolex's adventurous younger brother and one of our favorite watch brands, tapped the 31-year-old athlete for its “Born to Dare” campaign, which celebrates high-achieving risk-takers. For Bonnet, that partnership means "daring to take risks, daring to try new things, and daring to push the limit a bit further."

When Men's Health caught up with Bonnet in Chamonix, France, during UTMB Mont-Blanc, one of the world's most prestigious trail-running events, he shared how his gear has evolved over the years. While he once obsessed over shaving every possible gram off his equipment, he's learned that lighter isn't always better, especially when it comes to performance.

We asked Bonnet to share the six pieces of gear he swears by, from his favorite trail-running shoes to a blister-prevention product.

As a Tudor ambassador, Bonnet has his pick of some seriously handsome watches, but the Ranger is his favorite. We're also big fans. #4893. Peterbilt makes it easier than ever for government fleets to go electric

Legendary truck brand Peterbilt is lowering procurement barriers for state and municipal fleets looking to electrify their assets by adding its growing lineup of medium- and heavy-duty battery electric trucks to its cooperative purchasing Agreement with Sourcewell.

The expanded purchasing contract, extended through July 2028, provides Sourcewell’s more than 50,000 member organizations access to competitively solicited contracts with pre-negotiated pricing and terms across the Peterbilt brand’s full lineup of Class 5-8 commercial trucks. That contract allows eligible agencies to streamline procurement by sourcing Peterbilt’s on-highway, vocational and medium-duty BEVs through Sourcewell’s pre-negotiated agreement, potentially eliminating the need to go through a long, agonizing bidding process of their own.

“Our long-standing relationship with Sourcewell reflects Peterbilt’s commitment to delivering solutions tailored to the unique needs of public fleets,” explains Erik Johnson, assistant general manager of sales and marketing at Peterbilt. “By adding our electric vehicles to the contract and eliminating lengthy bidding processes, we’re helping organizations maximize uptime and invest their resources where it matters most, keeping their communities running.”

I hear some of you asking, “Why is this important, and what exactly is Sourcewell?” And you’re very smart to ask.

See, when your state or local government decides it needs a new truck, it can’t just call up someone’s cousin and cut an inflated check backed by your tax dollars (regardless of what you saw on Fox News). Instead, public agencies generally have to follow competitive purchasing rules that are designed to ensure fairness, transparency, and responsible use of taxpayer money. That can mean writing and publishing a request for proposals (RFP), publicly soliciting bids, evaluating competing offers, and even allowing public comments that can all-too easily devolve into 17 hours of Pawnee-level discourse among stakeholders (real and imagined) about the relative merits of competing proposals.

Sourcewell is a government agency that basically does all that ahead of time, publishing RFPs, soliciting ,and evaluating bids against established criteria, then awarding contracts through a transparent, auditable process that makes competitively negotiated pricing available to more than 50,000 government, education and nonprofit organizations across the US and Canada.

In practice, that means your local government fleets can leverage Sourcewell to quickly adapt to pressing needs in a way that doesn’t bypass the bidding process (which would be illegal), but which effectively says, “we trust Sourcewell’s process.”

You can check out the full text of Sourcewell’s contract with Peterbilt, below, then let us know what you think of Pete’s efforts to electrify your local government fleet in the comments.

#4892. Heart Failure Is Becoming More Common in Adults Under 65, Report Finds

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

An estimated 56 million people worldwide were living with heart failure in 2023, more than double the number in 1990, according to a new Global Burden of Disease (GBD) analysis published September 30 in JAMA Cardiology.

Heart failure is a chronic condition in which the heart cannot pump enough blood to meet the body’s needs. Despite its name, heart failure doesn’t mean the heart has stopped beating.

A separate 2026 report from the Heart Failure Society of America (HFSA) found that heart failure contributed to more than 450,000 deaths in the United States in 2023, with particularly sharp increases in mortality among adults under 65.

Approximately 7.7 million U.S. adults (3%) are living with heart failure, according to the HFSA report. That number is projected to rise to 8.7 million by 2030, 10.3 million by 2040, and 11.4 million by 2050.

The GBD researchers said the findings highlight the growing burden of heart failure associated with population growth and aging, as well as the need for prevention and treatment strategies targeting common causes such as ischemic heart disease and hypertension (high blood pressure).

Between 2010 and 2022, heart failure hospitalization rates increased among adults under 65 while declining among those 65 and older. Between 2001 and 2020, estimated heart failure mortality rates more than doubled among adults ages 25 to 44 and nearly doubled among those ages 45 to 64.

“I think we’re seeing the impact of cardiovascular risk factors developing earlier in life. Conditions such as obesity, diabetes, hypertension, and sleep apnea are affecting younger adults at higher rates than in previous generations,” Kevin Shah, MD, Program Director of Heart Failure Outreach at MemorialCare Heart & Vascular Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in Long Beach, CA, who wasn’t involved in the analysis, told Healthline.

“When those risk factors are present for decades, they can eventually lead to heart failure at a younger age,” he said.

Amit Blumfield, MD, a cardiologist at Manhattan Cardiology in New York City who was also not involved in the analysis, noted that improvements in early detection may also play a role.

“Our tools to detect early heart failure are also improving with time, and as a result, patients may receive an earlier diagnosis and can hopefully start medical therapy to prevent progression or possibly even reverse the process entirely.”

Heart failure contributed to 452,573 deaths in the United States in 2023, accounting for approximately 14.6% of all deaths that year, according to the HFSA report.

Age-adjusted heart failure mortality rates increased by approximately 37% between 2010 and 2023, with preliminary 2024 data suggesting the upward trend is continuing.

This increase appears to have reversed more than a decade of previous progress in reducing heart failure mortality.

Ischemic heart disease and hypertension were among the most common causes of heart failure in older adults, according to the GBD report. However, the underlying causes varied considerably by age group and region.

Among children and young adults, congenital heart abnormalities and rheumatic heart disease (RHD) were common causes of heart failure. Heart failure associated with alcohol use was most prevalent during middle adulthood.

The analysis also identified regional differences in the underlying causes of heart failure:

The global burden of heart failure is expected to grow as populations age.

While not all cases of heart failure can be prevented, managing certain risk factors can help reduce your risk. These include:

According to Shah, other important steps to help prevent heart failure include maintaining a healthy weight, staying physically active, and managing conditions such as diabetes and high cholesterol.

He added that regular medical care is important because many of these risk factors can be identified and treated long before heart failure develops.

“Heart failure is often treatable and may even be reversible with early diagnosis and treatment. It is important for patients to seek help early if they have concerning symptoms, such as exertional shortness of breath, chest pain, leg swelling, or anything else they’re concerned about. Talk with your doctors early and often,” said Blumfield.

#4889. Bella Hadid Goes All-In on Colorado With the Purchase of a $5.5 Million Mountain Home

Looks like Bella Hadid is putting down roots in Colorado. On November 10, the Wall Street Journal revealed that the model had spent $5.5 million on a home in Crested Butte, about 20 miles south of Aspen. The news came just a day after Hadid hard-launched her new boyfriend, construction project manager Nathan Lacy, whose connection to the area goes back five generations. According to a statement Hadid’s listing agent gave to the WSJ, she had been living in the region for “several months” before pulling the trigger on her unique new digs, built from two coal miner sheds that date to the early 20th century.

Situated just a few feet from Coal Creek, the 2,000-square-foot residence is complete with three bedrooms and three bathrooms. The sellers built the home in 2001, using two existing structures composed of wood and corrugated metal that had stood on the property for 100 years. The sheds were reassembled over a new foundation, with each wooden plank returned to its exact spot on its wall, according to the WSJ. Antique timber from an old Montana mine makes up the rest of the exterior, while pine reclaimed from an 18th-century Virginia barn lines the floors.

A reverse floor plan was designed to let more light into the upper level common areas, like the fireplace-warmed great room, when snow piles up around the ground floor. Reclaimed warehouse beams from a similar time period to the original structures line the vaulted ceiling, with original iron bolts still in place.

#4888. Yes, electric motorcycles will replace gasoline motorcycles, but not the way you think

For years, the conventional wisdom surrounding electric motorcycles has been fairly straightforward: Build an electric motorcycle that’s just as good as a gasoline-powered motorcycle, make it affordable enough, and eventually riders will switch.

It’s essentially the same playbook that electric cars have followed. Make the electric version competitive enough, and eventually the market will move in that direction.

But I think we’ve been looking at electric motorcycles all wrong. Because the future of electric motorcycles isn’t necessarily about convincing today’s gasoline motorcycle riders to switch. It’s about the next generation of riders who won’t have any reason to start with gasoline in the first place.

And that’s a much bigger deal than it might sound. In fact, it’s the entire key to the puzzle. Because make no mistake, the future of motorcycling is electric. It just won’t happen the way most people think.

The problem with applying the electric car playbook to motorcycles is that motorcycles aren’t cars.

For most people, a car is a tool. It’s transportation. Sure, some drivers are deeply passionate about their cars, but they are a minority. The vast majority simply want something comfortable, reliable, affordable, and convenient to get them from point A to point B.

Motorcycles, especially in markets like the US and Europe, are different. In fact, it’s the complete opposite of the car situation. For a minority of Western riders, and especially American riders, a motorcycle is a tool. For the vast majority, it’s a passion. People generally buy motorcycles because they love motorcycles. And for many riders, that love is deeply intertwined with the gasoline engine itself.

The sound, the vibration, the shifting, the mechanical character. These aren’t necessarily flaws to be engineered away. They’re part of the experience for traditional riders.

You can show someone like that an electric motorcycle with better acceleration, lower maintenance costs, cheaper operating expenses, and the convenience of charging at home, and they still won’t want it.

Not because the electric motorcycle isn’t good enough, but because it isn’t what they love.

And that’s fine. There’s nothing wrong with loving gasoline motorcycles if that’s your thing. But it means that trying to convert those riders may be a losing battle, at least on any meaningful timeline.

Unfortunately though, it seems many years were wasted on that approach. Take Harley-Davidson, for example.

The brand started building its first electric motorcycle over a decade ago – commendable among all the other legacy motorcycle manufacturers that turned their noses up at the thought of going electric. When it was released in 2018, the Harley-Davidson LiveWire received rave reviews, but it just didn’t sell well. For one, it was insanely expensive at $30,000. But the other major reason was that no matter how amazing it was, it wasn’t going to scratch that gasoline itch that most riders still had.

That motorcycle still exists in small-scale production as the LiveWire One through H-D’s electric spinoff, but now LiveWire has wisened up to the real pathway to moving higher numbers of electric motorcycles with its S4 Honcho line. And this brings us to how electric motorcycles will really replace their gasoline counterparts.

Now consider what’s happening with younger riders today.

Millions of people are growing up riding electric bicycles, electric scooters, and increasingly powerful e-motos. For them, electric two-wheelers aren’t some strange new alternative to the real thing. They are the real thing.

They’ve grown accustomed to instant torque, quiet operation, and the simplicity of twisting a throttle and going. To these riders, a gasoline-powered motorcycle is laughably primitive. It’d be like me handing you a gasoline-powered drill or blender. Yeah, exactly. Now look who’s suddenly on team battery, eh?

So now we have a whole new generation of riders getting ready to come of age. And eventually, many of those riders are going to want something more.

Maybe they’ve spent years riding a 28 mph electric bike, or a 40 mph electric moto. Now they’re young adults, they want to ride on faster roads, and they want something capable of 70 or 80 mph that’s actually street legal. They’ve gone from a sixteen-year-old in a hoodie to a twenty-six-year-old in a collared shirt. Instead of getting to high school, now they’re getting to work. But they haven’t lost that desire to ride.

It’s not because they’ve carefully compared electric and gasoline motorcycles and decided that electric is superior. It’s not because they’re trying to save the planet or reduce their maintenance costs. It’s simply because they grew up with electric two-wheelers as the norm.

It’s simply the next logical step in the type of riding they’ve already been doing for years.

Will a few of them transition over to gasoline-powered motorcycles? Sure. But for most, gasoline motorcycles will never even enter the equation.

Don’t get me wrong, we haven’t seen the last of gasoline motorcycles.

And I don’t even think electric motorcycles are going to suddenly outsell gasoline motorcycles because they become objectively better machines. At least not in the traditional recreational motorcycle markets we’re talking about here.

Instead, I think we’re going to see a somewhat more gradual generational transition.

Today’s gasoline motorcycle enthusiasts will largely keep riding gasoline motorcycles. They’ll keep buying them, maintaining them, and enjoying them for as long as they can.

But as those riders age out of motorcycling, the next generation won’t replace them with another generation of gasoline motorcycle buyers. In other words, gasoline motorcycles will die out with their riders.

Increasingly, those younger riders will have already entered the world of powered two-wheelers through electric bikes and e-motos. When they’re ready to move up, electric motorcycles will be waiting for them.

And over those next few years, the technology will only become better. The only remaining Achilles’ heel of electric motorcycles – their run time – will get better and better as battery technology improves and we move towards solid-state batteries.

Suddenly they’ll be on par with gasoline motorcycles for range and ‘fueling’ time, yet will beat those combustion bikes elsewhere. But at that point, “better” won’t even matter anymore as electrics will be the only comfortable option for the vast majority of new riders. Again, you wouldn’t buy a power drill that runs on a gasoline engine when you’ve seen how convenient and effective a lithium-ion power drill has been your whole life.

Over time, the traditional gasoline motorcycle market will simply shrink as its existing customer base gets older, while electric motorcycles build an entirely new customer base alongside it.

And again, there will be exceptions in both directions. Some lifelong gasoline riders will enthusiastically embrace electric motorcycles, and some younger riders will still discover the appeal of combustion engines and running through the gears.

Living in SE Asia this all sounds very strange. The vast majority of the worlds bikes are not owned as toys, they are the main form of transport for the family. I agree with a lot of the article but Asia is already years ahead. The fuel shortages and sky high prices are pushing people electric far faster than riders are "aging out"

But the broader trend will be driven less by conversion and more by generational replacement.

That’s why electric motorcycles will eventually become the standard, even if they never manage to convince most of today’s gasoline motorcycle enthusiasts to switch.

Gasoline motorcycles won’t necessarily disappear because electric motorcycles finally beat them at their own game. They’ll fade because the next generation of riders will be playing a different game entirely.

In other words, and to paraphrase T. S. Eliot, gasoline motorcycles won’t go out with a bang, but with a whimper.
