#4885. Viola Davis Says Shingles Pain Was a ‘9 Out of 10’: How It Changed Her Life

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Emmy, Grammy, Oscar, and Tony Award-winning actress and producer Viola Davis was 50 years old and at the height of her career when she developed shingles.

“All of a sudden, I got a rash in the middle of traveling and juggling [back-to-back] movies that started in the middle of my stomach [and] wrapped around my back,” she told Healthline. “And the pain was definitely at a 9 out of 10.”

Focused on work, Davis didn’t see a doctor right away. Her symptoms eventually subsided but later returned on her upper back.

“That is when I was like, ‘Oh, my goodness, I just got bitten by a poisonous spider,” said Davis. “It felt like really bad nerve pain…then someone mentioned the word shingles.”

After seeing a doctor, Davis learned she had shingles, which is caused by the same virus responsible for chickenpox. She also learned that the risk increases with age as the immune system naturally weakens.

“It can also lead to herpetic neuralgia, lifelong pain that can result from a shingles outbreak,” Robyn Liu, MD, member of the board of directors at the American Academy of Family Physicians, told Healthline.

Shingles can affect daily life, including work and sleep.

“Many people don’t realize that shingles can be associated with nerve pain, and for some, that pain can last for months or even years after the rash has resolved due to potential long-term complications,” Jennifer Caudle, DO, family medicine physician and associate professor at Rowan University School of Osteopathic Medicine, told Healthline.

Nearly one in three people in the U.S. develops shingles in their lifetime.

“Anyone who has had chickenpox can get it, but the risk goes up as we age,” said Liu.

After learning about shingles, Davis said she realized that she could no longer ignore her body.

“You just cannot. I mean, I know that people say it at nauseam, but, for me, it meant slowing down,” she said. “But then the shingles slowed me down…even though I was getting up, I was working out, [the] pain and the sluggishness was awful.”

Now 61, Davis has teamed up with GSK for its “Shingles, actually” campaign to raise awareness about shingles.

“[I’m] sending the message out there for women to really honor themselves and honor their bodies,” she said. “Their bodies literally speak. They’re the other character in their lives.”

After her experience with shingles, Davis began scheduling regular annual checkups.

“Before that time, I did absolutely go to my doctor, but only when it was absolutely necessary, you know, to get my colonoscopy, get my mammogram, but I never went if something felt odd,” said Davis.

Now, whenever she feels off, she doesn’t hesitate to see her doctor. She makes it a point to be honest with them about what’s going on with her body, and she is not shy about asking questions.

“I feel that it’s my job. It’s what I owe myself, and not only [do] I owe myself, it’s what I owe my daughter, it’s what I owe my husband, it’s what I owe my elderly mom, who is in advanced dementia [and] I take care of her,” Davis said.

Caudle stressed the importance of bringing questions to your doctor.

“That’s what we’re here for. Sometimes patients ​tell me they don’t realize they can ask questions or introduce a topic that their healthcare provider didn’t bring up first,” she said. “But a simple question like, ‘Am I at risk for shingles?’ can be a great place to begin.”

Davis has made stress reduction a priority. When she developed shingles, she was juggling an intense workload and adjusting to life as a new mom after adopting her daughter.

“I was someone who just ignored stress,” said Davis. “[When] we meet each other, it becomes a contest: Who can work through their stress the best? I felt like I had to strike while the iron was hot.”

Today, Davis makes stress reduction a priority through activities such as spraying her pillows with lavender, taking Epsom salt baths, meditating, going to therapy, exercising, spending time outdoors, using vagus nerve stimulation, and connecting with family and friends.

“It’s like I overdo it now because I feel like I have to overdo it in order to compete with my schedule. But that’s what I owe myself,” Davis said.

Still, her profession can be intense and time consuming due to shooting and promotional schedules. She still makes time to de-stress and focuses on projects that bring her joy.

“Your career is only as important as how much you enjoy it; how much it adds to your life,” said Davis. “[For] me, it used to be something that I felt defined my worth. I don’t feel like it defines my worth now…I feel that I’m already worthy… I was born worthy. That’s how I see it.”

Vaccination is the best way to protect against shingles, Liu said. The shingles vaccine is more than 90% effective at preventing the disease.

“For children, that protection begins with the chickenpox vaccine, which can be given along with other routine childhood immunizations. Preventing chickenpox also lowers the risk of developing shingles later in life,” she said.

Liu recommends two doses of the shingles vaccine for adults 50 and older, as well as adults 19 and older with weakened immune systems.

“Some patients may have more questions because side effects can be more noticeable than with other vaccines, so it’s important to understand the tradeoff: short-term discomfort from the vaccine versus the risk of lasting complications from shingles,” said Liu.

Side effects may include headache or redness, swelling, itching, and soreness where the shot was given.

“I tell my patients that these reactions are common and usually clear up within a day or two. They’re a normal sign that your body is building protection,” said Liu.

#4881. PMOS, Formerly PCOS, May Be Associated with Alzheimer's Disease Risk

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome (PMOS), formerly known as polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS), has been linked to changes in brain function and cognition that may overlap with those seen in Alzheimer’s disease.

However, a review published October 13 in the Journal of the Endocrine Society suggests that metabolic conditions commonly associated with PMOS, rather than PMOS itself, may help explain a potential link to Alzheimer’s disease and dementia risk.

“Researchers are exploring whether some metabolic conditions commonly seen in women with PMOS, such as insulin resistance, obesity, type 2 diabetes, [and] elevated cholesterol, could play a role in dementia, since these are already known risk factors for cognitive decline,” Yesika Garcia, MD, board certified endocrinologist with Medical Offices of Manhattan, who was not involved in the study, told Healthline.

“In other words, any potential connection may be related to these underlying metabolic issues rather than PMOS itself. I think it’s important to clarify that an association does not mean causation,” she added.

The researchers searched PubMed for studies published through 2025 examining brain structure and function, cognitive performance, biomarkers associated with neurodegenerative diseases, and dementia risk in people with PMOS.

Because PMOS is a newer term, the researchers searched for studies using both PMOS and PCOS.

The review identified studies suggesting that people with PMOS may experience mild differences in certain cognitive abilities, including:

Some brain imaging studies using MRI and PET scans identified differences in brain structure and function among people with PMOS. Researchers also observed hormonal changes associated with differences in gray matter.

Some studies identified elevated biomarkers associated with Alzheimer’s disease in people with PMOS. These findings appeared to overlap with metabolic changes seen in type 2 diabetes, particularly those associated with obesity and insulin resistance.

The researchers also found that neurological differences were less pronounced when people with PMOS were compared with metabolically healthy control groups, suggesting that associated metabolic conditions may play an important role in these findings.

Overall, the review suggests that metabolic conditions associated with PMOS may contribute to changes in brain health, although more research is needed to determine whether PMOS independently affects Alzheimer’s disease or dementia risk.

“I think we need to be careful about drawing conclusions too early. The research raises some good questions, but it doesn’t prove that PCOS [PMOS] causes Alzheimer’s disease,” said Dung Trinh, MD, internist, of MemorialCare Medical Group and Chief Medical Officer of Healthy Brain Clinic in Irvine, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

“What it does reinforce is how important metabolic health may be when it comes to protecting our brains as we get older,” he added.

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who also wasn’t involved in the study, agreed. She also added that more attention and research need to be given to PMOS, brain changes, and future risks of dementia.

There are various ways that PMOS and dementia may share pathways and overlap, according to the researchers. Some of these shared pathways include:

Trinh explained that insulin resistance may be one of the most important connections between PMOS and dementia.

Although insulin is commonly associated with diabetes and blood sugar regulation, it also plays an important role in brain function. When insulin signaling doesn’t work properly, it may interfere with how brain cells use energy, potentially contributing to problems with memory and thinking over time.

Chronic inflammation, which can occur in people with PMOS, may also play a role in Alzheimer’s disease, Trinh told Healthline.

“Researchers are looking at how these metabolic changes may affect amyloid and tau, two proteins associated with Alzheimer’s. Some brain imaging studies have even found differences in brain structure and function among women with PCOS [PMOS],” he said.

Ross added that elevated androgen levels, which are common in people with PMOS, may also play a role in dementia risk.

“An important distinction is that these risk factors are not present in every woman with PMOS, and having PMOS does not automatically mean someone will experience cognitive decline,” cautioned Garcia.

Garcia noted that many of the dementia risk factors associated with PMOS are modifiable.

“Regular exercise, including both cardiovascular activity and strength training, can improve insulin sensitivity, help with weight management, and support cardiovascular and brain health,” she added.

“A Mediterranean-style diet, with plenty of vegetables, fruits, whole grains, healthy fats, and lean sources of protein, is a reasonable place to start,” he said.

Managing blood sugar, cholesterol, and blood pressure is also important, Trinh and Garcia noted.

“Medication, like metformin, can also improve insulin resistance, which may further reduce the risk of dementia,” Ross added.

Getting adequate sleep, avoiding smoking, and staying mentally and socially active are additional ways to support long-term brain health, Garcia told Healthline.

“For someone with PCOS [PMOS], I would encourage regular visits with their healthcare provider to monitor metabolic health and address problems early,” said Trinh.

“While we cannot say that these interventions specifically prevent dementia in women with PMOS, we know they benefit overall metabolic and cardiovascular health, which are closely connected to brain health,” explained Garcia.

#4878. Eli teases the launch of a much faster electric microcar

Eli Electric Vehicles is getting ready to reveal a new version of its tiny electric microcar, and this one could expand its usefulness beyond the slow lane.

In an Instagram teaser ahead of EICMA in Milan, the company announced the upcoming Eli ZERO MX, describing it as a new L7e vehicle joining its lineup. Eli also teased a special Race Edition, with promotional artwork showing a dark, sporty-looking ZERO with red accents and a visibly open-sided cabin.

The teaser doesn’t give us much to go on in terms of hard and fast details, but the L7e designation is the biggest clue here.

The familiar European Eli ZERO that we test-drove a few years ago is an L6e light quadricycle, with a top speed of 45 km/h (28 mph). Its compact two-seat layout makes it an appealing urban runabout, though that speed ceiling limits its usefulness on faster roads. But L7e vehicles can typically travel at speeds of up to 80-90 km/h (50-56 mph).

A faster version could make the little EV practical for considerably more trips. And there’s already a tantalizing hint: Eli’s Italian website lists an L7e ZERO capable of 85 km/h (53 mph), with a claimed range of up to 200 km (124 miles). Whether those specifications directly describe the upcoming ZERO MX remains unconfirmed by the teaser.

If they do, that would suggest a much more versatile commuter while retaining the tiny footprint that makes these vehicles so easy to park.

I like the idea of a faster LSV, maybe able to cruise at 40 mph. Not a good idea for freeways but suitable for almost all urban and suburban streets. For some a better option than a bike as an urban or second vehicle.

The Race Edition raises a different set of questions. The new vehicle could feature a sportier design, such as race seats and alternative doors. That could point toward a playful or more tuned vehicle, though we’ll have to wait to learn whether “Race” means additional performance or primarily a styling package.

It’s also unclear how closely the Race Edition and ZERO MX are related. The caption presents them separately, while the artwork additionally points towards a second-generation ZERO.

Either way, a roughly 50 mph Eli would be an interesting proposition: still small enough to rethink how much vehicle a daily commute requires, but potentially fast enough to cover routes the original couldn’t comfortably handle.

Eli says the reveal takes place at EICMA, November 3–8, in Hall 22, Stand B40. We’ll be there to see what they unveil.

#4877. Bunny Williams Never Set Out to Collect Birdhouses—Now a Whole Village of Them Stands in Her Connecticut Garden

Among the apple trees at the edge of AD100 Hall of Famer Bunny Williams’s storied country garden in Falls Village, Connecticut, stands a church with a sensible tin roof; a thatched cottage; a pagoda; a red house flying a tiny flag that she takes for a general store; and a shuttered confection that suggests a Russian dacha. Each is raised high on its own post, finished on every side, painted and weathered like the structures of a settled town—and none has a door you could fit a hand through. It is a hamlet fashioned for birds, assembled building by building, the way real towns often grow: slowly, and without a master plan.

For many years as Williams and her husband, the antiques dealer John Rosselli, combed fields and fairs for Treillage, the much-loved shop they ran in New York City until 2015, the birdhouses kept finding her. They gathered on shelves in the hayloft of her barn. “When I had two, I didn’t know what to do with them,” she says. “Then all of a sudden there were 10.” She decided they had been in storage long enough.

A farmhouse with a side porch crafted by the late Larry Smith, a North Georgia birdhouse builder known for fashioning charmingly detailed structures with metal roofs.

An A-frame house embellished with gingerbread trim and decorative brackets

Williams, on a tree stump next to the pagoda. The notch in the base of the birdhouse indicates that it was probably designed to be mounted on a roof.

At that point, Williams and Robert Reimer, head gardener on her property, started the process of reclaiming a long-overlooked point of land. To create space and liberate a stand of existing apple trees, they removed invasive species, then planted oat grass, bird-friendly scrub plants, and fruit-bearing pawpaw trees. To create the neighborhood, she had uniform posts made—tall, “so that you look up at them,” each wrapped with a metal band to deter ambitious squirrels from climbing too high and disturbing the tenants she had in mind. Finally, up went the houses, now totaling 17.

The attraction had nothing to do with value or pedigree. “Most of these birdhouses are sort of folk art,” she says. “They were somebody having fun building an unusual structure.” Not Monticello in miniature, not a replica of someone’s own house, just unknown makers, each with “an eye and a kind of humor,” none of them trying to be perfect. The proof is in the details: the gingerbread scallops on one many-holed high-rise (“somebody had a great time with their little saw”), the thatched-roof cottage she prizes because she has never seen another like it, the weather vane crowning the would-be dacha.

A church with a brick chimney, an oversized cupola, and Gothic Revival design elements.

A rare combination house and feeder that evokes a neoclassical temple

Williams calls this one the general store.

A colonial revival house with a columned portico built by Larry Smith

“It has a kind of Russian dacha feeling,” says Williams.

What she didn’t have was history—the dealers who sold them seldom had any to pass along. The record, it turns out, is often recoverable. “The period from 1890 to 1930 is really the birdhouse’s golden age,” says Jacob Torkelson, an architectural historian and researcher at the University of Pennsylvania’s Stuart Weitzman School of Design, and among the very few scholars in the country studying these objects, whose makers and manufacturers he has painstakingly been identifying, where a name survives. Across those decades, government pattern books encouraged farmers to install birdhouses to reduce pesticide use (the birds would eat the insects), while mail-order companies offered them to homeowners as pure whimsy, not to mention a way of promoting biodiversity in their gardens. Shown photographs of Williams’s collection, Torkelson supplied some of what the dealers couldn’t. He traced one house down to the catalog page: the six-room Bungalow Martin House sold by the E.F. Hodgson Company, which listed the model from about 1917 until the firm closed in 1944. “Birdhouses are these wonderful mirrors to the human condition,” Torkelson says. “They tell us the values that we have as a society or as individuals, in our houses, in our gardens.”

A double porch house also built by Larry Smith

This pagoda-inspired birdhouse provides multiple roosting spots.

An unusual thatched-roof cottage prized by Williams, who has never seen another like it.

Williams isn’t finished collecting. Last May at Trade Secrets, the annual Litchfield Hills garden fair she cofounded in 2001 to raise funds for a local nonprofit domestic violence support agency, she stopped at the tent of Jim Sidon, whose company, Sparrow 10:29, crafts avian-size replicas of real buildings. Sidon, based outside Atlanta, came to the work after retiring from the printing industry. His first architectural birdhouse, built in 2017, was a replica of the 1792 Federal-style mansion in Newburyport, Massachusetts, where he and his wife had rented two rooms as newlyweds. It was Sidon who recognized, in three of Williams’s houses, the hand of his late friend Larry Smith, a North Georgia birdhouse builder known for his metal roofs and charmingly rustic details. Williams has asked Sidon to build her something new, though she is still deciding exactly what. Unlike her anonymous makers, Sidon numbers and signs every house. “My hope is that years from now, someone’s searching that out,” he says, “and when they open it up, they’ll see my signature.”

A dovecote-inspired birdhouse offers multiple residences

A picturesque house topped with a prominent weather vane

Drawing the eye like miniature garden follies, the houses rise on poles amid a stand of apple trees from ground planted with oat grass and bird-friendly scrub plants.

In Falls Village, the upkeep routine is minimal: Every couple of years the houses come down, receive a coat of wood preservative, and go back up into the weather. Sidon’s constructions, with cedar exteriors and sealed against the elements, ask even less. His replica of the Newburyport house has stood in his own garden for nearly a decade untouched. “I just hose it off,” he says. None of this troubles Torkelson, a preservationist by training. “These houses were made to be ephemeral,” he says. “They weren’t made to last forever, and they should absolutely be used.”

As for the intended residents? Williams thinks the birds mostly prefer making their homes in the trees, where they carry on, she says, like a symphony orchestra: “I’ve always felt that one of the reasons they don’t live in these houses is the birds think it’s suburbia. But they like the atmosphere.” Come winter, when the woods go bare and the village shows through the branches, Sidon would bet the houses do get used, songbirds crowding in two and three at a time for warmth. Williams can’t say—she has never once climbed up to look. “If they are nesting,” she says, “I don’t want to bother them.”

#4874. RJ Mitte's Death Highlights Mental Health Challenges for People With Cerebral Palsy

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

“Breaking Bad” actor and disability advocate RJ Mitte has died at age 34.

Mitte reportedly died by suicide, his representative told People on October 12. However, local authorities have said the official cause and manner of his death remain under investigation.

Mitte, who was best-known for playing Walter White Jr. on the Emmy Award-winning television series “Breaking Bad,” lived with cerebral palsy and advocated for disability inclusion.

There is currently no evidence to suggest that cerebral palsy caused the actor’s death. Still, Mitte’s untimely passing highlights the mental health challenges that people with cerebral palsy may face.

Research has demonstrated elevated risks of anxiety and depression among those with the condition, alongside barriers to diagnosis, social participation, and employment.

“Living with cerebral palsy can affect mental health through several interacting pathways,” said Shannel Elhelou, neuropsychologist and founder of Brain Centric Neuropsychology in Westlake Village, CA.

“Chronic pain, fatigue, disrupted sleep, and the effort required to navigate daily activities can take an emotional toll. Experiences of bullying, discrimination, social exclusion, and difficulty accessing care can add to that burden. Also, the early brain injury underlying cerebral palsy may contribute to difficulties with emotional regulation, although each person’s experience is different,” Elhelou told Healthline.

If you or someone you know is in crisis and considering suicide or self-harm, please seek support:

If you’re calling on behalf of someone else, stay with them until help arrives. You may remove weapons or substances that can cause harm if you can do so safely.

If you’re not in the same household, stay on the phone with them until help arrives.

Mitte played a key role in challenging stereotypes by portraying a teenager with cerebral palsy on “Breaking Bad.”

Outside of acting, Mitte advocated for people with disabilities through organizations including United Cerebral Palsy (UCP) and Shriners Children’s.

He also led the Mitte Foundation, a non-profit corporation founded in 1994 by his grandparents, Roy F. and Joann Cole Mitte, that improves the quality of life for people in Central Texas.

Mitte’s advocacy emphasized representation, inclusion, and opportunities for people with disabilities.

The legacy he leaves behind underscores the need to recognize mental health as an essential part of care for individuals with cerebral palsy and other disabilities.

Cerebral palsy (CP) is a group of conditions affecting movement, posture, and coordination.

Most people with cerebral palsy have typical intelligence, although some also have intellectual disabilities or other comorbid physical and mental health conditions.

A 2018 study published in JAMA Neurology found that adults with cerebral palsy had a 28% higher risk of developing depression and a 40% higher risk of anxiety than adults without the condition.

A 2024 study in JAMA Network Open found higher recorded rates of anxiety and attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) among children and young adults with cerebral palsy.

The researchers also identified comparatively low rates of depression and suicidal ideation, highlighting concerns that these conditions may be underdiagnosed.

“Research shows that adults with cerebral palsy have an increased risk of depression and anxiety,” Elhelou said.

“However, these conditions should never be viewed as an inevitable part of having a disability. One concern is ‘diagnostic overshadowing,’ when symptoms of depression, anxiety, or other mental health conditions are attributed to cerebral palsy itself, leading those conditions to go unrecognized and untreated. Someone’s fatigue, withdrawal, or changes in behavior deserve careful assessment rather than the assumption that ‘this is just their cerebral palsy,'” she continued.

“While these findings help us understand mental health risks among people with cerebral palsy, they cannot tell us what any one person was experiencing. Many interacting factors may contribute to someone dying by suicide, and it would be inappropriate to attribute an individual’s death to their disability.”

A 2019 study published in Developmental Medicine & Child Neurology found that children with cerebral palsy had higher odds of anxiety disorders and behavioral challenges than children without the condition.

Difficulties with friendships and bullying were associated with some of these differences.

Factors that may affect well-being include:

The researchers also found that supportive friendships may help reduce some of these disparities, reinforcing the importance of inclusive schools and communities.

A 2021 study published in the American Journal of Preventive Medicine found that U.S. adults with disabilities faced increased risks of suicidal thoughts, planning, and attempts compared with adults without disabilities.

The 988 Lifeline received more than 8 million contacts through calls, texts, chats, and American Sign Language videophone in 2025, according to the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration.

Federal funding for the Lifeline’s dedicated LGBTQ+ youth service ended in July 2025, removing the option to press 3 for specialized support.

After pressure from Congress, the service was recently restored at the end of September, according to the Kaiser Family Foundation (KFF).

Employment and financial independence can influence well-being for anyone, particularly those with cerebral palsy.

However, a 2024 study found that just 30.4% of young adults with cerebral palsy who participated in vocational rehabilitation programs were employed when they exited those programs.

Workplace accommodations, accessible hiring, vocational rehabilitation, and inclusive social opportunities can help address these barriers.

“Depression and anxiety are treatable, and they should never be dismissed as an inevitable part of living with a disability,” Elhelou said.

“If you notice persistent sadness, anxiety, hopelessness, or withdrawal, reach out to someone you trust and a mental health professional. You deserve a clinician who takes your concerns seriously and adapts treatment to your communication, mobility, or other access needs. Care should also address pain, fatigue, and sleep difficulties, which can affect emotional well-being.”

Organizations such as United Cerebral Palsy offer information and support for people with cerebral palsy and their families.

Healthcare professionals can also improve outcomes by screening for mental health concerns, using appropriate assessment tools, and connecting patients with accessible treatment.

Improved access to care, inclusive communities, and meaningful opportunities can help people with cerebral palsy and other disabilities thrive.

“Asking for help does not diminish your resilience or your contributions. If you are experiencing thoughts of suicide or feel overwhelmed, you do not have to wait until things feel unbearable to reach out,” Elhelou said.

“Supportive relationships, peer groups, and disability communities can offer connection and understanding. Staying engaged in activities that bring enjoyment and meaning can also help.”

#4870. Pope Leo XIV Diagnosed With Lung Cancer After Surgery to Remove Part of Lung

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Pope Leo XIV has been diagnosed with lung cancer following a successful surgery, the Vatican said.

The Pope, 71, underwent surgery at Agostino Gemelli University Policlinic in Rome on October 13 to remove a nodule from his right lung. This was determined to be pulmonary adenocarcinoma, the most common type of lung cancer.

The surgeons removed the lower lobe of the Pope’s lung to “ensure a radical procedure; the lower lobe of the right lung was removed using a video-assisted technique,” said Dr. Luigi Carbone, the Director of Vatican Healthcare Services, and Professor Stephano Margaritora, the lead surgeon, in a press release.

“An intraoperative histological examination confirmed the diagnosis of lung adenocarcinoma, as suspected from the preoperative examinations,” they said.

Pope Leo was in the operating room for 3 hours, while the operation itself lasted around 90 minutes.

The doctors noted that it was a successful operation with no complications.

According to Matteo Bruni, the Director of the Holy See Press Office, the Pope asked for dinner after the surgery and spent the evening with his secretaries and other Vatican staff.

In a press release on Wednesday, the Vatican said that the Pope’s postoperative recovery is progressing well.

According to Vatican News, Pope Leo had a calm first night following his surgery.

In the coming days, exams will be conducted on the removed tissue. There has currently been no change in the initially announced week of hospitalization.

On October 14, the Pope underwent blood tests and chest X-rays that were scheduled following his surgery. The results of these tests confirm that his postoperative recovery is progressing as expected, and the values are within a normal range for the type of operation performed.

Pope Leo has also undergone some respiratory physiotherapy and has begun walking around his room, the Vatican said.

It has not yet been announced whether he will require any further treatment.

Adenocarcinoma is the most common subtype of lung cancer globally. It accounts for around 40% of all lung cancer diagnoses.

Adenocarcinoma is a type of non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC), which accounts for about 80–85% of all lung cancers.

Lung adenocarcinoma begins in the epithelial cells that produce mucus. It is the most common type of NSCLC.

Adenocarcinoma can be classified further by the genetic changes driving the tumor. For example, EGFR-positive lung cancer is found more often in people who have never smoked and can often be treated with targeted therapies, which is why biomarker testing of tumor tissue helps guide treatment decisions.

Treatment for NSCLC is generally determined by the stage. However, it typically involves one or a combination of:

Surgery is generally the first step in treatment for this type of lung cancer. The right lung has three lobes, or sections, and the left has two.

A doctor will often remove the entire lobe that has the tumor in order to remove the tumor and improve the outlook.

The body eventually adapts in order to compensate for the loss of function.

According to the American Cancer Society (ACS), for NSCLC that hasn’t spread outside of the lung, the 5-year relative survival rate is 67%.

#4869. How Europe’s battery passport could have a big impact on US e-bikes

Europe is preparing to introduce a new requirement that could change how electric bicycle batteries are tracked, repaired, recycled, and potentially even resold. Starting in February 2027, new e-bike batteries sold in the European Union will be required to carry a digital battery passport, giving each battery its own digital identity and making important information about its origins and characteristics accessible throughout its lifetime.

And while the rules are European, their impact could eventually reach American e-bike riders, too.

The battery passport requirement comes from the EU’s 2023 Batteries Regulation, which established new standards for battery sustainability, traceability, and transparency. The rules cover several categories of batteries, including those used in electric cars, electric bicycles, electric scooters, and other light electric vehicles.

Beginning February 18, 2027, newly placed batteries in these categories will need digital passports accessible through QR codes. Scanning the code will provide access to information about the battery, though exactly what users can see will depend on their authorization level.

The information can include details such as the battery’s manufacturer, chemistry, capacity, cycle count, carbon footprint, and materials used in its construction. The system also provides for information about battery performance and durability, including data related to battery health and degradation.

That last part could be particularly interesting for electric bicycle owners.

Anyone who has purchased a used electric bicycle knows that the battery is often one of the biggest unknowns. A bike might look nearly new, but its battery could have hundreds of charge cycles under its belt and significantly reduced capacity.

Even a quick test ride before a secondhand e-bike purchase may fail to demonstrate just how little capacity remains in a heavily used battery.

With replacement e-bike batteries costing hundreds of dollars, that’s a pretty important detail.

The battery passport system could eventually help address that problem by making battery information more transparent. However, there’s an important distinction: the regulations don’t necessarily guarantee that everyday consumers will be able to scan a QR code and immediately see a battery’s remaining health percentage. Some of that information may be restricted to authorized users.

Even so, the system could help repair shops, recyclers, and secondhand buyers better understand the batteries they’re dealing with.

It could also make life more complicated for e-bike manufacturers, especially smaller brands that will now need to ensure their batteries meet the new documentation and traceability requirements.

There’s currently no equivalent nationwide battery passport requirement for electric bicycles in the United States, but that doesn’t mean American riders won’t eventually benefit.

I think it's really great that European consumer protection laws have a global impact because of the size of the European single market, and that they make life just a little bit easier for consumers all over the world. USB-C was just the beginning—there's more to come!

Many major e-bike manufacturers sell their products in both Europe and North America. US-based companies like Trek and Specialized have major markets in Europe, and will have to comply with the new law. Chinese companies and other Asian manufacturers will also need to produce compliant batteries. Once those companies have developed systems to track individual batteries and provide digital passports for their European products, they may find it easier to extend those systems to their American models rather than maintain entirely separate processes.

That doesn’t mean US-bound batteries will automatically receive passports, but it could create an interesting spillover effect.

And considering how expensive e-bike batteries can be, anything that makes it easier to understand their condition, verify their origins, or facilitate repairs could be a welcome development.

For now, Europe is leading the charge on battery transparency. But if the system proves useful, American e-bike riders might eventually get some of the same benefits without waiting for Washington to catch up.

The e-bikes that make up Micah’s current daily drivers are the $999 Lectric XP 2.0, the $1,095 Ride1Up Roadster V2, the $1,199 Rad Power Bikes RadMission, and the $3,299 Priority Current. But it’s a pretty evolving list these days.

WHETHER YOU'RE A weightlifter, CrossFit acolyte, cardio junkie, or yoga lover, a dependable home gym setup is a must. You might not have the square footage or budget to create a space that replicates your go-to health club or weight room space, but you can still achieve great workouts at home with the best home gym equipment.

Building your home fitness setup should prioritize space, functionality, and usability over trends. Choose gear that suits your needs and storage space. Whether it's a full-on garage gym or portable equipment, the best home gym equipment can help you make the most of your home workouts.

From traditional heavy lifting (we love these barbell sets and weight benches) to HIIT workouts (this 15-minute interval workout is sure to make you sweat) to full-on indoor cardio rigs (like the best indoor rowing machines and the best ellipticals), we've tested and vetted something out there for every type of training imaginable. In fact, we've used hundreds of pieces of equipment throughout the years, from the most high-end, commercial-grade options all the way down to budget gear from Amazon. Our extensive testing process has involved a comprehensive evaluation of multiple factors, including durability, performance, suitability for different fitness levels, and value, to ensure that we recommend only the best.

SMRTFT’s Nüobell is the perfect ultra-durable adjustable dumbbell for my home workouts. It can easily be five pounds, perfect for warm-up work, or 80 pounds, enough to challenge most people on fundamental leg moves, Eric Sung, C.S.C.S., a member of the Men's Health Strength in Diversity initiative and trainer at Performance Lab in New York City, told us.

They're easy to use and offer superior durability thanks to their machined steel weight plates. If you're starting a home gym from scratch or live in tight confines with little room to spare, consider these a must-have. Few items pack as much versatility and return on investment in such a small package.

PowerBlock is practically synonymous with adjustable dumbbells—and for good reason. The Pro 100 model is compact, rugged, and replaces an entire weight rack. With a range from 5 to 100 pounds, it offers more versatility than almost any other dumbbell on the market.

One of our testers, Maillard Howell, who tried PowerBlock’s Pro 50 model, said he liked how compact the dumbbells were and that the square shape lets you set them down without rolling away. Men’s Health Fitness Director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S., likes how easy they are to adjust. There’s not much to nitpick with these dumbbells. That said, they can feel awkward and obtrusive during specific exercises. For example, because your hand and wrist are inside the bell, they can feel clunky during dumbbell curls. We dubbed this bench the “mid-range bench that delivers big value”. You’ll never outlift the 1,000-pound weight capacity, and the redesigned 2.0 version features a longer back leg to eliminate side-to-side wobble.

“Hands down, my favorite feature is the ladder-style adjustment system—you can drag the bench to any upright angle in seconds,” says NASM-certified personal trainer and product tester Andrew Gutman.

There are a few trade-offs. The 2.1-inch pad gap, while common among adjustable benches, could bother some lifters if their hips land in the gap mid-set. The bench also can’t be stored upright, and at nearly five feet long, it claims a chunk of floor space. Lastly, while $350 is a fair price for this level of quality, it’s not exactly pocket change.

Are you aiming to build muscle like a Greek god or shed those pesky love handles? Different goals call for different equipment. If you're into strength training, focus on getting some quality adjustable dumbbells or a full rack setup. For cardio enthusiasts, a treadmill, stationary bike, or rowing machine will get your heart pumping and your sweat dripping.

Unless you're living in a mansion, your home gym likely won't be as spacious as a commercial one. So choose equipment that fits your available space. If you're tight on space, consider compact options like resistance bands or adjustable dumbbells. They're small but mighty, perfect for those who need to optimize their space.

And quality matters, man! Don't skimp on the good stuff. You want equipment that can handle your beastly workouts without falling apart like a house of cards. Look for trusted brands that have a reputation for durability and longevity. Read reviews, ask around, and do your homework. You don't want wobbly machines or weights that crack under pressure. Go for sturdy materials and solid construction. You'll be glad in the long run.

It may be the spice of life, but contrary to what the market may tell you, the basics let you perform the largest variety of movements. Pass on over-specialized tools that let you do a few unique exercises and nothing more. Dumbbells and kettlebells let you do a mix of explosive and controlled isolation moves and can help you build strength, power, and mobility. Opt for those over stability balls and suspension trainers, which require fancier setups (especially if you're building a living room facility).

Set a budget and stick to it. Home gym equipment comes in a wide range of price points, and it's easy to get carried away. Determine how much you're willing to spend and prioritize your purchases accordingly. Remember, it's better to invest in a few quality pieces than to clutter your space with cheap, flimsy gear that won't stand the test of time.

So, you're thinking about setting up your very own home gym. Well, buckle up because it's going to be a game-changer.

Convenience is the name of the game. No more wasting precious time commuting to the gym or waiting for your turn on the bench. With a home gym, you're the boss of your own fitness kingdom. You can squeeze in a workout whenever it suits you, whether it's before the crack of dawn or after a long day at work, or in bite-sized bits between meetings. Plus, no more crowds or sweaty gym rats hogging the equipment. It's just you, your favorite tunes, and some iron to conquer.

Sure, creating a lasting home gym demands an initial investment, but it's a wise long-term savings strategy.

Consider adjustable dumbbells (priced between $400 and $800), a yoga mat (typically $20 to $140), and a cardio machine like a rower or bike (average cost around $2000). While that initial investment may seem steep, keep in mind that the average gym membership in the U.S. is about $55/month, plus initiation and cancellation fees. You'll recoup your investment in less than 5 years.

There's nothing worse than investing in a new piece of home gym equipment, only to find it's a one-trick pony you're bored with after a week. For an at-home setup that will keep you excited to use it, you want gear versatile enough to work in different ways. That way, you can keep your space uncluttered and your budget on-point.

When it comes to the best bang-for-your-buck, value-packed equipment, our answer might annoy you. But all you really need? A pair of reliable adjustable dumbbells, a training program, and a weight bench.

Start out strong with a pair of adjustable dumbbells like the ones from SMRTFT or PowerBlock. Yes, they're pricey, but they pack a ton of versatility in a minimal footprint. Pair it with a weight bench like the one from REP Fitness, or a luxe option like this one from Prime Fitness.

#4865. Ultra-Processed Food Intake May Not Be Linked to Colorectal Cancer

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Ultra-processed foods as a group do not increase the risk of colorectal cancer, according to a new study.

Researchers from the National Cancer Institute (NCI) report that they found no association between overall intake of ultra-processed foods and colorectal cancer incidence among the more than 400,000 adults they studied.

They said their findings are important because ultra-processed foods now account for more than half of adults’ energy intake in the United States. They published their research on October 15 in PLOS Medicine.

The study comes amid recent research that has connected ultra-processed foods to an increased risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes, and obesity.

However, the link between colorectal cancer and ultra-processed foods has been less clear.

A 2025 study reported that ultra-processed foods may increase the risk of colorectal cancer in females under 50.

A 2022 study found that males who consumed high rates of ultra-processed foods had a higher risk of developing colorectal cancer.

NCI scientists noted in their new study that ultra-processed products represent a wide range of foods.

They pointed out that foods such as whole grains and dairy products were associated with a lower risk of colorectal cancer among study participants, while foods such as processed meats were associated with a higher risk.

“We did not observe an association between total ultra-processed food (UPF) intake and colorectal cancer incidence,” said Erikka Loftfield, PhD, a former investigator with the NCI, in a news release. “However, when we looked at food groups, like whole grains, dairy, and meat, by UPF or non-UPF source, we found that associations were in line with current dietary guidance for colorectal cancer prevention.”

Experts not involved in the study told Healthline that the NCI scientists appear to be on the right track.

“While the study did not find that overall ultra-processed food intake was associated with colorectal cancer risk, the findings suggest that simply classifying foods by their level of processing may not tell the whole story,” said Marissa Shams-White, PhD, a senior principal scientist in population science at the American Cancer Society.

“One of the challenges with studying ultra-processed foods is that the category includes a very wide range of foods and beverages, and we don’t yet know whether they all relate to colorectal cancer risk in the same way,” she added.

Ketan Thanki, MD, a colorectal surgeon specializing in benign and malignant diseases of the colon, rectum, and anus at the MemorialCare Todd Cancer Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in California, agrees.

“Just because it is ultra-processed doesn’t mean it is inherently bad or good for you,” he said. “The source of the food and the additives like preservatives, flavorings, sugars, and pigments that are added to increase palatability probably play a much larger role in increasing cancer risk.”

“A whole grain cereal and a hot dog are both UPF, but they push risk in opposite directions,” Thanki noted.

In their study, the National Cancer Institute researchers analyzed data from 461,682 adults aged 50 to 71 years who enrolled in the NIH-AARP Diet and Health Study between 1995 and 1996.

The participants recorded their eating habits using a food frequency questionnaire. Researchers then used that data to estimate each person’s intake of ultra-processed foods. The median consumption of ultra-processed foods among participants was 43% of daily median intake.

The participants were then followed until 2018, for a median of nearly 19 years. The scientists reported that 10,075 cases of colorectal adenocarcinoma were diagnosed during that period.

The researchers reported no association between overall intake of ultra-processed foods and the incidence of colorectal cancer.

However, they said their findings varied by food type. Higher intake of whole grains, fiber, dairy products, and calcium derived from UPF sources was associated with lower colorectal cancer incidence, while meat from UPF sources was associated with higher incidence.

Researchers also found that replacing 10% of gram weight from UPF intake with minimally processed foods was associated with a 2% lower incidence of CRC.

The researchers acknowledged limitations to their study.

They noted that the participants were primarily older, white Americans. In addition, UPF intake was only measured once at the beginning of the study period, so the findings cannot account for changes in eating habits or the increasing availability of ultra-processed foods.

Nonetheless, Ashkan Farhadi, MD, a gastroenterologist at MemorialCare Orange Coast Medical Center in California, who wasn’t involved in the study, said he found the results “very interesting.”

“The definition of ultra-processed foods is so broad,” he told Healthline. “It’s important to designate what type of ultra-processed foods you’re talking about.”

“You shouldn’t just go by whether a food is designated as ultra-processed,” Farhadi added. “You should go by what’s on the [ingredient] label.”

Shams-White shared a similar approach. “When it comes to diet, it is important to follow an overall healthy dietary pattern,” she told Healthline. “Emphasize a variety of fruits and vegetables and whole grains and limit red and processed meats, sugar-sweetened beverages, ultra-processed foods, and refined grain products.”

“Eating a diet rich in fiber and whole grains, dairy and calcium, and limiting processed and red meat and alcohol is what really reduces your risk of developing cancer,” added Thanki.

The American Cancer Society estimates there will be about 108,000 new cases of colon cancer and nearly 50,000 new cases of rectal cancer in the United States in 2026.

The incidence rate is slightly higher in men than in females.

The organization reports that the rate of colorectal cancer cases in the United States has declined since the mid-1980s, but they note that most of that decrease has occurred in older adults.

The incidence rate among people under 50 increased by nearly 3% from 2013 to 2022. Colorectal cancer is now the leading cause of death from cancer for U.S. adults under the age of 50.

Colorectal cancer occurs when cells in the large intestine grow out of control. About 70% of cases begin in the colon.

There are a number of symptoms of colorectal cancer. Some of the common ones include:

Diet is considered one of the risk factors for colorectal cancer. However, there are other considerations.

Farhadi said genetics, obesity, smoking, alcohol consumption, and a sedentary lifestyle can also increase a person’s risk of developing colorectal cancer.

Shams-White noted that the American Cancer Society Guideline for Diet and Physical Activity for Cancer Prevention recommended achieving and maintaining a healthy weight, being physically active, following a healthy eating pattern, and avoiding alcohol and tobacco.

Thanki points out that there are risk factors for colorectal cancer that can be modified and others that can’t.

“Among non-modifiable factors, the biggest are age, a personal or family history of colorectal cancer or advanced polyps, inherited syndromes such as Lynch syndrome, and longstanding inflammatory bowel disease (ulcerative colitis or Crohn’s colitis),” he told Healthline.

“On the modifiable side, the evidence is strongest for obesity, physical inactivity, smoking, alcohol, and diets high in red and processed meat and low in fiber, whole grains, and calcium,” he added.

Colorectal cancer treatment outcomes improve if the disease is caught at an early stage. The 5-year relative survival for localized colorectal cancer is 91.3%. The 5-year relative survival rate for colorectal cancer caught at various stages, including cancer that has spread, is 65.4%.

Shams-White points out that the American Cancer Society Colorectal Cancer Guidelines recommend that people at average risk of colorectal cancer start getting screened at age 45.

“Screening can help prevent cancer by finding abnormal cells and polyps so they can be removed before they can become colorectal cancer, as well as detect cancer at an earlier stage,” she said.

Thanki had similar advice. “Get screened starting at 45, or earlier if you have a family history or other risk factors, because colonoscopies can prevent cancer by removing polyps,” he said. “And never ignore symptoms like rectal bleeding or a change in bowel habits at any age.”

Wondering how long your new electric car might last? This 2019 Chevrolet Bolt EV is proving that electric vehicles can rack up miles with the best of them – and its owner is committed to taking it past the half million mile mark.

You’d think a 250,000 mile Ford Mustang Mach-E maintaining 92% of its battery life, a 360,000 mile Hyundai IONIQ 5 with 87% battery health still intact, and a half-dozen stories of Tesla Model S, 3, and Y ride share vehicles racking up six figures on their odometers later would do more to convince the slow adopters and doubting Thomases of the world that EV batteries are built to last. The good news is that the evidence keeps piling up, and this latest example of electric endurance is a good one.

On the Out of Spec Renew YouTube channel, the crew took in a 2019 Chevy Bolt EV – one of the most affordable new EVs you could buy in 2019 – that had more than 470,000 miles on the clock and 330,000 miles on its post-recall battery. That’s nearly half a million miles on the original electric motor, and it’s still got more to go.

Average gas car lasts ~150k miles. For sure requiring more maintenance to reach that mileage than an EV. Achieving ~500k on the original motor and over 300k on a battery is quite impressive.

Getting towards the 100k mile mark in both our EVs. Both feel like brand new and have required almost no maintenance - a fraction of what would be required with ICE. Look forward to hitting 200k and beyond in each. For the first time in over 30 years of driving, I don’t look forward to replacing our cars as they get over 100k miles - rather I look forward to the next 100k miles of driving.

Out of Spec Renew replaced the 2019’s battery pack that was experiencing serious battery faults and cell-voltage imbalances with a stable, lower-mile example from a 2017 model. Once it was up and in, they got the car powered up, programmed, and driving again, putting the owner on track to reach the 500,000 mile milestone (sorry) in their trusty steed.

#4862. Bestselling US e-bike Lectric XP4 relaunched in limited-edition with new colors and gear

The Lectric XP series has become something of an institution in the American electric bicycle market. As the country’s best-selling e-bike lineup, it’s helped put hundreds of thousands of riders on two wheels, largely thanks to its combination of affordable pricing, impressive performance, and practical features. It’s become the go-to electric bike in the US, but that hasn’t made Lectric complacent. Instead, the company is continuing to innovate.

Now, Lectric is giving its flagship XP4 a fresh makeover with three new limited-edition versions, each sporting a unique color and a new collection of accessories and upgrades designed for different types of riders, from urban assault roles to conquering your favorite off-road fishing spots.

The new Lectric XP4 Limited Editions come in three flavors: District, (black) Jubilee (pink), and Ridge (green). Each is priced at $1,399 and includes a 750W motor, upgraded accessories, and a distinctive matte paint job. That’s just a $100 premium over the existing XP 750, yet comes with a whole new host of accessories designed specifically for each special edition model.

So while the underlying bike remains largely unchanged from the bones of the XP 750, the new packages are designed to make the XP4 even more useful straight out of the box.

The District edition is aimed squarely at urban commuters, sporting a stealthy Matte Black finish and an accessory package designed for daily transportation.

That includes two waterproof pannier bags, a redesigned LevelUp rear cargo rack, a heavy-duty bike lock, and an upgraded high-output headlight. There’s also a fast charger, suspension seat post with comfort saddle, and handlebar phone mount.

It’s essentially a ready-made commuter setup, particularly for riders looking to replace some of their car trips with an e-bike.

The Jubilee edition takes a more casual approach, arriving in a distinctive Matte Thistle colorway. Designed for neighborhood cruising, weekend errands, and trips to the local farmers market, it swaps the commuter-focused panniers for a front and rear cargo package and a small roll-top bag.

It also includes the upgraded headlight, lock, fast charger, suspension seat post, comfort saddle, and phone mount.

Finally, the Ridge edition is built for riders who prefer their adventures a bit farther from the pavement.

Finished in Matte Forest green, the Ridge comes with front and rear cargo accessories for carrying outdoor gear, plus a fishing rod holder on the High-Step version. The Step-Thru model instead receives a weather-resistant roll-top bag.

Like the other two editions, the Ridge also includes the upgraded lighting, lock, charger, and comfort accessories.

“We wanted to deliver ready-made, purpose-built setups that empower our community to jump on and ride without having to configure their gear from scratch,” explained Lectric co-founder and CEO Levi Conlow.

Conlow also seems particularly proud of the new finishes, calling them the three best paint jobs Lectric has produced in its seven-year history.

While the new colors and accessory bundles are the main attraction, the bikes retain the same core features that helped make the XP4 such a popular choice.

That includes Lectric’s in-house developed dynamic torque sensor, which measures how hard the rider pedals to deliver a more natural-feeling electric assist experience.

The bikes also feature hydraulic disc brakes, an 8-speed Shimano Altus drivetrain, and a full-color TFT display.

As with the standard XP4, riders get the familiar folding frame design, integrated turn signals and brake lights, and 20-inch puncture-resistant mixed-terrain tires.

All three limited editions are available in both High-Step and Step-Thru frame configurations.

At $1,399, the new models aren’t trying to reinvent the XP4 formula. Instead, they’re offering a more fully equipped version of a bike that has already proven itself with American riders.

And that’s probably a smart move. Lectric has built its reputation on delivering an impressive amount of e-bike for the money, and bundling useful accessories into a relatively affordable package plays right into that strategy.

The limited-edition models are available now, though Lectric says quantities are strictly limited.

For riders who have been considering an XP4 but wanted something a bit more distinctive than the standard colors, these new editions could be a compelling reason to finally pull the trigger.

And for those looking to score an even better deal, Lectric has also officially kicked off its Black Friday sale.

The company is calling it its biggest sale of the year, with some of its lowest prices ever and discounts of up to $800 across its lineup.

So whether you’re eyeing one of the new limited-edition XP4 models or another e-bike in Lectric’s extensive catalog, now could be a particularly good time to buy.

#4859. Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: What Breast Cancer Patients Need When Treatment Ends

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Supporting your health & well-being at every stage of your breast cancer journey

Resources to support your emotional health and well-being

Tips for nourishing your body and mind while living with breast cancer

Navigating your next steps after diagnosis

A guide to medications, treatments, and procedures for breast cancer

Guide to symptoms, tests, and screenings for early detection

Resources to support you during remission or recurrence

Breast cancer awareness has never been higher. Every October, the world turns pink and the key messages—that early diagnosis saves lives and that knowing the risk factors reduces the risk—are hard to miss.

Breast cancer remains the leading cause of cancer death amongst women in most countries, with an estimated 694,000 deaths annually and around 8.2 million women globally living with a breast cancer diagnosis, according to 2024 data from the World Health Organization (WHO)’s Global Health Observatory.

But when October ends, and the wall-to-wall campaigns move on, what has awareness actually changed for those impacted by breast cancer?

For Marisa Weiss, MD, the answer is that there is always more to be done, and she’s uniquely placed to hold that view.

Dr. Weiss has treated breast cancer patients for more than 30 years. In 2000, she founded Breastcancer.org, a not-for-profit that provides expert-reviewed medical and lifestyle information on breast health and breast cancer to millions of people globally.

Sixteen years ago, she was diagnosed with stage 1 breast cancer, becoming, in her own words, a “dual citizen” in that moment, with lived experience on both sides of the doctor’s desk.

I had the pleasure of sitting down with Dr. Weiss as part of Healthline’s Breast Cancer Awareness Month coverage to talk about how awareness has changed care for the better for millions of people around the world, but why critical gaps remain, and how access to trusted information and community can help close those gaps.

“It’s good that awareness is high, but knowledge remains low,” she said. The misconception she sees most is the belief that breast cancer is something that happens to other families.

“Most people think they’re off the hook because it doesn’t run in their family,” Dr. Weiss explained. In fact, 85%-90% of people who get breast cancer have no known family history and no known gene in the family, “so we’re all at risk,” she warns.

For most, a diagnosis arrives as a shock. “They’re completely surprised, flattened by it. Their life is upside down, and they scramble to find what they need to know,” Dr. Weiss noted.

That aftershock caused by a diagnosis is at the core of why Breastcancer.org exists. Because the person whose life is at stake is increasingly expected to direct their own care and advocate for their needs, access to accurate and empowering information is critical in that process. “Decisions aren’t being made around the patient and for the patient in a bubble,” Dr. Weiss said. “Decision-making is supposed to be shared.”

With appointments hard to get and clinicians stretched, she said patients have to do more of the work.

In practice, Dr. Weiss says that means preparing by writing down your family history on both sides and making sure your care team knows who you are. “What’s important to you? Who’s in your life? Do you want more children? Are you planning a vacation? All those decisions can have an impact on the care you receive.”

Where Dr. Weiss sees the widest gap is after treatment ends. Speaking from personal experience, she said: “I had the best care possible, and there were still gaps. You can find yourself alone with lots of questions and fears.”

Understandably, a great deal of focus and attention is centered on diagnosis and treatment, she said. Then it stops. “When your life is supposed to go back to normal, it never does. You have to find a new normal. That quiet phase can be really lonely and difficult.”

For Dr. Weiss, the questions that fill that silence are often as practical as they are emotional: “How do I know my treatment is working? How do I tell leftover side effects from new ones? How do I go back to work, and who do I tell? How do I talk to my kids? What about that new treatment I heard about on TV last night?”

That post-treatment period can also be the first time anyone has really had a chance to stop, take stock, and start asking “what next?”

“You might think, what happened to me? Who do I want to be moving forward? How do I have a long life full of wonderful things, and remain as independent as possible?”

Some of what patients do post-care never reaches their doctor at all, with Dr. Weiss pointing to a growing amount of personal care happening outside the hospital: GLP-1s, peptides, hormonal therapies, supplements, medical cannabis, to name a few. Breastcancer.org’s annual Pulse survey shows that people reported being interested in all of those things and more, and that they tend to learn about them online rather than risk the stigma of bringing them up with their doctors.

“They don’t really trust that their doctor knows much about these things,” she said, “and they don’t want a conversation that makes them feel judged. Some patients worry about what ends up in their medical record and then everyone’s going to know.”

Dr. Weiss says the stakes are real, because some of those choices can interact with cancer medications with potential for adverse or unexpected side effects. “That is why people turn to Breastcancer.org with questions that are personal, private, and important, including “what do I need to share with my doctor, and who’s on a need-to-know basis?”

Peer communities fill part of that gap. “Breastcancer.org’s members share medical information, tips, and practical advice with people who have just had the call, or are about to start treatment and don’t know what to expect,” Dr. Weiss shared, and said that while she values that kind of support system hugely, it does come with a caveat:

The same community can also be intrusive. Dr. Weiss describes standing at a party, drink in hand, when someone asks what kind of cancer you had, what you did, and whether it came back. Her advice to patients, which she took herself: “Put up your hand and say, ‘thank you so much for caring, but I cannot hear another story right now.’”

On the topic of AI and its impact on treatment and care, Dr. Weiss is cautiously optimistic. On the treatment front, she points to tools that help radiologists read mammograms more thoroughly and assess future risk as game changers in the fight against breast cancer.

Breastcancer.org also uses AI for tasks such as finding citations and interpreting pathology reports. But like many, she is wary of misinformation. “When you’re fearful, you look for reassurance, and you don’t want to be reassured by misinformation. When it comes to health information, every word matters.”

Her final advice is for the appointment itself, which she says she has even struggled with. “When they start speaking quickly in complex medical language, you can get lost really fast. The risk is you kind of fake it. That must not happen.”

“Do your research in advance. Write things down. Bring someone you trust as a second set of ears. Ask direct questions. Don’t worry if it sounds funny or silly. It’s always a smart question when you are dealing with your health.” Just be prepared for the questions to multiply from there. “Once you get a certain set of questions answered, that raises more questions. So just write it all down.”

If breast cancer has touched your life, know that you are not alone. Healthline’s Bezzy Breast Cancer Community is a space for peer support, feel-good stories, and advice in a welcoming, stigma-free environment when your lived experience is seen, heard and valued.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#4857. Electric trucks hit 50% of China’s heavy truck sales, diesel demand falls

Electric trucks made up 50.3% of all heavy trucks sold in China in September. That’s 33,100 electric big rigs in a single month, up 37% from a year ago, while the overall heavy-truck market shrank by 21%.

And it’s showing up in the oil data. China’s diesel demand is on track to fall by about 230,000 barrels per day this year, and trucks running on batteries and natural gas are doing most of the work.

In 2024, new-energy vehicles accounted for 13.6% of heavy trucks (12 tonnes and up) sold in China. In 2025, sales nearly tripled to 231,100 units, up 182%, for a 28.9% share.

December 2025 hit 53.9%, but that was a rush. Buyers piled in before trade-in subsidies expired and new purchase tax costs kicked in, and the share dropped back to about 23% by March.

Then it came right back. Through the first nine months of 2026, China sold 226,400 electric heavy trucks, up 64%, for a 38.1% share. That’s already almost as many as in all of 2025, with a quarter still to go. In tractors, the big rigs that pull long-haul trailers, the September share hit 64.5%.

That’s well ahead of Beijing’s own plan. This year, 11 government bodies set a target of 40% new-energy heavy-truck sales by 2030, along with 1.6 million of them on the road, 30,000 km of zero-carbon freight corridors and about 3,000 charging and swap stations. The market blew past that sales target four years early on a monthly basis, and CATL now says new-energy trucks will be half of China’s annual truck sales by 2028.

It comes down to cost. In most uses, an electric heavy truck in China is now cheaper to own and run than a diesel one, and fleets run that math every day.

CATL claims savings of about 0.6 yuan per km. Independent numbers are more measured, but still favorable: a study by the ICCT and China’s CATARC found battery-electric trucks about 19% cheaper per tonne-km than diesel in good conditions, though they lose that edge at low annual mileage or when battery weight eats into payload.

Charging time is the other objection, and China’s answer is swapping. CATL’s Qiji brand operated about 300 heavy-truck swap stations at the end of 2025 and plans 900 by the end of this year and 2,000 in 2027. In August, it agreed to pay 2.56 billion yuan (about $370 million) for nearly 25% of Qiyuan Green Power, China’s largest heavy-truck swap operator with more than 1,600 charging and swap stations. Its new standardized packs hold 432 kWh and 513 kWh and swap in about five minutes.

CATL is already taking the swapping model to Europe with Octopus Energy.

The trucks themselves are domestic. XCMG led the segment in 2025 with a 15.4% share, ahead of SANY and FAW Jiefang, and the top three held 42.9% of the market. CATL supplied 60% of the batteries.

For years, the alternative to diesel trucks in China was liquefied natural gas. LNG trucks took nearly 30% of the market in 2024, when gas was much cheaper than diesel.

Now they’re losing to batteries. According to Kpler, LNG trucks had a 37% share of heavy-duty registrations in March, against 23% for electric. By July, electric was at 47% and LNG had collapsed to 11%. Monthly LNG truck registrations fell from more than 30,000 to about 6,200.

So China is skipping the “bridge fuel” phase for trucks almost as fast as it started it.

China’s diesel consumption already peaked. Rhodium Group found that diesel refinery runs topped out in April 2023, and apparent consumption dropped from 4.7 million barrels per day then to 4.0 million barrels per day in April 2025.

2026 is the steepest drop yet. Kpler estimates diesel demand fell about 420,000 barrels per day year-over-year in May, roughly 10%, and 460,000 in June. It expects a decline of about 230,000 barrels per day for the full year and only a 64,000 barrel-per-day rebound in 2027.

Kpler puts the gross diesel displacement from alternative trucks at about 680,000 barrels per day this year: roughly 450,000 from the LNG fleet built in recent years and 230,000 from electric trucks. Road freight still grew about 3% through July. China is moving more goods with less diesel.

There’s a catch. The fuel price spike this spring, tied to the Iran war, cut demand on its own and pushed more fleets toward electric. But that only explains part of it, and the fleet is still early: electric trucks will be only about 8% of China’s heavy trucks on the road by the end of 2026, according to Kpler. Every year of 40-50% sales shares compounds the diesel losses.

Europe is growing fast from a tiny base. ACEA data shows 5,120 heavy electric trucks (16 tonnes and up) registered in the EU through September, up 59.9%, for a 2.5% share. That’s up from 1.7% a year ago, but diesel still took 91.7% of all EU truck registrations.

A few countries stand out. Switzerland is at 24%, Norway 14.4%, the Netherlands 11.1% and Sweden 10.3%. Germany registered the most units at 1,722, but that’s only a 4.2% share.

The US doesn’t publish an official electric Class 8 sales figure, and that tells you something. The best indicator is the recent order for 2,500 Tesla Semis through ZET SCALE, which would roughly double the number of electric Class 8 trucks on US roads. Against about 230,000 Class 8 trucks sold per year, the electric share is well under 1%.

Price is the big difference. The ICCT puts the median electric Class 8 tractor in the US at $411,200, against $172,500 for diesel. Federal tax credits for commercial EVs ended in 2025, and California shelved the rule that would have required fleets to buy zero-emission trucks.

This is the biggest under-covered story in the EV industry right now.

Everyone argued for years about whether electric semis could work. Range, weight, charging, cost. China settled it the boring way. It got the price of a battery-electric heavy truck close enough to diesel, built swap stations along freight routes, and let fleet operators do what fleet operators always do: pick the cheapest truck per kilometer.

And the result is showing up where it matters, in oil demand. Trucks are where diesel lives. Displacing a quarter of a million barrels a day in one year, with electric trucks still only around 8% of the fleet, means we’re still early on this curve.

The contrast with the US is hard to watch. America has Tesla, a Semi factory in Nevada built for 50,000 trucks a year, and some of the best freight corridors in the world for electrification. But electric Class 8 trucks cost 2.4 times as much as diesel here, the federal incentives are gone, and the market is under 1%. The ZET SCALE order is real progress. But it came from shippers pooling their demand, and twe have yet to see these trucks get delivered.

China having a lot of phev and EV vehicles has helped with oil pricing around the world, more notably in the current american-caused oil crisis. It would be worse if they were all traditional powered cars and trucks.

Now to be clear, I'm not saying that it has made a massive impact or is even the only reason why the oil crisis isn't much worse, it's just one of a few steps China made to reduce the impact of the oil crisis. But going electric everywhere we can has a stabilizing effect when oil shocks show up

Europe at least has the regulation, and its best markets, at 10% to 24%, are roughly where China was in 2024 and 2025.

The question now is how long Chinese truck makers stay home. SANY and BYD already sell electric trucks in Europe, and CATL is building the swap network for them. If those trucks can come close to China’s pricing, how long can Western truck makers keep charging 2x diesel?

Diesel trucks aren’t the only thing batteries are displacing. At home, pairing solar with an EV or a home battery can cut your energy bills dramatically. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer.
